Chapter 38

Streaming And Temporal State

Orientation

A rate limiter counts requests in the last sixty seconds. It works in testing, then lets a burst through at exactly second sixty, because one request sits on the boundary and the code disagrees with the spec about whether that second still counts. This chapter teaches APIs whose answers change as values or timestamped events arrive. Each lesson asks what the object may forget, what it must keep queryable, and when stale state disappears.

Prerequisites

You should know queues, sliding windows, prefix sums, heaps, ordered sets, hash maps and binary search. A stream is a sequence of values that arrive one call at a time. A stream is append-only when old values never change, correctable when a call can replace an earlier value, and expiring when old values stop counting after a duration. A window is the part of the stream that still contributes to the answer. A timestamp is the time of an event, and every lesson here passes time into the methods so tests stay deterministic.

What Each Lesson Protects

Each lesson names one fact that every call must keep true.

  • Windowed Streams keeps exactly the active window and its running total, so each call does constant amortized work.
  • Expiring State never shows a record past its deadline, even when cleanup is lazy.
  • Online Prefixes turns a question about the newest k values into one relation between stored prefixes.
  • Stream Order Statistics keeps every active value in exactly one ordered region, so the median or extreme stays readable.
  • Frequency Streams keeps counts and the containers built on them in agreement.
  • Temporal Indexes makes bucket boundaries and query boundaries mean the same instants.
  • Randomized Evolution keeps the promised probability as the collection changes.
  • Queue Time Window joins count limits, time limits, corrections and keyed expiry in one queue.
  • Ordered Stream State joins ranks, windows and corrections with heaps and ordered maps.

Reading Order

Every lesson opens with a class that passes short tests but is slow, wrong or unsafe on longer ones. You predict the failure before the reasoning appears. Two traces follow the fields call by call, then Java code and the limits of the idea. The four exercises climb from a basic build to a variation, an edge case and a recognition problem. A false friend is a design that looks like the pattern but breaks its precondition. Solutions check each class against a brute-force oracle, a slow but obviously correct version, on random inputs.

What You Can Do After This Chapter

You can state for any stream API whether it is append-only, correctable or expiring before you choose a structure. You can say whether the boundary instant is inside or outside the window and keep that rule on every path. Reservoir sampling proofs belong to chapter 35, and caches belong to chapter 33.

Windowed Streams

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Rate Limiter Slows Down

An API gateway allows each client 100 requests per minute. Its rate limiter appends the timestamp of every request to a list. To decide whether a new request passes, the limiter scans the list and counts the timestamps from the last 60 seconds. The code is correct and the tests pass. Then a customer script sends 5000 requests per second for an hour. The list grows by millions of entries, each check scans all of them, and the gateway stops answering within minutes. Memory fills with timestamps that can never change an answer again.

A second service shows the same shape with a count in place of a clock. A dashboard reports the average latency of the last 100 requests, and it copies the whole latency history before it averages. Both programs keep everything and look at a small tail. This lesson asks which part of an endless stream a program must keep, and how it forgets the rest without losing the answer.

Simple approach

Store Everything And Scan

The direct design stores every value and recomputes the answer from the stored list. The class below answers "how many pings arrived in the last span time units" by keeping all timestamps. A second method averages the last k numbers by summing the tail of a list.

final class NaiveStreams {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> times = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Integer> values = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    int ping(long t, long span) {
        times.add(t);                              // keeps every timestamp forever
        int count = 0;
        for (long u : times)                       // scans the whole history on every call
            if (u > t - span) count++;
        return count;
    }

    double average(int v, int k) {
        values.add(v);
        long sum = 0;
        int from = Math.max(0, values.size() - k);
        for (int i = from; i < values.size(); i++) sum += values.get(i);   // re-adds k numbers on every call
        return (double) sum / (values.size() - from);
    }
}

With span = 5, the pings at times 1, 3, 3, 8 return 1, 2, 3 and 1. With k = 2, the values 4, 6, 10 average to 4.0, 5.0 and 8.0. Every answer is correct.

Predict first: A client sends one million pings, one per time unit, with span = 100. Roughly how many timestamps does the class hold at the end, and roughly how many comparisons does the last call to ping perform? What does the total number of comparisons look like for all one million calls?

The class holds all one million timestamps, although at most 100 of them can matter. The last call compares against all one million entries. The total is about 1 + 2 + ... + 10^6, close to 5 * 10^11 comparisons, so the program slows down in proportion to the square of the stream length while its memory grows with every call.

Bottleneck

Cost Grows With The Whole Stream

After n calls the naive class stores n timestamps and scans n entries on the next call. A single call costs O(n) time, a run of n calls costs O(n^2) time in total, and the memory is O(n). The average method scans only the last k entries, so a call costs O(k) time. That cost still repeats work, because two neighboring calls add up almost the same k numbers. Only one number leaves and one number enters between them.

Both methods also keep entries that are certain to be useless. Once the clock passes t + span, a timestamp t can never count again, because later calls only move the clock forward. Once k values have arrived after a value, that value can never enter an average again. The program holds on to data whose future effect is zero.

A better design must meet three needs. It must store only the entries that can still change an answer, so memory depends on the window and not on n. It must update the answer from the change between two calls, and not from a fresh scan. It must remove an entry at the moment it stops counting, and the moment must come from a written rule.

Key idea

Keep The Window, Update The Sum

Decide What Kind Of Stream It Is

Before choosing a structure, classify the stream. An append-only stream never revises a value after it arrives. A correctable stream can replace a value that arrived earlier. An expiring stream drops a value when its age or its position passes a limit. The streams in this lesson are append-only, and their values expire. Arrivals come in order, no call changes an older value, and the only removal is expiry from the old end. A correctable stream needs a different structure, which later lessons build.

The Window Is A Queue With A Sum

The active window is the set of stored values that can still change the next answer. For a count rule, it holds the last k values. For a time rule with length span, it holds the events whose timestamp lies in a stated range that ends at the current time. Arrivals enter at the new end and expiry leaves from the old end, so a queue stores the window in arrival order.

The running sum is the total of the values in the window, stored in one long field. A new value adds its amount to the field. An expired value subtracts the same amount. Each call changes the field by a constant number of operations, so the cost does not depend on k or on n. For a pure count of events, the size of the queue replaces the sum.

Eviction Needs A Written Rule

Eviction is the removal of expired entries from the old end of the queue. It runs inside the call that needs the new answer, as a loop that stops at the first entry that is still inside the window. Each entry enters once and leaves once, so the loops add up to O(1) amortized time per call.

The boundary is the exact timestamp that separates an expired event from a live one. A rule such as "events from the last 5 seconds" does not say whether the event exactly 5 seconds old counts. The contract must state it. The trace uses one convention. An event with timestamp u counts at time t when t - span < u <= t. Eviction therefore removes every entry with u <= t - span. The exercises use other conventions on purpose.

The invariant is that the queue holds exactly the entries inside the window at the latest call, and the running sum equals their total.

State

Queue, Sum, And Clock

The two classes in this lesson share the same few pieces of state.

  • window is the queue of entries in arrival order, with the oldest at the front.
  • sum is the long total of the values in window, and it equals 0 when window is empty.
  • k is the largest number of values the count rule keeps.
  • span is the length of the time rule, in the same unit as the timestamps.
  • now is the timestamp of the current call, passed in by the caller and never read from a system clock.

Timestamps never decrease from one call to the next. Equal timestamps are allowed, and each one is a separate event. In the traces, the pointer head marks the oldest entry still in the window and tail marks the newest entry that has arrived. The window size is tail - head + 1.

Walkthrough

Tracing Both Window Rules

A Window Of The Last Three Values

The first trace uses the count rule with k = 3 and the values 4, 1, 7, 2, 9 and 5. Before any call, head is 0 and tail is -1, so the window is empty. The first three values enter without eviction, and the sum grows to 12. The fourth value, 2, makes the window size 4, which exceeds k. The loop evicts the oldest value, 4, so the sum becomes 12 + 2 - 4 = 10, and head moves to 1. The fifth value, 9, evicts 1 and gives 10 + 9 - 1 = 18. The sixth value, 5, evicts 7 and gives 18 + 5 - 7 = 16. At every step the sum is the total of the cells from head to tail, and the average is that sum divided by the window size.

A Window Of The Last Five Time Units

The second trace uses the time rule with span = 5 and the pings at times 1, 3, 3, 8, 9 and 13. The rule keeps a ping when t - 5 < u. At time 3, nothing expires, because 3 - 5 is negative, so the count is 3 and includes both pings at time 3. At time 8, the cutoff is 3, so the loop evicts the pings at times 1, 3 and 3 and stops at the first entry that is newer. Only the ping at time 8 remains. At time 9, the cutoff is 4 and nothing leaves, so the count is 2. At time 13, the cutoff is 8, so the ping at time 8 leaves and the count is 2 again. The pointer head only moves right, and it moves right at most once per ping in total.

Code

Two Windows With Injected Time

The Count Window

The count window keeps a queue of at most k values and a long sum. Adding a value adds it to the sum, and one eviction subtracts the oldest value when the queue is too long.

final class CountWindow {
    private final int k;
    private final java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> window = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    private long sum;                       // long, because k values of 10^9 overflow an int

    CountWindow(int k) { this.k = k; }

    double add(int v) {
        window.addLast(v);                  // the new value enters at the new end
        sum += v;                           // the sum changes by this value only
        if (window.size() > k) sum -= window.pollFirst();   // at most one value leaves per arrival
        return (double) sum / window.size();   // size is at least 1 here
    }
}

The Time Window

The time window stores timestamps and takes the current time as an argument, so a test controls the clock. The eviction loop uses the convention u <= now - span.

final class TimeWindow {
    private final long span;
    private final java.util.ArrayDeque<Long> window = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();

    TimeWindow(long span) { this.span = span; }

    int ping(long now) {
        window.addLast(now);                                          // the event enters at the new end
        while (!window.isEmpty() && window.peekFirst() <= now - span)   // stops at the first live entry
            window.pollFirst();                                       // each timestamp leaves once
        return window.size();                                         // the size is the count of live events
    }
}

The Slot Array

The slot array is the second design that the Hit Counter exercise asks for. It keeps one slot per time unit, and each slot remembers which second it holds. The sketch below uses span = 3.

final class SlotSketch {
    private final int span;
    private final int[] tag;     // the second that each slot holds
    private final int[] hits;    // the hits counted for that second

    SlotSketch(int span) { this.span = span; tag = new int[span]; hits = new int[span]; }

    void hit(int t) {
        int i = t % span;                                  // seconds 4 and 7 share slot 1
        if (tag[i] != t) { tag[i] = t; hits[i] = 0; }      // an old second is overwritten
        hits[i]++;
    }
}

After hits at seconds 4, 4 and 5, slot 1 holds tag 4 with 2 hits and slot 2 holds tag 5 with 1 hit. A hit at second 7 lands in slot 1, sees tag 4, and resets the slot to tag 7 with 1 hit. The old count never needs a timer.

Cost

  • CountWindow costs O(1) time per call and O(k) space.
  • TimeWindow spends O(1) time per call when averaged over the stream, since every timestamp is removed once, and uses O(w) space, where w is the largest number of events inside one window.
When to use it

When A Window Fits

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when values arrive in order and the answer depends only on the last k values or on the events of a recent time range. The aggregate must be one that a removal can undo, such as a count or a sum. Before coding, state the stream kind, write the boundary rule as an inequality, and choose the unit of the clock. The invariant to keep is that the queue holds exactly the entries inside the window and the sum matches them. Evict before reading the answer, and never after.

A fixed-bucket array is a second way to hold a time window. It keeps one slot per time unit, and each slot stores the second it belongs to and a count. Its memory depends on span and not on the number of events. That helps when many events share one timestamp.

A False Friend

A running maximum looks like a running sum, and it is a false friend. When the oldest value leaves, the old maximum and the departing value do not give the new maximum. The second largest value was never stored. The same holds for a median. These aggregates need a different structure, such as a monotonic deque or a pair of heaps.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If timestamps can arrive out of order, the front of the queue is not the oldest entry and eviction stops too early. If the stream is correctable, the queue cannot find the old value to subtract. If a query asks about an arbitrary past time and not the latest time, one forward-moving window is not enough, and the entries need an index by time.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Rolling Average Of Readings

LeetCode 346

Problem Specification. Design the class MovingAverage with a constructor that receives an integer size. The method next(val) appends val to the stream and returns the arithmetic mean of the last size values, or of all values when fewer than size have arrived. The class stores at most size values.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size is from 1 to 10^4.
  • Values are int values from -10^5 to 10^5.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Result is the exact sum divided by the count, as a double.

Example 1. Input calls ["MovingAverage","next","next","next","next"] with arguments [[3],[5],[8],[2],[11]], output [null,5.0,6.5,5.0,7.0].

Example 2. Input calls ["MovingAverage","next","next","next"] with arguments [[1],[4],[-2],[0]], output [null,4.0,-2.0,0.0].

Prerequisites. The count window and the running sum from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the window length is a count, one long total replaces a re-sum of the tail, and a size of 1 makes every call replace the whole window.

Hint

Which value leaves when the 4th value arrives, and what happens to the stored total when it leaves?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps a queue of at most size values and a long total of the values in the queue. A new value enters at the back and adds to the total. When the queue then holds more than size values, exactly one value leaves from the front and the total drops by it. The invariant is that the total equals the sum of the queued values and the queue length is at most size. The result divides the total by the queue length, which is at least 1 after an insertion.

The program compares each answer with a direct re-sum of the last size values on random streams, including a size of 1 and negative values.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because one insertion and at most one removal change the total.
  • Space is O(size), because the queue never holds more than size values.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class MovingAverageBuild {
    static final class MovingAverage {
        private final int size;
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> window = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private long total;

        MovingAverage(int size) {
            // The bound is stored once; it never changes.
            this.size = size;
        }

        /**
         * Appends a value and returns the mean of the last size values.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(size) for the whole object.
         * Invariant: total equals the sum of window, and window.size() <= size.
         */
        double next(int val) {
            // The value enters at the back of the queue.
            window.addLast(val);
            // The total changes by this one value.
            total += val;
            // At most one value is over the bound, and it is the oldest.
            if (window.size() > size) total -= window.pollFirst();
            // The queue holds at least one value, so the division is defined.
            return (double) total / window.size();
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the window of 3 drops the first value at the fourth call.
        MovingAverage a = new MovingAverage(3);
        double[] want1 = {5.0, 6.5, 5.0, 7.0};
        int[] in1 = {5, 8, 2, 11};
        for (int i = 0; i < in1.length; i++)
            if (a.next(in1[i]) != want1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        // Example 2: a size of 1 returns each value itself.
        MovingAverage b = new MovingAverage(1);
        if (b.next(4) != 4.0 || b.next(-2) != -2.0 || b.next(0) != 0.0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Large values must not overflow the total: 10^4 values of 10^5 fit a long.
        MovingAverage big = new MovingAverage(10000);
        double last = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) last = big.next(100000);
        if (last != 100000.0) throw new AssertionError("large sum");
        // Random streams compare with a direct re-sum of the tail.
        Random rnd = new Random(3801L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int size = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            MovingAverage real = new MovingAverage(size);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(20); step > 0; step--) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(2001) - 1000;
                all.add(v);
                long sum = 0;
                int from = Math.max(0, all.size() - size);
                for (int i = from; i < all.size(); i++) sum += all.get(i);
                double expected = (double) sum / (all.size() - from);
                if (real.next(v) != expected) throw new AssertionError("random stream");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Recent Pings Within A Span

LeetCode 933

Problem Specification. Design the class RecentCounter with a constructor that receives an integer span. The method ping(t) records a ping at time t and returns how many recorded pings have a time u with t - span <= u <= t. The count includes the new ping. The time t of each call is at least the time of the previous call.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Span is from 0 to 10^9.
  • Times are int values from 0 to 10^9, and equal times are allowed.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Memory holds only pings that can still count at the latest time.

Example 1. Input calls ["RecentCounter","ping","ping","ping","ping","ping","ping"] with arguments [[4],[1],[3],[5],[5],[9],[10]], output [null,1,2,3,4,3,2].

Example 2. Input calls ["RecentCounter","ping","ping","ping"] with arguments [[0],[2],[2],[3]], output [null,1,2,1].

Prerequisites. The Rolling Average exercise above. This exercise changes the bound from a count of values to a length of time.

What This Exercise Changes. The bound is now time, so the number of pings inside the window varies. Eviction becomes a loop, and the inclusive range changes the comparison to u < t - span.

Hint

Which comparison decides that the oldest ping has expired when both ends of the range count?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The counter stores the timestamps that can still count in a queue, oldest first. A ping appends its time. The inclusive range t - span <= u <= t means that a timestamp is expired exactly when u < t - span, so a loop removes front entries while that holds. The loop stops at the first live entry, because timestamps never decrease and every later entry is at least as new. The queue size is the answer. Equal timestamps are separate entries, so two pings at time 2 return 1 and then 2.

The invariant is that after each call the queue holds exactly the pings inside the range ending at the latest time. The program compares with a scan of the full history on random non-decreasing times, including a span of 0.

Complexity.

  • Time averages O(1) per call over the stream, since each timestamp is appended once and removed at most once.
  • Space is O(m), where m is the largest number of pings that fit in one range, because older pings are removed.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RecentCounterVary {
    static final class RecentCounter {
        private final int span;
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> window = new ArrayDeque<>();

        RecentCounter(int span) {
            // The span is part of the contract, so it is a constructor argument.
            this.span = span;
        }

        /**
         * Records a ping and counts the pings in [t - span, t].
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(m) for the live pings.
         * Invariant: the queue holds exactly the pings with t - span <= u <= t.
         */
        int ping(int t) {
            // The new ping is the newest entry.
            window.addLast(t);
            // An entry is expired when it is strictly older than the lower end; the loop stops at the first live one.
            while (window.peekFirst() < t - span) window.pollFirst();
            // The size of the queue is the number of live pings.
            return window.size();
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: span 4 with a repeated time at 5.
        RecentCounter a = new RecentCounter(4);
        int[] in1 = {1, 3, 5, 5, 9, 10};
        int[] want1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2};
        for (int i = 0; i < in1.length; i++)
            if (a.ping(in1[i]) != want1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        // Example 2: span 0 counts only pings at the same time.
        RecentCounter b = new RecentCounter(0);
        if (b.ping(2) != 1 || b.ping(2) != 2 || b.ping(3) != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The lowest end is inclusive: a ping exactly span old still counts.
        RecentCounter c = new RecentCounter(3);
        if (c.ping(0) != 1 || c.ping(3) != 2 || c.ping(4) != 2) throw new AssertionError("inclusive end");
        // Random non-decreasing times compare with a scan of the whole history.
        Random rnd = new Random(3802L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int span = rnd.nextInt(8);
            RecentCounter real = new RecentCounter(span);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            int now = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                now += rnd.nextInt(4);
                all.add(now);
                int expected = 0;
                for (int u : all) if (now - span <= u && u <= now) expected++;
                if (real.ping(now) != expected) throw new AssertionError("random times");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Large Sum Window

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class TimedSum with a constructor that receives a long named span. The method record(time, value) stores an event. The method total(now) returns the sum of the values of recorded events with now - span <= time <= now. Both ends of the range count. The time and now arguments of all calls, taken together in call order, never decrease.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Span and times are long values from 0 to 9 * 10^18.
  • Values are long values from -10^12 to 10^12, and 0 is allowed.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Clock is always an argument, and the class never reads a system clock.

Example 1. Input calls ["TimedSum","record","record","total","total"] with arguments [[10],[5,100],[15,-30],[15],[16]], output [null,null,null,70,-30].

Example 2. Input calls ["TimedSum","record","record","total","total"] with arguments [[9000000000000000000],[0,5000000000000],[9000000000000000000,5000000000000],[9000000000000000000],[9000000000000000001]], output [null,null,null,10000000000000,5000000000000].

Prerequisites. The Recent Pings exercise above. This exercise changes the aggregate from a count to a sum and enlarges the number range.

What This Exercise Changes. Both ends are inclusive, a query alone can expire entries, the total needs long, and the cutoff now - span is computed instead of time + span.

Hint

Why can time + span overflow a long when both are near 9 * 10^18, and which rewriting avoids it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores each recorded event as a pair of a time and a value in two parallel long queues, and keeps a long total of the values in the queue. A recorded event enters at the back and adds to the total. A query first computes the cutoff now - span. Both arguments are at least 0 and at most 9 * 10^18, so the difference lies between -9 * 10^18 and 9 * 10^18 and cannot overflow. The sum time + span could reach 1.8 * 10^19, which exceeds the largest long, which is why the code never forms it. The query removes front events with time < cutoff and subtracts their values, then returns the total.

The invariant is that after a query at now the queue holds exactly the events with now - span <= time <= now. A query also expires events, which is correct because no later call has a smaller time. The program checks both examples and compares with a scan on random streams, including a huge span.

Complexity.

  • Time averages O(1) per call over the stream, since each event enters once and leaves at most once.
  • Space is O(m), where m is the largest number of events inside one range, because expired events are removed.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class LargeSumWindowBoundary {
    static final class TimedSum {
        private final long span;
        private final ArrayDeque<Long> times = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private final ArrayDeque<Long> values = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private long total;

        TimedSum(long span) {
            // The span is a long, because it can exceed the int range.
            this.span = span;
        }

        /**
         * Stores an event.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1) per live event.
         * Invariant: total equals the sum of values in the queue.
         */
        void record(long time, long value) {
            // The event enters at the newest end.
            times.addLast(time);
            values.addLast(value);
            // The total changes by this value only.
            total += value;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the sum of values with now - span <= time <= now.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterwards the queue holds exactly the events in the range.
         */
        long total(long now) {
            // Subtraction of two non-negative longs below 9e18 cannot overflow, unlike time + span.
            long cutoff = now - span;
            // Events strictly older than the cutoff expire; the loop stops at the first live event.
            while (!times.isEmpty() && times.peekFirst() < cutoff) {
                times.pollFirst();
                total -= values.pollFirst();
            }
            // The remaining total belongs to the live events.
            return total;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: both ends count, so time 5 is still live at now 15 and gone at now 16.
        TimedSum a = new TimedSum(10);
        a.record(5, 100);
        a.record(15, -30);
        if (a.total(15) != 70 || a.total(16) != -30) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: timestamps near the long limit with a span of 9e18.
        long big = 9_000_000_000_000_000_000L;
        TimedSum b = new TimedSum(big);
        b.record(0, 5_000_000_000_000L);
        b.record(big, 5_000_000_000_000L);
        if (b.total(big) != 10_000_000_000_000L || b.total(big + 1) != 5_000_000_000_000L)
            throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The forbidden sum overflows a long, while the cutoff stays in range.
        if (big + big >= 0 || big - big != 0) throw new AssertionError("overflow premise");
        // A sum of 10^5 events of 10^12 is 10^17, which fits a long but not an int.
        TimedSum c = new TimedSum(big);
        for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) c.record(i, 1_000_000_000_000L);
        if (c.total(99999) != 100_000_000_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("long sum");
        // A query alone expires events, and a zero value is an ordinary event.
        TimedSum d = new TimedSum(2);
        d.record(1, 0);
        d.record(1, 7);
        if (d.total(3) != 7 || d.total(4) != 0) throw new AssertionError("query expiry");
        // Random streams compare with a scan of the whole history.
        Random rnd = new Random(3803L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            long span = rnd.nextInt(8);
            if (t % 10 == 0) span = 9_000_000_000_000_000_000L - rnd.nextInt(8);
            TimedSum real = new TimedSum(span);
            List<long[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            long now = t % 10 == 0 ? 9_000_000_000_000_000_000L - 40 + rnd.nextInt(3) : rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                now += rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    long v = rnd.nextInt(21) - 10;
                    all.add(new long[]{now, v});
                    real.record(now, v);
                } else {
                    long expected = 0;
                    for (long[] e : all) if (now - span <= e[0] && e[0] <= now) expected += e[1];
                    if (real.total(now) != expected) throw new AssertionError("random stream");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Hit Counter With Two Designs

LeetCode 362

Problem Specification. Design the class HitCounter with a constructor that receives an integer span. The method hit(t) records one hit at second t. The method count(t) returns the number of recorded hits with t - span < u <= t. Implement the class twice, once with a queue of hit entries and once with a fixed array of span slots, and show that both give equal answers.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Span is from 1 to 3600.
  • Times are int values from 1 to 10^9 and never decrease across calls.
  • Hits may share one second, and up to 10^5 hits may arrive in one second.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.

Example 1. Input calls ["HitCounter","hit","hit","hit","count","hit","count","count","count"] with arguments [[5],[1],[2],[2],[4],[7],[7],[11],[12]], output [null,null,null,null,3,null,1,1,0].

Example 2. Input calls ["HitCounter","hit","count","count","count","hit","hit","count","count"] with arguments [[3],[4],[4],[6],[7],[7],[7],[9],[10]], output [null,null,1,1,0,null,null,2,0].

Prerequisites. The Large Sum Window exercise above. This exercise has the lesson's half-open range and asks for two designs.

What This Exercise Changes. The range is half-open, many hits can share one second, and the design choice changes memory from the number of live hits to the number of time units.

Hint

In the slot design, how does a slot know that its stored count belongs to an old second and must be ignored?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The queue design stores pairs of a second and a count of hits in that second, oldest first. A hit at the same second as the newest pair adds 1 to its count, and a hit at a later second appends a pair. The counter also keeps a long total of all counts in the queue. A query at t removes front pairs with second <= t - span, subtracts their counts and returns the total. Merging hits per second keeps the queue length at most span, even when 10^5 hits share one second.

The slot design keeps two arrays of length span. A hit at second t uses slot t % span. If the slot's stored second differs from t, the slot holds hits from an older second, so the code overwrites the tag with t and resets the count to 1. Otherwise it adds 1. A query at t sums the counts of slots whose tag satisfies t - span < tag <= t. The tag check is the invariant that makes stale slots harmless, because a slot is never cleared by a timer.

The queue costs memory in proportion to the distinct live seconds, and its query is O(1) amortized. The slots cost fixed memory in proportion to span, and each query reads all span slots. The program runs both designs against a scan of the full history on random streams, and requires equal answers from all three.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call for the queue design, and O(1) per hit and O(span) per count for the slot design.
  • Space is O(min(span, live seconds)) for the queue design and O(span) for the slot design.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class HitCounterRecognize {
    /** Queue design: one pair per distinct second. */
    static final class QueueCounter {
        private final int span;
        private final ArrayDeque<int[]> window = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private long total;

        QueueCounter(int span) { this.span = span; }

        /**
         * Records one hit.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1) amortized.
         * Invariant: total equals the sum of counts in the queue.
         */
        void hit(int t) {
            // Hits in the newest second merge into one pair.
            if (!window.isEmpty() && window.peekLast()[0] == t) window.peekLast()[1]++;
            // A later second starts a new pair with count 1.
            else window.addLast(new int[]{t, 1});
            // The total counts the new hit.
            total++;
        }

        /**
         * Counts hits with t - span < u <= t.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterwards the queue holds only seconds above t - span.
         */
        int count(int t) {
            // A second at or below the cutoff has expired; the loop stops at the first live pair.
            while (!window.isEmpty() && window.peekFirst()[0] <= t - span) total -= window.pollFirst()[1];
            // The total now belongs to the live pairs.
            return (int) total;
        }
    }

    /** Slot design: a fixed array indexed by the second modulo span. */
    static final class SlotCounter {
        private final int span;
        private final int[] tag;
        private final int[] hits;

        SlotCounter(int span) {
            this.span = span;
            // Each slot starts with tag 0, which no hit can use because times start at 1.
            this.tag = new int[span];
            this.hits = new int[span];
        }

        /**
         * Records one hit.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(span) for the object.
         * Invariant: a slot's count belongs to the second in its tag.
         */
        void hit(int t) {
            int i = t % span;
            // A different tag means the slot holds an older second, so the old count is discarded.
            if (tag[i] != t) { tag[i] = t; hits[i] = 0; }
            // The hit adds to the slot of its own second.
            hits[i]++;
        }

        /**
         * Counts hits with t - span < u <= t.
         * Time: O(span). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: only slots whose tag lies in the range contribute.
         */
        int count(int t) {
            int sum = 0;
            // Every slot is checked, because slots hold no clock of their own.
            for (int i = 0; i < span; i++)
                if (t - span < tag[i] && tag[i] <= t) sum += hits[i];
            return sum;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: span 5, and the hits at second 2 expire at second 7.
        int[] ops1 = {0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 1};
        int[] arg1 = {1, 2, 2, 4, 7, 7, 11, 12};
        int[] want1 = {-1, -1, -1, 3, -1, 1, 1, 0};
        // Example 2: span 3, and the hit at second 4 expires at second 7.
        int[] ops2 = {0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1};
        int[] arg2 = {4, 4, 6, 7, 7, 7, 9, 10};
        int[] want2 = {-1, 1, 1, 0, -1, -1, 2, 0};
        run(5, ops1, arg1, want1);
        run(3, ops2, arg2, want2);
        // Many hits in one second merge into one pair and still count exactly.
        QueueCounter q = new QueueCounter(2);
        SlotCounter s = new SlotCounter(2);
        for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) { q.hit(9); s.hit(9); }
        if (q.count(9) != 100000 || s.count(9) != 100000 || q.count(10) != 100000 || q.count(11) != 0 || s.count(11) != 0)
            throw new AssertionError("many hits in one second");
        // Random non-decreasing streams compare both designs with a scan.
        Random rnd = new Random(3804L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int span = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            QueueCounter rq = new QueueCounter(span);
            SlotCounter rs = new SlotCounter(span);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            int now = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(30); step > 0; step--) {
                now += rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    all.add(now); rq.hit(now); rs.hit(now);
                } else {
                    int expected = 0;
                    for (int u : all) if (now - span < u && u <= now) expected++;
                    if (rq.count(now) != expected || rs.count(now) != expected) throw new AssertionError("random stream");
                }
            }
        }
    }

    /** Replays one call list on both designs; op 0 is hit, op 1 is count, and want -1 marks a hit. */
    static void run(int span, int[] ops, int[] args, int[] want) {
        QueueCounter q = new QueueCounter(span);
        SlotCounter s = new SlotCounter(span);
        for (int i = 0; i < ops.length; i++) {
            if (ops[i] == 0) { q.hit(args[i]); s.hit(args[i]); }
            else if (q.count(args[i]) != want[i] || s.count(args[i]) != want[i]) throw new AssertionError("example call " + i);
        }
    }
}verified

Expiring State

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Logged-Out Session Still Works

A web shop stores each login session in a map from session id to a record. The record holds the user and the time at which the session ends. A background job removes ended sessions once a minute. One afternoon the job stalls behind a long garbage collection pause. A customer who logged out ten minutes earlier replays an old cookie, the map still holds the record, and the shop accepts the request. On the same day the map grows without limit, because ended sessions pile up faster than the stalled job removes them.

Both failures have one cause. The program let the physical content of the map decide what is valid. A record in the map is only a claim that the session was valid at some earlier moment. This lesson asks how a class can answer every call as if ended entries were gone, while it removes them whenever it finds it convenient.

This stream is expiring. Every entry carries an end time, calls arrive with a clock reading that never moves backward, and an answer depends on which entries have not ended at that reading. An append-only stream never forgets, and a correctable stream rewrites old values. An expiring stream forgets on its own schedule, and the class has to know exactly when.

Simple approach

Keeping Every Event In A List

The direct design for a message rate limiter records every print in a list. A message may print again only when the previous print of the same text is at least gap time units old. The class below scans the list from the end to find the latest print of the text.

final class NaiveLogger {
    private final long gap;
    private final java.util.ArrayList<String> texts = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> times = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    NaiveLogger(long gap) { this.gap = gap; }

    boolean shouldPrint(long now, String text) {
        for (int i = texts.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) {                 // newest event first
            if (texts.get(i).equals(text)) {
                if (now - times.get(i) < gap) return false;           // printed too recently
                break;                                                // older prints cannot matter
            }
        }
        texts.add(text);                                              // remember this print
        times.add(now);
        return true;
    }
}

With gap 10, the calls (1, "a"), (8, "a"), (11, "a") return true, false and true. The answers are correct and the code is short.

Predict first: A service logs 10^6 different error texts over a day and then logs one text that it has never seen before. How many list entries does that last call look at, and what happens to memory when the service runs for a month?

The call compares the new text with every stored entry, so it looks at all 10^6 entries and finds none. The list never shrinks, so after a month it holds every print the service ever made, even though only prints from the last few seconds can change an answer. Both time per call and memory grow with the age of the service, not with the amount of state that matters.

Bottleneck

Cost That Grows With Age

Let n be the number of prints made so far. A call that meets a new text scans all n entries, so one call costs O(n) time. A stream of n calls costs O(n^2) in total. The list also stores n entries, so memory is O(n) and never decreases.

The scan repeats work that the clock already settled. A print made 10^6 time units ago cannot block anything when gap is 10. The class still stores that print and still reads it on every miss. The list also mixes two different questions into one structure. The first question is which texts have a block that is still running. The second is when each block ends. A text that is looked up by comparing against the whole history never gets a direct answer to either question.

A better design must meet three needs. A call must find the state for its own text without reading other texts. A call must decide from the stored time and the clock alone whether the old print still blocks. The class must be free to drop old state without any caller noticing the difference.

Key idea

Store One Deadline Per Key

One Deadline Replaces The History

A deadline is the first clock reading at which a stored entry no longer counts. For the rate limiter, the deadline of a text is the time of its latest print plus gap. A hash map from text to deadline holds the whole answer. The history of earlier prints adds nothing, because each new print replaces the deadline of its text. The map has one entry per distinct text and a call costs O(1) expected time.

An entry is live at clock reading now when its deadline is greater than now. The comparison is strict, so an entry whose deadline equals now is already not live. That choice is a contract, and the class must apply it in every method in the same way. A print at time 1 with gap 10 has deadline 11, and a call at time 11 may print again.

Validate On Every Read

Every method that reads an entry compares its deadline with now first. A method that finds a deadline at or below now treats the entry as absent, whatever the map physically contains. This is the rule behind the invariant of the lesson. A query never exposes expired state, even when cleanup runs late or never.

Validation alone fixes the wrong answers but not the memory. Entries for texts that never return stay in the map. A purge is the physical removal of entries that are not live. The class may purge in any of three ways. It may do nothing, as validation already keeps answers correct. It may purge on a cadence, such as every k calls. It may purge exactly when time passes a deadline, with a min-heap of deadlines. All three produce identical answers, and they differ only in how much memory they hold and when the work happens.

Counting Live Entries Without A Scan

The second class in this lesson is an authentication manager. It hands out tokens that expire after a fixed time to live, called ttl. The method generate creates a token, renew extends a live token, and count reports how many tokens are still live.

A method that counts live entries needs more than validation, because a scan of all entries costs O(size). When every entry gets the same time to live and the clock never moves backward, the deadlines appear in the order of the latest touch. A map that keeps insertion order then holds the earliest deadline at its front. A purge removes entries from the front until it finds one that is live, and the size after the purge is the exact count. When time to live differs per entry, the order breaks, and the min-heap takes over.

State

Fields Of The Rate Limiter

The trace follows a rate limiter with gap 10 and then an authentication manager with a fixed time to live.

  • gap is the number of time units after a print during which the same text stays blocked.
  • deadline is the stored value for one key, equal to the latest accepted time plus gap.
  • now is the clock reading passed to the call, and it never decreases from one call to the next.
  • ttl is the fixed time to live of a token in the authentication manager.
  • expiry is the stored deadline of one token, equal to its latest generate or renew time plus ttl.

The map is rewritten only when a call is accepted. A rejected call leaves every deadline unchanged. For the authentication manager, the front of the ordered map always holds the smallest expiry.

Walkthrough

Tracing Two Expiring Maps

The Rate Limiter With Gap Ten

The first trace replays six prints with gap 10, and the pointer i marks the call in progress. Text a arrives at time 1 with no stored deadline, so it prints and the map records 11. Text b at time 3 records 13. A second a at time 8 faces deadline 11, stays blocked and leaves the map alone. When a returns at time 11, its deadline equals the clock, so the entry no longer counts, and the print goes through with a new deadline of 21. Text b at time 11 is still blocked by 13. At time 13 the same text meets its deadline exactly and prints. Calls with equal times can differ in outcome, since each decision reads only the deadline of its own key.

The Authentication Manager With Time To Live Five

The second trace feeds seven calls to a manager with ttl 5, and the pointer i marks the call in progress. Token A generated at 0 expires at 5, and token B generated at 2 expires at 7. Renewing A at 4 succeeds because 4 is below 5. It receives expiry 9 and moves behind B. The count at 5 inspects the front, finds B with expiry 7, removes nothing and reports 2. At 7 the front entry B has reached its expiry, so the purge deletes it before the renew looks for it, and the renew finds nothing to extend. The count at 7 therefore reports 1. The count at 9 deletes A and reports 0. The strict comparison has to run before any mutation, as the renew at 7 shows.

Code

Two Maps With Validated Reads

The Rate Limiter

The class stores one deadline per text. The decision reads the stored deadline and the clock only. The optional method purge removes entries that are not live and never changes any answer.

final class RateLogger {
    private final long gap;
    private final java.util.HashMap<String, Long> deadline = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    RateLogger(long gap) { this.gap = gap; }

    boolean shouldPrint(long now, String text) {
        Long d = deadline.get(text);                       // one hash lookup, no scan of other texts
        if (d != null && now < d) return false;            // still live, so the print is blocked
        deadline.put(text, now + gap);                     // accepted, so the deadline moves forward
        return true;
    }

    void purge(long now) {
        deadline.values().removeIf(d -> d <= now);         // physical cleanup only, answers do not change
    }
}

The Authentication Manager

This class uses one fixed ttl. The ordered map moves a renewed token to the back, so the front always holds the smallest expiry. Every public method purges the front first.

final class AuthManager {
    private final long ttl;
    private final java.util.LinkedHashMap<String, Long> expiry = new java.util.LinkedHashMap<>();

    AuthManager(long ttl) { this.ttl = ttl; }

    private void purge(long now) {
        var it = expiry.entrySet().iterator();
        while (it.hasNext() && it.next().getValue() <= now) it.remove();   // stops at the first live entry
    }

    void generate(String id, long now) {
        purge(now);
        expiry.remove(id);                                 // a re-generated id moves to the back
        expiry.put(id, now + ttl);
    }

    void renew(String id, long now) {
        purge(now);                                        // an entry that ended at now is gone before the check
        if (expiry.remove(id) != null) expiry.put(id, now + ttl);   // only a live token is renewed
    }

    int countLive(long now) { purge(now); return expiry.size(); }
}

Cost

  • RateLogger.shouldPrint costs O(1) expected time. Memory is O(d) for d distinct texts until a purge runs.
  • AuthManager costs O(1) amortized time per call, because each entry is removed once, and O(t) space for t live tokens.
When to use it

When To Store A Deadline

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when entries become invalid after a time and the class answers with the clock as part of the question. Words such as "within the last", "expires after" or "renew" in a problem statement signal it. Before coding, write the stream kind, the exact meaning of equality at the deadline, and whether the clock can repeat a value. The invariant to keep is that each read compares the stored deadline with now before it uses the entry. Physical removal is a separate optimization and never decides an answer.

A False Friend

A fixed-size sliding window over the last k values is a false friend. It looks like expiry, but entries leave by count and not by a deadline, so a deque of values is enough and no per-key deadline exists. A second false friend is a cache with least-recently-used eviction. It removes entries because of capacity, and a removed entry was still valid.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If the clock can move backward, a purge from the front loses entries that become valid again, and the class needs a different design. If entries must be reported in the order they expired, a plain map does not keep that order, and a heap or an ordered map keyed by deadline is the starting point. If many threads share the map, the check and the update need one lock, which this lesson does not cover.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Logger Rate Limiter

LeetCode 359

Problem Specification. Design the class Logger with a constructor that receives a positive integer gap. The method shouldPrint(timestamp, message) returns true and records the print when no print of message exists with a timestamp greater than timestamp - gap. Otherwise it returns false and records nothing.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Gap is from 1 to 10^9.
  • Timestamps are long values from 0 to 10^12 and never decrease between calls. Equal timestamps are allowed.
  • Messages are non-empty strings of at most 30 lowercase letters.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.

Example 1. Input calls ["Logger","shouldPrint","shouldPrint","shouldPrint","shouldPrint"] with arguments [[4],[2,"x"],[5,"x"],[6,"x"],[6,"y"]], output [null,true,false,true,true].

Example 2. Input calls ["Logger","shouldPrint","shouldPrint","shouldPrint"] with arguments [[3],[0,"q"],[0,"q"],[3,"q"]], output [null,true,false,true].

Prerequisites. The deadline and live definitions from this lesson. The stream is expiring: each printed text blocks itself for a fixed gap.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the class stores the next allowed time per message and never stores a history.

Hint

Which single number per message is enough to decide the next call, and when does a rejected call write it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps one map from message to the first timestamp at which that message may print again. A call looks up its message. When a deadline exists and the call time is below it, the call returns false and writes nothing. Otherwise the call stores timestamp + gap and returns true. The invariant is that the stored deadline of a message equals the latest accepted timestamp plus gap, so no earlier print can matter. A call at exactly the deadline prints, because the print at timestamp - gap lies outside the blocking window.

The method purge removes entries whose deadline is at or below the given time. The harness runs purges at random moments and asserts that no answer changes, which shows that physical cleanup is separate from validity. The oracle keeps the full list of accepted prints and scans it.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, because one hash lookup and at most one hash write run, and purge costs O(size) when it is called.
  • Space is O(d) for d distinct messages, since each message keeps one long, and a purge lowers it to the messages still blocked.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class LoggerBuild {
    static final class Logger {
        private final long gap;
        private final HashMap<String, Long> deadline = new HashMap<>();

        Logger(long gap) {
            // The gap is fixed for the object, so every deadline uses one rule.
            this.gap = gap;
        }

        /**
         * Decides whether a message may print at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) extra per new message.
         * Invariant: deadline.get(m) equals the latest accepted time of m plus gap.
         */
        boolean shouldPrint(long timestamp, String message) {
            // One lookup finds the only state that can block this message.
            Long d = deadline.get(message);
            // A stored deadline above the clock means the earlier print still blocks.
            if (d != null && timestamp < d) return false;
            // The call is accepted, so the deadline moves to timestamp + gap.
            deadline.put(message, timestamp + gap);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Removes entries that no longer block anything.
         * Time: O(size). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: removed entries had deadline <= now, so answers do not change.
         */
        void purge(long now) {
            // Removal only touches entries that validation already ignores.
            deadline.values().removeIf(d -> d <= now);
        }

        int size() { return deadline.size(); }
    }

    /** Brute force: scans every accepted print. */
    static final class Oracle {
        private final long gap;
        private final List<String> texts = new ArrayList<>();
        private final List<Long> times = new ArrayList<>();
        Oracle(long gap) { this.gap = gap; }
        boolean shouldPrint(long t, String m) {
            // Blocked when some accepted print of m has time greater than t - gap.
            for (int i = 0; i < texts.size(); i++)
                if (texts.get(i).equals(m) && times.get(i) > t - gap) return false;
            texts.add(m);
            times.add(t);
            return true;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the exercise.
        Logger a = new Logger(4);
        if (!a.shouldPrint(2, "x") || a.shouldPrint(5, "x") || !a.shouldPrint(6, "x") || !a.shouldPrint(6, "y"))
            throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        Logger b = new Logger(3);
        if (!b.shouldPrint(0, "q") || b.shouldPrint(0, "q") || !b.shouldPrint(3, "q")) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Large timestamps stay exact because the deadline is a long.
        Logger big = new Logger(1_000_000_000L);
        if (!big.shouldPrint(1_000_000_000_000L, "z") || big.shouldPrint(1_000_000_000_000L + 999_999_999L, "z")
            || !big.shouldPrint(1_000_000_000_000L + 1_000_000_000L, "z")) throw new AssertionError("long width");
        // Random streams compare every answer with the brute force, with random purges in between.
        Random rnd = new Random(3801L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            long gap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            Logger real = new Logger(gap);
            Oracle model = new Oracle(gap);
            long now = rnd.nextInt(3);
            int calls = rnd.nextInt(30);
            for (int c = 0; c < calls; c++) {
                // The clock repeats or advances, never decreases.
                now += rnd.nextInt(4);
                String m = String.valueOf((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(4)));
                if (real.shouldPrint(now, m) != model.shouldPrint(now, m)) throw new AssertionError("answer differs");
                if (rnd.nextInt(5) == 0) {
                    real.purge(now);
                    // After a purge every stored message is still blocked, so memory tracks live entries.
                    if (real.size() > 4) throw new AssertionError("purge left too much");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Authentication Manager

LeetCode 1797

Problem Specification. Design the class AuthenticationManager with a constructor that receives timeToLive. The method generate(tokenId, currentTime) creates a token that ends at currentTime + timeToLive. The method renew(tokenId, currentTime) sets the end of a token to currentTime + timeToLive only when the token exists and has not ended at currentTime. Otherwise it does nothing. The method countUnexpiredTokens(currentTime) returns the number of tokens that have not ended at currentTime. A token ends at time e when currentTime >= e.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Time to live is from 1 to 10^9.
  • Current times are long values from 1 to 10^12 and never decrease between calls. Equal values are allowed.
  • Token ids are non-empty strings of at most 5 lowercase letters or digits, and generate may reuse an id that has ended.
  • Calls number at most 2000 per object.

Example 1. Input calls ["AuthenticationManager","generate","generate","renew","countUnexpiredTokens","countUnexpiredTokens"] with arguments [[6],["p",1],["q",3],["p",6],[8],[12]], output [null,null,null,null,2,0].

Example 2. Input calls ["AuthenticationManager","generate","renew","countUnexpiredTokens","generate","countUnexpiredTokens"] with arguments [[2],["m",5],["m",7],[7],["m",7],[8]], output [null,null,null,0,null,1].

Prerequisites. The Logger Rate Limiter exercise above. This exercise adds renewal and a count of live entries, so a read must also drop ended entries from the count.

What This Exercise Changes. The class now counts, so validation on each read is not enough. The ordered map lets a purge from the front keep the size exact.

Hint

If every token has the same time to live and the clock never decreases, in what order do the expiry times appear when tokens are sorted by their latest touch?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Every token has the same time to live and the clock never decreases. The expiry of a token is therefore its latest generate or renew time plus timeToLive, so tokens sorted by latest touch are also sorted by expiry. A LinkedHashMap keeps tokens in that order when a renewed or regenerated token is removed and put again at the back. The front of the map always holds the smallest expiry.

Each public method first purges the front while the front expiry is at or below the current time. After the purge, every remaining token is live, so countUnexpiredTokens returns the map size. A renew then needs only to test membership, because an ended token was just removed. The invariant is that the map holds exactly the tokens with expiry above the latest clock reading, in non-decreasing expiry order. The oracle stores expiries in a HashMap and counts them with a full scan.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because each token enters the map once per generate or renew and leaves the front at most once for each entry.
  • Space is O(t) for t tokens not yet purged, and the purge on every call keeps t equal to the live tokens after the call.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.LinkedHashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class AuthenticationManagerVary {
    static final class AuthenticationManager {
        private final long timeToLive;
        private final LinkedHashMap<String, Long> expiry = new LinkedHashMap<>();

        AuthenticationManager(long timeToLive) {
            // The shared time to live keeps expiry order equal to touch order.
            this.timeToLive = timeToLive;
        }

        /**
         * Removes tokens from the front while they have ended.
         * Time: O(r) for r removed entries, amortized O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterwards every stored expiry is above now.
         */
        private void purge(long now) {
            Iterator<Map.Entry<String, Long>> it = expiry.entrySet().iterator();
            // The loop stops at the first live entry, because later entries end later.
            while (it.hasNext() && it.next().getValue() <= now) it.remove();
        }

        /**
         * Creates or recreates a token.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per new token.
         * Invariant: the token sits at the back with the largest expiry.
         */
        void generate(String tokenId, long currentTime) {
            purge(currentTime);
            // Removing first makes a reused id move to the back instead of keeping an old slot.
            expiry.remove(tokenId);
            expiry.put(tokenId, currentTime + timeToLive);
        }

        /**
         * Renews a token that has not ended.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: an ended token is absent, so presence means live.
         */
        void renew(String tokenId, long currentTime) {
            purge(currentTime);
            // Presence after the purge proves the token has not ended at currentTime.
            if (expiry.remove(tokenId) != null) expiry.put(tokenId, currentTime + timeToLive);
        }

        /**
         * Counts tokens that have not ended.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: after the purge the map size is the live count.
         */
        int countUnexpiredTokens(long currentTime) {
            purge(currentTime);
            return expiry.size();
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: counts by scanning every expiry. */
    static final class Oracle {
        private final long ttl;
        private final HashMap<String, Long> expiry = new HashMap<>();
        Oracle(long ttl) { this.ttl = ttl; }
        void generate(String id, long now) { expiry.put(id, now + ttl); }
        void renew(String id, long now) {
            Long e = expiry.get(id);
            if (e != null && e > now) expiry.put(id, now + ttl);
        }
        int count(long now) {
            int c = 0;
            for (long e : expiry.values()) if (e > now) c++;
            return c;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: renewing before the end keeps a token, and the count falls to 0 later.
        AuthenticationManager m1 = new AuthenticationManager(6);
        m1.generate("p", 1); m1.generate("q", 3); m1.renew("p", 6);
        if (m1.countUnexpiredTokens(8) != 2 || m1.countUnexpiredTokens(12) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a renew at the exact end time is ignored, and an id can be generated again.
        AuthenticationManager m2 = new AuthenticationManager(2);
        m2.generate("m", 5); m2.renew("m", 7);
        if (m2.countUnexpiredTokens(7) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 renew at expiry");
        m2.generate("m", 7);
        if (m2.countUnexpiredTokens(8) != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 regenerate");
        // Large times keep exact arithmetic with long.
        AuthenticationManager big = new AuthenticationManager(1_000_000_000L);
        big.generate("t", 1_000_000_000_000L);
        if (big.countUnexpiredTokens(1_000_000_000_000L + 999_999_999L) != 1
            || big.countUnexpiredTokens(1_000_000_000_000L + 1_000_000_000L) != 0) throw new AssertionError("long width");
        // Random call sequences compare every count with the brute force.
        Random rnd = new Random(3802L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            long ttl = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            AuthenticationManager real = new AuthenticationManager(ttl);
            Oracle model = new Oracle(ttl);
            long now = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int c = rnd.nextInt(40); c > 0; c--) {
                // The clock repeats or advances, never decreases.
                now += rnd.nextInt(4);
                String id = String.valueOf((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(5)));
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) { real.generate(id, now); model.generate(id, now); }
                else if (op == 1) { real.renew(id, now); model.renew(id, now); }
                else if (real.countUnexpiredTokens(now) != model.count(now)) throw new AssertionError("count differs");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Renew At Expiry

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class LeaseBook with a constructor that receives ttl. A lease for a key starts at the time of a successful acquire and ends at that time plus ttl. The method acquire(key, now) returns true and starts a lease when the key has no lease or its lease has ended at now. Otherwise it returns false and changes nothing. The method renew(key, now) returns true and moves the end to now + ttl when the lease has not ended at now. Otherwise it returns false. The method isHeld(key, now) returns whether the lease has not ended at now. A lease has ended at now when now is greater than or equal to its end.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Time to live is from 1 to 10^12.
  • Times are long values from 0 to 10^12 and never decrease between calls. Equal values are allowed.
  • Keys are non-empty strings of at most 8 lowercase letters.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Mutation by a returned false is forbidden: a rejected call leaves every lease as it was.

Example 1. Input calls ["LeaseBook","acquire","renew","isHeld","renew","acquire"] with arguments [[5],["k",2],["k",6],["k",11],["k",11],["k",11]], output [null,true,true,false,false,true].

Example 2. Input calls ["LeaseBook","acquire","acquire","isHeld","acquire","renew"] with arguments [[3],["a",0],["a",2],["a",3],["a",3],["a",5]], output [null,true,false,false,true,true].

Prerequisites. The Authentication Manager exercise above. This exercise drops the count and tests only the meaning of equality at the deadline, for acquiring as well as renewing.

What This Exercise Changes. One strict comparison now guards three methods. An acquire at the exact end time succeeds, while a renew at the exact end time fails.

Hint

Which comparison decides every method, and does it run before or after the method writes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores one end time per key in a HashMap and never purges; it relies on validated reads. A private method held returns true only when an end time exists and the end is strictly greater than now. All three public methods call held before they write, so the equality case is decided once. At now equal to the end, held is false. acquire therefore succeeds and starts a new lease, renew fails and writes nothing, and isHeld returns false.

The invariant is that a rejected call writes no field, and a stored end time that is at or below the clock is treated as missing. The oracle stores the start time of each lease and tests now - start < ttl, which is a different representation of the same rule. The harness also asserts the three equality cases directly, since the random clock often lands on them.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, because each call performs one lookup and at most one write.
  • Space is O(k) for k distinct keys ever acquired, since validated reads leave ended entries in the map until they are overwritten.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RenewAtExpiryBoundary {
    static final class LeaseBook {
        private final long ttl;
        private final HashMap<String, Long> end = new HashMap<>();

        LeaseBook(long ttl) {
            // One lease length applies to every key.
            this.ttl = ttl;
        }

        /**
         * Tells whether the key holds a lease that has not ended.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the comparison is strict, so now == end means ended.
         */
        private boolean held(String key, long now) {
            Long e = end.get(key);
            // A missing entry and an entry with end <= now are both not held.
            return e != null && e > now;
        }

        /**
         * Starts a lease when none is held.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) per new key.
         * Invariant: a false result writes nothing.
         */
        boolean acquire(String key, long now) {
            // The check runs before any write, so a held lease stays untouched.
            if (held(key, now)) return false;
            end.put(key, now + ttl);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Extends a lease that is held.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: an ended lease is never revived by renew.
         */
        boolean renew(String key, long now) {
            // At now == end the lease has ended, so renew must refuse.
            if (!held(key, now)) return false;
            end.put(key, now + ttl);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Reports whether the lease has not ended.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the call changes no field.
         */
        boolean isHeld(String key, long now) {
            return held(key, now);
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: stores the start of the latest lease and tests elapsed time. */
    static final class Oracle {
        private final long ttl;
        private final HashMap<String, Long> start = new HashMap<>();
        Oracle(long ttl) { this.ttl = ttl; }
        boolean held(String k, long now) { Long s = start.get(k); return s != null && now - s < ttl; }
        boolean acquire(String k, long now) { if (held(k, now)) return false; start.put(k, now); return true; }
        boolean renew(String k, long now) { if (!held(k, now)) return false; start.put(k, now); return true; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: at the exact end time the lease has ended, so renew fails and acquire succeeds.
        LeaseBook a = new LeaseBook(5);
        if (!a.acquire("k", 2) || !a.renew("k", 6) || a.isHeld("k", 11) || a.renew("k", 11) || !a.acquire("k", 11))
            throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a rejected acquire changes nothing, and an acquire at the end time starts a new lease.
        LeaseBook b = new LeaseBook(3);
        if (!b.acquire("a", 0) || b.acquire("a", 2) || b.isHeld("a", 3) || !b.acquire("a", 3) || !b.renew("a", 5))
            throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The rejected acquire at time 2 must not have moved the end from 3 to 5.
        LeaseBook c = new LeaseBook(3);
        c.acquire("z", 0); c.acquire("z", 2);
        if (c.isHeld("z", 3)) throw new AssertionError("rejected call wrote state");
        // Large values stay exact with long.
        LeaseBook big = new LeaseBook(1_000_000_000_000L);
        big.acquire("w", 1_000_000_000_000L);
        if (!big.isHeld("w", 1_999_999_999_999L) || big.isHeld("w", 2_000_000_000_000L)) throw new AssertionError("long width");
        // Random sequences compare all three methods with the brute force.
        Random rnd = new Random(3803L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            long ttl = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            LeaseBook real = new LeaseBook(ttl);
            Oracle model = new Oracle(ttl);
            long now = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int s = rnd.nextInt(40); s > 0; s--) {
                // Small clock steps make many calls land exactly on an end time.
                now += rnd.nextInt(3);
                String key = String.valueOf((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean r, m;
                if (op == 0) { r = real.acquire(key, now); m = model.acquire(key, now); }
                else if (op == 1) { r = real.renew(key, now); m = model.renew(key, now); }
                else { r = real.isHeld(key, now); m = model.held(key, now); }
                if (r != m) throw new AssertionError("op " + op + " differs");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Expiring Key Store

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class ExpiringStore with methods put(key, value, ttl, now), get(key, now) and liveCount(now). The method put stores the value under the key with end time now + ttl and replaces any earlier entry of that key. The method get returns the stored value when the entry has not ended at now, and -1 otherwise. The method liveCount returns the number of keys whose entries have not ended at now. An entry has ended at now when now is greater than or equal to its end.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Time to live per call is from 1 to 10^9.
  • Times are long values from 0 to 10^12 and never decrease between calls. Equal values are allowed.
  • Values are integers from 0 to 10^9, so -1 never collides with a stored value.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Memory after liveCount returns must be proportional to the live entries plus pending cleanup records.

Example 1. Input calls ["ExpiringStore","put","put","get","get","liveCount"] with arguments [[],["u",7,10,0],["v",8,2,1],["v",3],["u",3],[3]], output [null,null,null,-1,7,1].

Example 2. Input calls ["ExpiringStore","put","put","get","liveCount","get"] with arguments [[],["w",4,9,2],["w",5,1,3],["w",4],[4],["w",5]], output [null,null,null,-1,0,-1].

Prerequisites. The Renew At Expiry exercise above. Each entry now has its own time to live, so the expiry order no longer matches the order of the latest touch.

What This Exercise Changes. Per-entry time to live breaks the front-of-the-map order. Validated reads stay correct, and a min-heap of expiry records with a check against the map keeps the count and the memory exact.

Hint

When a shorter time to live overwrites a longer one, which stored record must a cleanup step ignore, and how does it recognize that record?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Two designs share one rule: an entry is live when its end is strictly above now. The lazy design keeps a HashMap and validates every get. Its liveCount scans all entries, which costs O(size), and its memory keeps every ended key until it is overwritten. The heap design adds a min-heap of cleanup records ordered by end time. A record holds the end time and the key. Each method first pops records whose end is at or below now. It removes the map entry only when the entry for that key still has the same end time. An overwrite leaves the older record in the heap, and the check against the map makes that record harmless when it surfaces.

The invariant of the heap design is that every live entry has a record in the heap, and a popped record deletes the entry only when it describes the entry's current end. After a purge, the map size equals the live count, so liveCount is O(1) after the pops. The design never calls PriorityQueue.remove(Object), which costs O(n). The extra memory is one record per put, and those records leave the heap when their time passes.

The harness runs both designs and the oracle on random streams and asserts that all three agree. It also asserts that the heap design holds no ended entries in its map after liveCount, while the lazy design may hold some.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log h) amortized per put, get and liveCount in the heap design, where h is the heap size, because each record is pushed and popped once.
  • Space is O(h) for h records pushed and not yet popped, which counts live entries plus overwritten or ended ones waiting for their time. The lazy design uses O(k) for k distinct keys and answers liveCount in O(k).
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ExpiringKeyStoreRecognize {
    /** Lazy design: validation on read, no cleanup. */
    static final class LazyStore {
