        private final HashMap<String, long[]> map = new HashMap<>();   // value, end

        /**
         * Stores a value with its own time to live.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) per new key.
         * Invariant: the entry of a key holds its latest value and end.
         */
        void put(String key, long value, long ttl, long now) {
            // Overwriting replaces both fields at once.
            map.put(key, new long[]{value, now + ttl});
        }

        /**
         * Reads a value that has not ended.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: an entry with end <= now is never returned.
         */
        long get(String key, long now) {
            long[] e = map.get(key);
            // The strict comparison hides ended entries without deleting them.
            return e != null && e[1] > now ? e[0] : -1;
        }

        /**
         * Counts entries that have not ended.
         * Time: O(k) for k stored keys. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the count uses the same comparison as get.
         */
        int liveCount(long now) {
            int c = 0;
            // The scan visits every stored entry because nothing is ordered by end time.
            for (long[] e : map.values()) if (e[1] > now) c++;
            return c;
        }

        int physicalSize() { return map.size(); }
    }

    /** Heap design: records ordered by end time remove ended entries when time passes them. */
    static final class HeapStore {
        private record Rec(long end, String key) {}

        private final HashMap<String, long[]> map = new HashMap<>();   // value, end
        private final PriorityQueue<Rec> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x.end, y.end));

        /**
         * Removes every entry whose end is at or below now.
         * Time: O(r log h) for r popped records, amortized O(log h) per push. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a record deletes an entry only when it matches the entry's current end.
         */
        private void purge(long now) {
            // The smallest end sits at the top, so the loop stops at the first record still in the future.
            while (!heap.isEmpty() && heap.peek().end <= now) {
                Rec r = heap.poll();
                long[] e = map.get(r.key);
                // A mismatch means the key was overwritten, so this record is stale and is skipped.
                if (e != null && e[1] == r.end) map.remove(r.key);
            }
        }

        /**
         * Stores a value with its own time to live.
         * Time: O(log h). Space: O(1) per call for one record.
         * Invariant: the new end always has a record in the heap.
         */
        void put(String key, long value, long ttl, long now) {
            purge(now);
            long end = now + ttl;
            map.put(key, new long[]{value, end});
            // The older record, if any, stays in the heap and is skipped when it surfaces.
            heap.add(new Rec(end, key));
        }

        /**
         * Reads a value that has not ended.
         * Time: amortized O(log h). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the extra end check keeps the answer valid even without the purge.
         */
        long get(String key, long now) {
            purge(now);
            long[] e = map.get(key);
            return e != null && e[1] > now ? e[0] : -1;
        }

        /**
         * Counts entries that have not ended.
         * Time: amortized O(log h). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: after the purge the map holds only live entries.
         */
        int liveCount(long now) {
            purge(now);
            return map.size();
        }

        int physicalSize() { return map.size(); }
    }

    /** Brute force with a plain list of events replayed on every query. */
    static long oracleGet(java.util.List<long[]> puts, java.util.List<String> keys, String key, long now) {
        long best = -1;
        // The latest put of the key decides, whether or not it has ended.
        for (int i = puts.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--)
            if (keys.get(i).equals(key)) { best = puts.get(i)[1] > now ? puts.get(i)[0] : -1; break; }
        return best;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a shorter time to live ends v early, and u is still held.
        HeapStore h1 = new HeapStore();
        h1.put("u", 7, 10, 0); h1.put("v", 8, 2, 1);
        if (h1.get("v", 3) != -1 || h1.get("u", 3) != 7 || h1.liveCount(3) != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a shorter overwrite ends the key at once, and the stale longer record changes nothing.
        HeapStore h2 = new HeapStore();
        h2.put("w", 4, 9, 2); h2.put("w", 5, 1, 3);
        if (h2.get("w", 4) != -1 || h2.liveCount(4) != 0 || h2.get("w", 5) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The same two examples through the lazy design.
        LazyStore l2 = new LazyStore();
        l2.put("w", 4, 9, 2); l2.put("w", 5, 1, 3);
        if (l2.get("w", 4) != -1 || l2.liveCount(4) != 0) throw new AssertionError("lazy example 2");
        // Random streams compare the lazy design, the heap design and the brute force.
        Random rnd = new Random(3804L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            LazyStore lazy = new LazyStore();
            HeapStore heap = new HeapStore();
            java.util.List<long[]> puts = new java.util.ArrayList<>();   // value, end
            java.util.List<String> keys = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
            long now = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int s = rnd.nextInt(40); s > 0; s--) {
                // The clock repeats or advances, never decreases.
                now += rnd.nextInt(3);
                String key = String.valueOf((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(4)));
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    long value = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    long ttl = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
                    lazy.put(key, value, ttl, now);
                    heap.put(key, value, ttl, now);
                    puts.add(new long[]{value, now + ttl});
                    keys.add(key);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    long want = oracleGet(puts, keys, key, now);
                    if (lazy.get(key, now) != want || heap.get(key, now) != want) throw new AssertionError("get differs");
                } else {
                    // The oracle counts the distinct keys whose latest put has not ended.
                    int want = 0;
                    for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'd'; c++) if (oracleGet(puts, keys, String.valueOf(c), now) != -1) want++;
                    if (lazy.liveCount(now) != want || heap.liveCount(now) != want) throw new AssertionError("count differs");
                    // After liveCount the heap design stores only live entries, never more than the lazy design.
                    if (heap.physicalSize() != want || heap.physicalSize() > lazy.physicalSize())
                        throw new AssertionError("heap design kept ended entries");
                }
            }
        }
        // A long stream of one-tick entries shows memory staying small in the heap design.
        HeapStore many = new HeapStore();
        LazyStore keep = new LazyStore();
        for (int i = 0; i < 2000; i++) { many.put("k" + i, i, 1, i); keep.put("k" + i, i, 1, i); }
        many.liveCount(2000); 
        if (many.physicalSize() != 0 || keep.physicalSize() != 2000) throw new AssertionError("memory comparison");
    }
}verified

Problem

Why A Traffic Meter Falls Behind

A hosting company shows each customer a dashboard with the bytes served for the last k requests. The server appends one number per request, and the dashboard asks for the total of the newest k entries on every refresh. At launch the numbers look right. A year later two complaints arrive on the same day. Large customers see the dashboard freeze, because every refresh adds up 200000 numbers and 500 refreshes arrive each second. One customer sees a negative traffic total, because the sum was stored in a 32-bit int and wrapped past 2147483647.

The data has a property that the first design ignores. The stream is append-only: a recorded size never changes and never expires, and new entries only arrive at the end. Every query asks about the same kind of range, the newest k entries. This lesson asks how a program can answer such a query without touching those k entries again, and which kinds of values allow it.

Simple approach

Adding Up The Newest Entries

The direct design stores every size in a list. A query walks the last k positions and adds them. The class below follows the dashboard exactly, including the 32-bit total.

final class NaiveByteMeter {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Integer> sizes = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    void record(int bytes) { sizes.add(bytes); }

    int lastTotal(int k) {
        int total = 0;
        for (int i = sizes.size() - k; i < sizes.size(); i++) total += sizes.get(i);   // touches k entries
        return total;
    }
}

After the calls record(5), record(3), record(8), the call lastTotal(2) returns 11. On small tests the class is correct and quick to write.

Predict first: A meter records 200000 sizes of 100000 bytes each, and then lastTotal(200000) runs once for every one of 500 dashboard refreshes in a second. What does one call return, and how many additions does the second cost?

The true total is 20000000000, which does not fit in an int. The sum wraps and the call returns -1474836480. Each call performs 200000 additions, so 500 refreshes cost 10^8 additions in one second, and a customer with more history makes it worse. The class is slow and wrong for the same input.

Bottleneck

Repeated Sums Over The Same Entries

A query for the newest k entries costs O(k) time. Two refreshes one second apart cover almost the same entries, yet the second refresh adds them from scratch. Of the 200000 entries in the window, only a handful are new since the previous refresh, and the program learns nothing from the first sum. For q queries over a stream of n entries, the total cost is O(q * k), which reaches 10^10 additions when q and k are both about 10^5.

The int total is a second weakness, and it hides inside the same loop. A stream of n entries with sizes up to M produces totals up to n * M. Storing that value in an int fails as soon as the product passes 2^31 - 1, which takes only 21475 entries of 100000 bytes.

A better design must therefore answer the query in time that does not depend on k. It must reuse the work from earlier appends instead of recomputing it. It must also use a number type wide enough for the largest total the constraints allow.

Key idea

Subtract Two Stored Totals

One Stored Total Per Position

A prefix total is the sum of the first i entries, stored once at the moment entry i arrives. Each append computes the new prefix total from the previous one with a single addition, so an append costs O(1) time. The array sums holds sums[0] = 0 for the empty stream and sums[i] for the first i entries. The newest k entries are the entries from position n - k up to n - 1, and their total is sums[n] - sums[n - k]. The query reads two numbers and subtracts, so it costs O(1) time no matter how large k is.

Why Subtraction Is Allowed

The subtraction works because addition has an inverse: subtracting x undoes adding x. The stored total sums[n] equals the wanted total plus sums[n - k], and removing the second term leaves the first. Any operation with an inverse allows the same plan. A product has the inverse of division, so a stream of positive multipliers answers "product of the newest k" as P[n] / P[n - k], where P[i] is the product of the first i entries. Division is exact here, because P[n] is P[n - k] times the wanted product.

A Reset Restores The Inverse

The inverse fails when the value has no inverse. Division by zero is undefined, and a zero entry also makes every later stored product zero, so the stored numbers no longer carry information about earlier entries. A reset repairs this. When a zero arrives, the stream discards all stored products and starts again from the empty product 1. A query that asks for more entries than the stream holds since the last reset includes the zero, so its answer is 0.

Append-Only Is The Condition

This plan needs the stream to be append-only. A stored total includes every earlier entry, so changing or expiring entry j would change every sums[i] with i > j. The stored numbers would cost O(n) to repair. The invariant of this method is that sums[i] always equals the fold of the first i entries, and each new entry extends that fold by exactly one step.

State

Fields Behind Both Streams

The lesson uses two small classes. The trace follows the first fields of each.

  • sums is the long array of stored totals, with sums[0] = 0.
  • n is the number of entries recorded so far, and sums[n] is the total of all of them.
  • k is the number of newest entries a query asks for, from 1 to n.
  • products is the long array of stored products, where the empty product 1 sits in slot base.
  • base is the slot that holds the empty product after the latest zero.
  • last is the index of the newest entry in the traces, so it equals n - 1 and is -1 before any entry.
  • top is the slot of the product of every entry since that zero, so top - base counts those entries.

The invariant for sums is sums[i] = entry 0 + ... + entry i - 1 for every i from 0 to n. The invariant for products is the same statement with multiplication, restricted to the entries after the last zero.

Walkthrough

Following Totals And Products

Stored Totals Answer Two Queries

The first trace records the sizes 5, 3, 8, 2 and 6. The pointer last marks the index of the newest recorded entry. After the first three appends, the stored totals are 0, 5, 8 and 16. The query for the newest 2 entries computes sums[3] - sums[1], which is 16 - 5 = 11, and that equals 3 + 8. Two more appends bring the stored total to 24. The query for the newest 4 entries computes sums[5] - sums[1], which is 24 - 5 = 19. The query for all 5 entries uses sums[0] = 0 and returns 24. Every answer takes one subtraction, and the appends never revisit earlier entries.

A Zero Resets The Stored Products

The second trace records the multipliers 4, 3, 0, 5, 2 and 6. The pointer last marks the index of the newest entry, and the pointer zero marks the position of the latest zero, or -1 before any zero. The first two entries store the products 1, 4 and 12, and the query for 2 entries returns 12 / 1 = 12. The zero arrives, the stored list shrinks back to the single value 1, and zero moves to position 2. The entries 5, 2 and 6 rebuild the list as 1, 5, 10 and 60. A query for 3 entries returns 60 / 1 = 60. A query for 2 entries returns 60 / 5 = 12. A query for 4 entries asks for more than the three entries since the zero, so it returns 0 without any division.

Code

Two Streams With Stored Totals

The Byte Meter

The meter keeps a growing long array and the count n. The array doubles when it fills, so appends cost O(1) amortized time.

final class ByteMeter {
    private long[] sums = new long[8];     // sums[i] is the total of the first i sizes
    private int n;                         // number of sizes recorded

    void record(long bytes) {
        if (n + 1 == sums.length) sums = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(sums, sums.length * 2);   // grow, amortized O(1)
        sums[n + 1] = sums[n] + bytes;                                                    // extend the total by one step
        n++;
    }

    long lastTotal(int k) {
        if (k < 1 || k > n) throw new IllegalArgumentException("k out of range");
        return sums[n] - sums[n - k];                                                     // two reads and one subtraction
    }
}

The Growth Factor With A Reset

The growth tracker stores multipliers as long values. The field base is the array slot that holds the empty product after the latest zero, so a reset moves one index and copies nothing.

final class GrowthTracker {
    private long[] products = new long[8];
    private int base;           // slot of the empty product after the latest zero
    private int top;            // slot of the product of every entry since that zero

    GrowthTracker() { products[0] = 1; }

    void record(int factor) {
        if (factor == 0) {                                  // a zero has no inverse, so history ends here
            base = top + 1;
            top = base;
            ensure(top);
            products[top] = 1;                              // the empty product starts the new history
            return;
        }
        top++;
        ensure(top);
        products[top] = products[top - 1] * factor;         // extend the product by one step
    }

    long lastProduct(int k) {
        if (k > top - base) return 0;                       // the range reaches back to a zero
        return products[top] / products[top - k];           // exact division, because both slots lie after the zero
    }

    private void ensure(int slot) {
        if (slot >= products.length) products = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(products, products.length * 2);   // amortized O(1)
    }
}

Cost

  • ByteMeter costs O(1) amortized time per record, O(1) time per query and O(n) space for the stored totals.
  • GrowthTracker costs O(1) amortized time per record, O(1) time per query and O(n) space, including slots from before a zero.
When to use it

When Stored Totals Answer Queries

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when entries only arrive and never change, and each query asks for a fold over the newest k entries or over any range. Before coding, name the fold, check that it has an inverse, and decide what happens at a zero or another value that destroys the inverse. Then choose the number type from the largest stored total, not from the largest single entry. The invariant to keep is that every stored number equals the fold of everything before its position, and an append extends it by one step.

A False Friend

The maximum of the newest k entries looks like the same query, and it is a false friend of stored totals. A maximum has no inverse, because knowing the maximum of the first n entries and of the first n - k entries does not reveal the maximum of the last k. That query needs a structure that keeps only candidates, which the stock span exercise below builds. A second false friend is a stored product over values that may be zero, if the code divides without a reset.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If entries change after they arrive, every stored number after the changed position is wrong, and a binary indexed tree, covered in chapter 31, fits better. If entries expire by timestamp and the question concerns the live ones, the expiry lessons of this chapter apply. If the totals can exceed the widest primitive type and no modulus is stated, the stored numbers need arbitrary-precision arithmetic, which changes the cost of each step.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Product Prefix Stream

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class ProductStream with a no-argument constructor. The method append(v) adds the integer v to the end of the stream. The method last(k) returns the product of the newest k appended integers as a long. The class never loops over k entries when last runs.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values v are integers from 1 to 9.
  • Appends number at most 18 per object.
  • Query k satisfies 1 <= k <= the number of values appended so far.
  • Result of last fits in a long for every allowed call.

Example 1. Input calls ["ProductStream","append","append","append","last","last"] with arguments [[],[2],[3],[4],[2],[3]], output [null,null,null,null,12,24].

Example 2. Input calls ["ProductStream","append","append","append","append","last","last","last"] with arguments [[],[5],[1],[7],[2],[1],[4],[3]], output [null,null,null,null,null,2,70,14].

Prerequisites. The stored totals and the division rule from this lesson. The stream is append-only and every value is positive.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the stored fold is a product, division replaces subtraction, and positivity guarantees that every division is exact and safe.

Hint

Which value does the stored list start with, so that a query for all entries needs no special case?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores p, where p[i] is the product of the first i appended values and p[0] is 1. An append multiplies the newest stored product by the value and writes the result in the next slot. A query for the newest k values reads p[n] and p[n - k] and divides. The division is exact because p[n] equals p[n - k] times the wanted product, and it never divides by zero because every value is at least 1. The limits keep p[n] at most 9^18, which is below 2^63, so long holds every stored number.

The program replays the examples, then compares the class with a loop over the newest k values on random streams. It also checks that the largest allowed stored product fits in a long.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per append and per query, since each call performs one multiplication or one division.
  • Space is O(n) for the n stored products.
import java.util.Random;

public final class ProductPrefixBuild {
    static final class ProductStream {
        private final long[] p = new long[19];   // p[i] is the product of the first i values
        private int n;                           // number of values appended

        ProductStream() {
            // The empty product is 1, so a query for all values needs no special case.
            p[0] = 1;
        }

        /**
         * Appends one value.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1) beyond the stored slot.
         * Invariant: p[n] equals the product of all appended values.
         */
        void append(int v) {
            // One multiplication extends the previous product by one step.
            p[n + 1] = p[n] * v;
            // The count moves only after the slot is written.
            n++;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the product of the newest k values.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: p[n] = p[n - k] * answer, so the division has no remainder.
         */
        long last(int k) {
            // Both stored products come from one array, and the divisor is at least 1.
            return p[n] / p[n - k];
        }
    }

    /** Multiplies the newest k values by a loop, used as the oracle. */
    static long brute(int[] vals, int count, int k) {
        long r = 1;
        // Walks exactly the k newest positions.
        for (int i = count - k; i < count; i++) r *= vals[i];
        return r;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: values 2, 3, 4.
        ProductStream a = new ProductStream();
        a.append(2); a.append(3); a.append(4);
        if (a.last(2) != 12 || a.last(3) != 24) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: values 5, 1, 7, 2.
        ProductStream b = new ProductStream();
        b.append(5); b.append(1); b.append(7); b.append(2);
        if (b.last(1) != 2 || b.last(4) != 70 || b.last(3) != 14) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The largest allowed total, 18 nines, fits in a long.
        ProductStream big = new ProductStream();
        for (int i = 0; i < 18; i++) big.append(9);
        long nines = 1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 18; i++) nines *= 9;
        if (big.last(18) != nines || nines <= 0) throw new AssertionError("width");
        // Random streams compare every query with the loop oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(3801L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(18);
            int[] vals = new int[n];
            ProductStream s = new ProductStream();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                vals[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(9);
                s.append(vals[i]);
                int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(i + 1);
                if (s.last(k) != brute(vals, i + 1, k)) throw new AssertionError("random query");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Product of the Last K Numbers

LeetCode 1352

Problem Specification. Design the class ProductOfNumbers with a no-argument constructor. The method add(num) appends the integer num. The method getProduct(k) returns the product of the newest k appended integers. The class must answer getProduct without a loop over k entries.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values num are integers from 0 to 100.
  • Calls number at most 4 * 10^4 per object.
  • Query k satisfies 1 <= k <= the number of values added so far.
  • Result of every getProduct call fits in a 32-bit signed integer.

Example 1. Input calls ["ProductOfNumbers","add","add","add","add","add","getProduct","getProduct","getProduct"] with arguments [[],[6],[2],[0],[5],[4],[2],[3],[1]], output [null,null,null,null,null,null,20,0,4].

Example 2. Input calls ["ProductOfNumbers","add","add","add","getProduct","add","add","getProduct","add","getProduct","getProduct"] with arguments [[],[7],[3],[2],[3],[0],[4],[2],[5],[2],[3]], output [null,null,null,null,42,null,null,0,null,20,0].

Prerequisites. The Product Prefix Stream exercise above. This exercise changes the values: zero is now allowed.

What This Exercise Changes. A zero has no inverse, so the stored list is discarded when a zero arrives and a query that reaches back to that zero returns 0 directly.

Hint

After a zero arrives, which stored numbers still help, and which query sizes can reach back to the zero?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps the stored products of the values after the latest zero, starting from the empty product 1. A nonzero value multiplies the newest stored product and appends the result. A zero has no inverse and also turns every later product into zero, so the class clears the list back to the single value 1. For getProduct(k), the list holds size - 1 values since the zero. When k is larger than that count, the range contains the zero and the answer is 0. Otherwise the answer is the newest stored product divided by the stored product k slots earlier, and the division is exact because both lie after the zero.

The program replays both examples and then compares the class with a loop over the whole stream on random sequences that contain many zeros.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because a clear discards stored values that each earlier add paid for once.
  • Space is O(n) for the values since the latest zero.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ProductLastKVary {
    static final class ProductOfNumbers {
        private final List<Long> stored = new ArrayList<>();   // products of the values after the latest zero

        ProductOfNumbers() {
            // The empty product 1 anchors every division.
            stored.add(1L);
        }

        /**
         * Appends one number.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) amortized.
         * Invariant: stored holds 1 followed by the running products since the latest zero.
         */
        void add(int num) {
            // A zero ends the useful history, so the list restarts from the empty product.
            if (num == 0) {
                stored.clear();
                stored.add(1L);
            } else {
                // A nonzero value extends the newest product by one step.
                stored.add(stored.get(stored.size() - 1) * num);
            }
        }

        /**
         * Returns the product of the newest k numbers.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the list has size - 1 entries after the latest zero.
         */
        long getProduct(int k) {
            int size = stored.size();
            // More entries requested than the list holds means the range includes the zero.
            if (k > size - 1) return 0;
            // Both stored products lie after the zero, so the quotient is exact.
            return stored.get(size - 1) / stored.get(size - 1 - k);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a zero sits inside the range of the second query.
        ProductOfNumbers a = new ProductOfNumbers();
        for (int v : new int[]{6, 2, 0, 5, 4}) a.add(v);
        if (a.getProduct(2) != 20 || a.getProduct(3) != 0 || a.getProduct(1) != 4) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: queries before and after a zero.
        ProductOfNumbers b = new ProductOfNumbers();
        b.add(7); b.add(3); b.add(2);
        if (b.getProduct(3) != 42) throw new AssertionError("example 2 first");
        b.add(0); b.add(4);
        if (b.getProduct(2) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 zero");
        b.add(5);
        if (b.getProduct(2) != 20 || b.getProduct(3) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 end");
        // Random streams with frequent zeros compare with a full loop.
        Random rnd = new Random(3802L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(14);
            int[] vals = new int[n];
            ProductOfNumbers s = new ProductOfNumbers();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                vals[i] = rnd.nextInt(4) == 0 ? 0 : 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
                s.add(vals[i]);
                int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(i + 1);
                long expect = 1;
                for (int j = i + 1 - k; j <= i; j++) expect *= vals[j];
                if (s.getProduct(k) != expect) throw new AssertionError("random query");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Overflow Contract

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class ModProductStream with a no-argument constructor. The method append(v) adds the integer v. The method product(k) returns the product of the newest k appended integers, taken modulo 1000000007. The result is an int between 0 and 1000000006. The class answers product without a loop over k entries.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values v are integers from 1 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Query k satisfies 1 <= k <= the number of values appended so far.
  • Modulus is the prime 1000000007, and no value is a multiple of it.

Example 1. Input calls ["ModProductStream","append","append","append","product","product","product"] with arguments [[],[1000000000],[999999999],[3],[2],[3],[1]], output [null,null,null,null,999999983,168,3].

Example 2. Input calls ["ModProductStream","append","append","append","append","product","product","product"] with arguments [[],[7],[1000000000],[1000000000],[5],[2],[3],[4]], output [null,null,null,null,null,999999972,245,1715].

Prerequisites. The Product of the Last K Numbers exercise above. This exercise changes the answer to a remainder and fixes the number width.

What This Exercise Changes. Division is no longer exact integer division. The stored list divides by multiplying with a modular inverse, and every product of two residues needs a long even though each residue fits in an int.

Hint

Which intermediate value is the largest, the answer or a product of two stored numbers, and what primitive type must hold it? A modular inverse of a is a number b with a * b leaving remainder 1 after division by the modulus. For the prime 1000000007, Fermat's little theorem gives b = a^(1000000005) mod 1000000007, which fast exponentiation computes in about 30 steps.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores two arrays. The array pre[i] holds the product of the first i values modulo 1000000007, and inv[i] holds the modular inverse of pre[i]. Each append multiplies by the value for pre and by the inverse of the value for inv. The inverse of a single value comes from Fermat's little theorem, v^(M - 2) mod M, because M is prime and no value is a multiple of M. A query returns pre[n] * inv[n - k] mod M, which equals the product of the newest k values because pre[n] = pre[n - k] * answer.

The width is the contract. Each stored residue is below M, which fits in an int, but the product of two residues reaches about 10^18, which needs a long. An int product would wrap. The program shows the wrap on 10^9 times 10^9 and then compares the class with a BigInteger oracle on random streams of large values.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log M) per append for the modular power and O(1) per query, where M is the modulus.
  • Space is O(n) for the two stored arrays.
import java.math.BigInteger;
import java.util.Random;

public final class OverflowContractBoundary {
    static final long MOD = 1_000_000_007L;

    static final class ModProductStream {
        private final long[] pre = new long[100_001];   // pre[i] is the product of the first i values modulo MOD
        private final long[] inv = new long[100_001];   // inv[i] is the modular inverse of pre[i]
        private int n;                                  // number of values appended

        ModProductStream() {
            // The empty product and its inverse are both 1.
            pre[0] = 1;
            inv[0] = 1;
        }

        /**
         * Computes base^exp modulo MOD by repeated squaring.
         * Time: O(log exp). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: result * base^exp stays equal to the original power.
         */
        private static long power(long base, long exp) {
            long result = 1;
            base %= MOD;
            // Each pass handles one bit of the exponent.
            while (exp > 0) {
                // A set bit multiplies the result; both factors are below MOD, so the product fits in a long.
                if ((exp & 1) == 1) result = result * base % MOD;
                // Squaring moves to the next bit.
                base = base * base % MOD;
                exp >>= 1;
            }
            return result;
        }

        /**
         * Appends one value.
         * Time: O(log MOD). Space: O(1) beyond the stored slots.
         * Invariant: pre[i] * inv[i] % MOD == 1 for every i up to n.
         */
        void append(int v) {
            // The widening to long happens before the multiplication.
            pre[n + 1] = pre[n] * v % MOD;
            // The inverse of v is v^(MOD - 2) by Fermat's little theorem.
            inv[n + 1] = inv[n] * power(v, MOD - 2) % MOD;
            n++;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the product of the newest k values modulo MOD.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: pre[n] = pre[n - k] * answer modulo MOD.
         */
        int product(int k) {
            // Multiplying by the stored inverse plays the role of division.
            return (int) (pre[n] * inv[n - k] % MOD);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: 10^9 and 999999999 are -7 and -8 modulo MOD.
        ModProductStream a = new ModProductStream();
        a.append(1_000_000_000); a.append(999_999_999); a.append(3);
        if (a.product(2) != 999_999_983 || a.product(3) != 168 || a.product(1) != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: the newest two values are 10^9 and 5.
        ModProductStream b = new ModProductStream();
        b.append(7); b.append(1_000_000_000); b.append(1_000_000_000); b.append(5);
        if (b.product(2) != 999_999_972 || b.product(3) != 245 || b.product(4) != 1715) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // An int product of two large values wraps, while the long product keeps the true value.
        int wrapped = 1_000_000_000 * 1_000_000_000;
        long exact = 1_000_000_000L * 1_000_000_000L;
        if (wrapped == exact || exact != 1_000_000_000_000_000_000L || wrapped >= 0) throw new AssertionError("width demonstration");
        // Random streams of large values compare with a BigInteger product of the newest k values.
        Random rnd = new Random(3803L);
        BigInteger mod = BigInteger.valueOf(MOD);
        for (int t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[] vals = new int[n];
            ModProductStream s = new ModProductStream();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                vals[i] = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 1 + rnd.nextInt(5) : 1_000_000_000 - rnd.nextInt(1000);
                s.append(vals[i]);
                int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(i + 1);
                BigInteger expect = BigInteger.ONE;
                for (int j = i + 1 - k; j <= i; j++) expect = expect.multiply(BigInteger.valueOf(vals[j]));
                if (s.product(k) != expect.mod(mod).intValueExact()) throw new AssertionError("random query");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Online Stock Span

LeetCode 901

Problem Specification. Design the class StockSpanner with a no-argument constructor. The method next(price) appends the integer price as the newest daily price. It returns the span of that day: the number of consecutive days that end on the newest day and whose prices are all less than or equal to price. The newest day counts, so the span is at least 1.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Prices are integers from 1 to 10^5.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Ties are included: an earlier price equal to the newest price belongs to the span.

Example 1. Input calls ["StockSpanner","next","next","next","next","next","next"] with arguments [[],[31],[41],[41],[26],[59],[53]], output [null,1,2,3,1,5,1].

Example 2. Input calls ["StockSpanner","next","next","next","next","next","next","next"] with arguments [[],[8],[6],[6],[7],[9],[3],[10]], output [null,1,1,2,3,5,1,7].

Prerequisites. The Overflow Contract exercise above. This exercise has no inverse operation, so stored totals do not apply.

What This Exercise Changes. The answer is a distance back to the nearest strictly greater price, which no inverse can produce. A stack of price and span pairs replaces the stored array, and each price enters and leaves the stack once.

Hint

When a new price is at least as large as an older price, can the older price ever matter to a later span on its own?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Subtraction cannot produce a span, because "the nearest earlier price above this one" has no inverse. The class keeps a stack of pairs, a price and the number of days it covers. The stack holds prices in strictly decreasing order from bottom to top, and the spans of its entries add up to the number of days so far. A new price starts with span 1. While the top entry has a price at most the new price, the class pops it and adds its span to the new span, because every day that top entry covered also has a price at most the new one. The class then pushes the new pair. The invariant is that the stack order stays strictly decreasing, so the entry below the new top is the nearest earlier price strictly above it.

Each price is pushed once and popped at most once, so a sequence of m calls does O(m) work in total. The program replays both examples and compares with a backward scan on random price streams with many ties.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because the total number of pops over m calls is at most m.
  • Space is O(m) in the worst case of strictly decreasing prices, since nothing is popped.
import java.util.Random;

public final class StockSpanRecognize {
    static final class StockSpanner {
        private final int[] prices = new int[10_001];   // stack of prices, strictly decreasing from bottom to top
        private final int[] spans = new int[10_001];    // days covered by each stacked price
        private int size;                               // number of stacked pairs

        /**
         * Appends a price and returns its span.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) amortized.
         * Invariant: stacked prices strictly decrease, and the spans add up to the days seen.
         */
        int next(int price) {
            int span = 1;
            // Pops every older price that is at most the new price.
            while (size > 0 && prices[size - 1] <= price) {
                // The popped entry covers days whose prices are also at most the new price.
                span += spans[size - 1];
                size--;
            }
            // The new pair keeps the stack strictly decreasing.
            prices[size] = price;
            spans[size] = span;
            size++;
            return span;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: ties and a drop in the sequence.
        StockSpanner a = new StockSpanner();
        int[] in1 = {31, 41, 41, 26, 59, 53};
        int[] out1 = {1, 2, 3, 1, 5, 1};
        for (int i = 0; i < in1.length; i++) if (a.next(in1[i]) != out1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        // Example 2: a long rising tail.
        StockSpanner b = new StockSpanner();
        int[] in2 = {8, 6, 6, 7, 9, 3, 10};
        int[] out2 = {1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 1, 7};
        for (int i = 0; i < in2.length; i++) if (b.next(in2[i]) != out2[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 2 at " + i);
        // Random streams with a small price range create many ties and compare with a backward scan.
        Random rnd = new Random(3804L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(20);
            int[] vals = new int[n];
            StockSpanner s = new StockSpanner();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                vals[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
                int expect = 1;
                for (int j = i - 1; j >= 0 && vals[j] <= vals[i]; j--) expect++;
                if (s.next(vals[i]) != expect) throw new AssertionError("random span");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Stream Order Statistics

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Median Query Stalls

Two Failures In Production

A metrics agent records the latency of every request and shows the median on a dashboard. The first version keeps all samples in a list and sorts a copy whenever the dashboard refreshes. After a week the list holds fifty million samples. One refresh takes several seconds, the next refresh starts before the first finishes, and the agent stops answering. The median moved by a few microseconds between two refreshes, yet each refresh sorted everything again.

A trading screen shows a second failure. It displays the highest price seen in a day. A data vendor then sends a correction for a trade from ten minutes ago, and the old price must stop counting. The screen still shows the old price as the maximum, because the code only knows how to add values.

The Question This Lesson Answers

Both programs answer a question about the order of values, such as the middle value, the largest value or an average of the values between two ranks. Both receive a stream, so values arrive one at a time and the answer is needed after every arrival. This lesson asks which small amount of ordering a program must keep so that arrivals, corrections and expirations each cost far less than sorting everything again.

Three kinds of stream appear below, and each one fixes the data structure before any code is written. An append-only stream only adds values. A correctable stream also replaces the value stored for a key, such as a timestamp. An expiring stream drops the oldest values when a window moves. The median question is append-only, the price question is correctable, and the last exercise is expiring.

Simple approach

Sorting On Every Query

The direct design stores every value in an ArrayList and sorts a copy when the caller asks for the middle value. The class below returns the lower middle value of the readings so far, which is the value at sorted position (n - 1) / 2 counted from 0.

final class NaiveMedian {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> readings = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    void add(long reading) { readings.add(reading); }

    long lowerMedian() {
        java.util.ArrayList<Long> copy = new java.util.ArrayList<>(readings);   // the original order stays intact
        java.util.Collections.sort(copy);                                       // full sort on every query
        return copy.get((copy.size() - 1) / 2);
    }
}

After the readings 12, 4 and 9 the call lowerMedian() returns 9, and after 20 it still returns 9. The answers are correct and the class looks finished.

Predict first: A program adds 100000 readings and calls lowerMedian() after each addition. Roughly how many comparisons does the sorting alone perform, and does the work per call stay the same as the list grows?

The call after the n-th reading sorts n values, which takes about n log n comparisons. The sum over all 100000 calls is on the order of 100000 * 100000 * 17 / 2, about 8.5 * 10^10 comparisons. The work per call grows with the list, so the last calls cost about 100000 times more than the first calls, although each call changed the data by one value.

Bottleneck

Repeated Sorting Of Unchanged Data

The Cost Of One Query

The call after the n-th reading sorts n values, so one query costs O(n log n) time and O(n) extra memory for the copy. A stream of q readings with a query after each one costs O(q^2 log q) in total. Sorting the stored list once and inserting each new value into its place does not fix the cost, because shifting list entries costs O(n) per insertion.

Why Most Of The Work Repeats

Almost all of that work repeats. Before the new reading arrives, the list is already ordered. The new reading changes the position of at most one value, and the query needs one value, the one in the middle. The program orders the values far below the middle and far above it, and no query ever looks at their order.

What Corrections Add

Corrections make the waste worse. A sorted array cannot drop one value from the middle without shifting the values after it. A PriorityQueue offers remove(Object), which scans the queue and costs O(n) per call. A correct design therefore needs three properties. It must find the middle value in O(1) or O(log n) time. It must absorb a new value in O(log n) time. It must make a corrected value stop counting without an O(n) scan.

Key idea

Two Halves Meet At The Middle

Keeping Only The Middle Ordered

The middle value needs only a dividing line between the smaller values and the larger values. Inside each side, the program needs only that side's boundary value. The lower half is a max-heap that holds the smaller values, so its root is the largest of them. The upper half is a min-heap that holds the larger values, so its root is the smallest of them. The two roots are the two values next to the dividing line.

The structure keeps three facts true after every operation, and together they form the invariant. Every value that counts belongs to exactly one half. Every value in the lower half is at most every value in the upper half. The size relation holds: the lower half has the same number of values as the upper half, or exactly one more. With that relation, the lower root is the lower middle value, and for an even count the average of the two roots is the usual median.

Inserting With One Move

A new value x goes into the lower half when x is at most the lower root, and into the upper half otherwise. That choice keeps the order between the halves. The insertion may break the size relation by one value. A single move repairs it: the root of the bigger half leaves that heap and enters the other heap. The order between the halves survives the move, because the moved root was the boundary of its side. One insertion costs a constant number of heap operations, so it costs O(log n).

Correcting Without Searching

A correction replaces the value stored under a key, such as a timestamp. A heap cannot find an arbitrary entry quickly. The program leaves the old entry in the heap and records the truth in a map from key to current value. An entry whose value no longer matches the map is a stale entry. The program checks the root against the map only when it reads the root. If the root is a stale entry, the program removes it and looks at the next root. Each entry enters a heap once and leaves it once, so the removals cost O(log n) amortized per operation.

Entries with equal values are different entries when they belong to different keys. A check by value alone cannot tell them apart, so each entry also carries its key, or a version number that the map increases with each correction. When the stream also expires its oldest values, the same idea fits into three ordered regions, and the last exercise builds them.

State

State In Both Classes

The lesson uses two small classes, and the trace follows the first fields of each.

  • lower is the max-heap of the smaller values, and its root is the lower middle value.
  • upper is the min-heap of the larger values, and its size is never larger than the size of lower.
  • version maps a sensor name to the number of readings recorded for it, and the newest reading carries that number.
  • heap holds one entry for every reading not yet polled, each with its value, sensor name and version.

The size relation is lower.size() == upper.size() or lower.size() == upper.size() + 1. Every value in lower is at most every value in upper. An entry in heap counts only when its version equals version.get(sensor).

Walkthrough

Tracing Both Structures

The Two Halves Over Six Readings

The first trace adds the readings 12, 4, 9, 20, 1 and 7 and shows both halves after each reading. The pointer i marks the reading just added. The reading 12 enters the empty lower half. The reading 4 is at most the lower root 12, so it also enters the lower half. The lower half then has two values and the upper half none, so the root 12 moves to the upper half. The reading 9 is above the lower root 4, so it enters the upper half. The upper half is now larger, so its root 9 moves to the lower half. The reading 20 enters the upper half and the sizes are equal. The reading 1 enters the lower half, which now holds one value more, and the size relation allows that. The reading 7 is at most the lower root 9, so it enters the lower half. That half is too large, so its root 9 moves to the upper half. The variable median holds the lower middle value after each reading.

The Heap With Stale Entries

The second trace records sensor readings A=7, B=9, A=12, B=4 and A=3 and asks for the peak after each one. The pointer i marks the reading just recorded. The heap keeps every reading, and the map version says which entry is current for each sensor. The peak query removes stale roots until the root is current. The variable heap counts entries, stale ones included, and popped counts the stale roots that the latest query removed. The first four queries find a current root, so the heap only grows. The fifth recording makes A the sensor with reading 3, and the entries 12, 9 and 7 are now stale, because sensor B and sensor A both have newer versions. The query removes those three roots one after another and stops at the entry 4, which is current. The entries that remain are the two current readings.

Code

Two Classes That Avoid Resorting

Lower Middle Of A Reading Stream

The class keeps two heaps and repairs the size relation after every insertion. Readings are long values, so a latency in nanoseconds does not overflow.

final class LatencyMedian {
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Long> lower =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>(java.util.Comparator.reverseOrder());   // root is the largest of the smaller values
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Long> upper = new java.util.PriorityQueue<>();   // root is the smallest of the larger values

    void add(long reading) {
        if (lower.isEmpty() || reading <= lower.peek()) lower.add(reading);   // keeps every lower value at most every upper value
        else upper.add(reading);
        if (lower.size() > upper.size() + 1) upper.add(lower.poll());         // lower half too large, move its root across
        else if (upper.size() > lower.size()) lower.add(upper.poll());        // upper half too large, move its root across
    }

    long lowerMedian() {
        if (lower.isEmpty()) throw new java.util.NoSuchElementException();    // no reading yet
        return lower.peek();                                                  // the lower root is the lower middle value
    }
}

Largest Current Reading Per Sensor

The class replaces the reading of a sensor without searching the heap. Each entry stores the version of its sensor at the time it was recorded, and peak discards entries whose version is no longer current.

final class PeakTracker {
    private record Entry(long reading, String sensor, int version) {}

    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> version = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Entry> heap =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(b.reading(), a.reading()));   // largest reading at the root

    void record(String sensor, long reading) {
        int v = version.merge(sensor, 1, Integer::sum);   // each recording makes the previous entry of this sensor stale
        heap.add(new Entry(reading, sensor, v));          // the heap is never searched, so this costs O(log n)
    }

    long peak() {
        while (!heap.isEmpty() && version.get(heap.peek().sensor()) != heap.peek().version())
            heap.poll();                                  // a stale root leaves the heap for good
        if (heap.isEmpty()) throw new java.util.NoSuchElementException();
        return heap.peek().reading();                     // the root is current, so it is the maximum of the current readings
    }
}

Counts In An Ordered Map

The Stock Price Corrections exercise needs the smallest and largest current price, with no stale entries left behind. A TreeMap from price to the number of timestamps that hold that price gives this. The sketch below shows the ordered counts alone.

final class PriceCounts {
    private final java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new java.util.TreeMap<>();   // price mapped to its number of timestamps

    void add(int price) { counts.merge(price, 1, Integer::sum); }

    void remove(int price) {
        if (counts.merge(price, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) counts.remove(price);   // the last timestamp with this price leaves the map
    }

    int smallest() { return counts.firstKey(); }   // O(log n) for the smallest current price
    int largest() { return counts.lastKey(); }     // O(log n) for the largest current price
}

A correction calls remove with the old price and add with the new price. A price shared by two timestamps stays in the map until both timestamps change.

Three Regions For The Last m Values

The Trimmed Mean exercise builds on the same idea, but a window adds one more step. Split the last m values into three regions by sorted rank. The low region holds the k smallest values, the high region holds the k largest values, and the middle region holds the other m - 2k values.

Each region is an ordered multiset like PriceCounts. A long variable holds the sum of the middle region, so the average costs one division. A queue holds the values in arrival order, because the oldest value must leave by value.

A new value enters the region that matches its rank. Then one value crosses each boundary that became too large. For example, the largest value of the low region moves into the middle region, and its amount moves into the sum. When the window already holds m values, the queue gives the oldest value, which leaves its region first. A move across one boundary repairs the region sizes again.

Cost

  • LatencyMedian costs O(log n) time per add and O(1) time per lowerMedian, with O(n) space for the stored readings.
  • PeakTracker costs O(log n) time per call, amortized, because every entry is polled at most once, with O(n + s) space for n recordings and s sensors.
When to use it

When To Keep Two Halves

Recognizing The Pattern

Use the method when a stream must answer the middle value, a rank or an aggregate between two ranks after every change, and the rank depends on the current count. The cue is a question about order whose answer moves slowly while the data changes. Before coding, name the kind of stream. Append-only streams need only the two heaps. Correctable streams add a map of current values and validation of every root. Expiring streams need a structure that removes the oldest value by value, such as an ordered multiset, because a heap cannot remove it quickly. The invariant to keep is that every counted value sits in exactly one region, and the regions respect their order and size relation.

A False Friend

A bounded heap that keeps the k largest values is a false friend. It answers the k-th largest value for a fixed k and discards the rest. The median has a rank that grows with the count, and a bounded heap cannot hold the values on the other side of the dividing line. A second false friend is PriorityQueue.remove(Object) for corrections. It gives right answers and costs O(n) per call, so the speed advantage of the heap disappears.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If the caller asks for arbitrary ranks, such as the 10th and the 90th percentile together, two halves with one dividing line are not enough. A balanced tree with subtree sizes, or a tree of counts over the sorted values, answers any rank in O(log n). The counts tree is covered in chapter 31. If every value is a small integer, a counting array answers any rank with a scan of the array. This lesson also assumes one caller, so it does not cover concurrent updates.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Running Median

LeetCode 295

Problem Specification. Design the class MedianFinder. The method addNum(num) appends the integer num to the stream. The method findMedian() returns the median of all integers added so far as a double. For an odd count the median is the middle value in sorted order. For an even count it is the average of the two middle values.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values from -10^5 to 10^5, and duplicates occur.
  • Calls number at most 5 * 10^4 per object.
  • Queries happen only after at least one addNum call.
  • Return type is double, and the sum of the two middle values must not overflow.

Example 1. Input calls ["MedianFinder","addNum","addNum","findMedian","addNum","findMedian"] with arguments [[],[4],[10],[],[2],[]], output [null,null,null,7.0,null,4.0].

Example 2. Input calls ["MedianFinder","addNum","findMedian","addNum","addNum","findMedian","addNum","findMedian"] with arguments [[],[-3],[],[-3],[8],[],[9],[]], output [null,null,-3.0,null,null,-3.0,null,2.5].

Prerequisites. The two halves, the size relation and the one-move repair from this lesson. The stream is append-only.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the stream only grows, so two heaps with the size relation are enough and no value ever leaves.

Hint

After each insertion, which heap is allowed to be larger, and which root moves when the sizes differ by two?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps a max-heap lower and a min-heap upper. Every added integer enters lower when it is at most the lower root and enters upper otherwise, so each value in lower stays at most each value in upper. A single move of a root repairs the size relation, which allows lower to hold the same number of values as upper or one more. For an odd count the median is the lower root. For an even count it is the average of the two roots, and the sum is formed as a long before the division by 2.0.

The invariant is that the two heaps hold every added value once, the order between them holds and the size relation holds. The program compares the class with a sorted copy of the list on random streams, including duplicates, negative values and a single element.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per addNum, because an insertion and at most one root move touch two heaps, and O(1) per findMedian, which reads one or two roots.
  • Space is O(n), because every added value stays in one of the two heaps.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RunningMedianBuild {
    static final class MedianFinder {
        private final PriorityQueue<Integer> lower = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
        private final PriorityQueue<Integer> upper = new PriorityQueue<>();

        /**
         * Appends one value to the stream.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: the halves stay ordered and lower holds the same count as upper or one more.
         */
        void addNum(int num) {
            // Values at most the lower root go left, so no lower value exceeds an upper value.
            if (lower.isEmpty() || num <= lower.peek()) lower.add(num);
            else upper.add(num);
            // One extra move restores the size relation after a single insertion.
            if (lower.size() > upper.size() + 1) upper.add(lower.poll());
            else if (upper.size() > lower.size()) lower.add(upper.poll());
        }

        /**
         * Returns the median of the values added so far.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the roots are the values next to the dividing line.
         */
        double findMedian() {
            // An odd count leaves the extra value in lower, so its root is the median.
            if (lower.size() > upper.size()) return lower.peek();
            // An even count averages the two roots, and long arithmetic avoids overflow.
            return ((long) lower.peek() + upper.peek()) / 2.0;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the medians 7.0 and 4.0.
        MedianFinder a = new MedianFinder();
        a.addNum(4); a.addNum(10);
        if (a.findMedian() != 7.0) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first");
        a.addNum(2);
        if (a.findMedian() != 4.0) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second");
        // Example 2: duplicates and an even count with a fractional median.
        MedianFinder b = new MedianFinder();
        b.addNum(-3);
        if (b.findMedian() != -3.0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 single");
        b.addNum(-3); b.addNum(8);
        if (b.findMedian() != -3.0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 duplicate");
        b.addNum(9);
        if (b.findMedian() != 2.5) throw new AssertionError("example 2 even");
        // Extreme values must not overflow when two middle values are added.
        MedianFinder c = new MedianFinder();
        c.addNum(Integer.MAX_VALUE); c.addNum(Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        if (c.findMedian() != (double) Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("overflow");
        // Random streams compare every median with a sorted copy.
        Random rnd = new Random(3801L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            MedianFinder real = new MedianFinder();
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(30);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(21) - 10;
                real.addNum(v);
                model.add(v);
                List<Integer> sorted = new ArrayList<>(model);
                Collections.sort(sorted);
                int sz = sorted.size();
                double want = sz % 2 == 1 ? sorted.get(sz / 2) : ((long) sorted.get(sz / 2 - 1) + sorted.get(sz / 2)) / 2.0;
                if (real.findMedian() != want) throw new AssertionError("median at " + sz);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Stock Price Corrections

LeetCode 2034

Problem Specification. Design the class StockPrice. The method update(timestamp, price) records the price at the timestamp. When the timestamp already has a price, the new price replaces the old price. The method current() returns the price at the largest timestamp recorded. The methods maximum() and minimum() return the largest and smallest of the prices that currently count, one price for each recorded timestamp.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Timestamps are int values from 1 to 10^9, and updates arrive in any order.
  • Prices are int values from 1 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Queries happen only after at least one update call.

Example 1. Input calls ["StockPrice","update","update","current","minimum","update","maximum","minimum","current"] with arguments [[],[3,20],[5,8],[],[],[3,2],[],[],[]], output [null,null,null,8,8,null,8,2,8].

Example 2. Input calls ["StockPrice","update","update","maximum","update","maximum","current","update","maximum","minimum"] with arguments [[],[10,6],[10,6],[],[4,9],[],[],[4,1],[],[]], output [null,null,null,6,null,9,6,null,6,1].

Prerequisites. The Running Median exercise above. This exercise changes the stream from append-only to correctable, and it keeps the extreme values queryable.

What This Exercise Changes. A correction now removes one occurrence of the old price from an ordered multiset of counts, so a shared price stays until both timestamps change.

Hint

When a correction removes the old price of a timestamp, how does the ordered collection know that other timestamps may hold the same price?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps three pieces of state. The map price stores the current price for each timestamp. The integer latest is the largest timestamp recorded. The TreeMap named counts is an ordered multiset: it maps each price that currently counts to the number of timestamps that hold it. An update to a new timestamp inserts one occurrence of the price. An update to a known timestamp removes one occurrence of the old price, deleting the key when its count reaches zero, and then inserts one occurrence of the new price.

The count is necessary because two timestamps can hold the same price. Removing the key outright would drop the other timestamp's price from the maximum and the minimum. The invariant is that the counts add up to the number of recorded timestamps and every key of counts is a price that some timestamp holds. The program compares the class with a scan over the plain map on random operation sequences with many repeated timestamps and prices.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per update, because the ordered map performs one removal and one insertion. Calls to maximum and minimum cost O(log n) as well, because they read the last and first key.
  • Space is O(n) for n distinct timestamps, because the two maps hold at most one entry per timestamp.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class StockPriceVary {
    static final class StockPrice {
        private final Map<Integer, Integer> price = new HashMap<>();
        private final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new TreeMap<>();
        private int latest;

        /**
         * Records or corrects the price at a timestamp.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: counts holds exactly one occurrence per recorded timestamp.
         */
        void update(int timestamp, int newPrice) {
            // The previous price of this timestamp, or null for a first record.
            Integer old = price.put(timestamp, newPrice);
            if (old != null) {
                // A correction removes one occurrence, so a shared price stays for the other timestamps.
                int left = counts.get(old) - 1;
                if (left == 0) counts.remove(old);
                else counts.put(old, left);
            }
            // The new price gains one occurrence.
            counts.merge(newPrice, 1, Integer::sum);
            // The latest timestamp can only grow, and a correction never lowers it.
            if (timestamp > latest) latest = timestamp;
        }

        /** Price at the largest timestamp. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int current() {
            // A hash lookup of the latest timestamp.
            return price.get(latest);
        }

        /** Largest current price. Time: O(log n). Space: O(1). */
        int maximum() {
            // The last key of the ordered multiset.
            return counts.lastKey();
        }

        /** Smallest current price. Time: O(log n). Space: O(1). */
        int minimum() {
            // The first key of the ordered multiset.
            return counts.firstKey();
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a correction lowers the minimum while the latest price stays.
        StockPrice a = new StockPrice();
        a.update(3, 20); a.update(5, 8);
        if (a.current() != 8 || a.minimum() != 8) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first");
        a.update(3, 2);
        if (a.maximum() != 8 || a.minimum() != 2 || a.current() != 8) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second");
        // Example 2: a repeated update and a shared price.
        StockPrice b = new StockPrice();
        b.update(10, 6); b.update(10, 6);
        if (b.maximum() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 2 repeat");
        b.update(4, 9);
        if (b.maximum() != 9 || b.current() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 2 second");
        b.update(4, 1);
        if (b.maximum() != 6 || b.minimum() != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 third");
        // A price held by two timestamps survives the correction of one of them.
        StockPrice c = new StockPrice();
        c.update(1, 7); c.update(2, 7); c.update(1, 3);
        if (c.maximum() != 7) throw new AssertionError("shared price");
        // Random sequences compare with a scan over the plain map.
        Random rnd = new Random(3802L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            StockPrice real = new StockPrice();
            Map<Integer, Integer> model = new HashMap<>();
            for (int step = 1 + rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                int ts = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6), p = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
                real.update(ts, p);
                model.put(ts, p);
                int hi = Integer.MIN_VALUE, lo = Integer.MAX_VALUE, last = 0;
                for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : model.entrySet()) {
                    hi = Math.max(hi, e.getValue());
                    lo = Math.min(lo, e.getValue());
                    last = Math.max(last, e.getKey());
                }
                if (real.maximum() != hi || real.minimum() != lo || real.current() != model.get(last))
                    throw new AssertionError("mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Duplicate And Stale Heap Roots

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class ScoreBoard. The method set(id, score) gives the item id the score score, and it replaces an earlier score of that item. The method remove(id) deletes the item and does nothing when the item is absent. The method lowestId() returns the id of the item with the smallest score, and the smaller id wins a tie. It returns -1 when no item exists. The class stores items in one binary heap and a map from id to current score, and it never calls PriorityQueue.remove(Object).

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Ids are int values from 0 to 10^9.
  • Scores are int values from -10^9 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5 per object, and set may repeat the same pair.
  • Heap size never exceeds the number of set calls.

Example 1. Input calls ["ScoreBoard","set","set","lowestId","set","lowestId","set","remove","lowestId","remove","lowestId"] with arguments [[],[7,4],[3,4],[],[3,9],[],[3,4],[7],[],[3],[]], output [null,null,null,3,null,7,null,null,3,null,-1].

Example 2. Input calls ["ScoreBoard","lowestId","set","set","remove","set","lowestId","set","lowestId","set","lowestId"] with arguments [[],[],[2,5],[2,5],[2],[2,5],[],[9,5],[],[2,8],[]], output [null,-1,null,null,null,null,2,null,2,null,9].

Prerequisites. The Stock Price Corrections exercise above and the stale entry definition from this lesson. This exercise replaces the ordered multiset with a plain heap, so every read of a root needs a check.

What This Exercise Changes. The check for a root is now "the map holds this id with this exact score", so one test covers removed items, replaced scores and duplicates.

Hint

If an item is removed and later set to the same score, is the old entry in the heap wrong, harmless or harmful?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The board keeps a map scores from id to the current score and a heap of entries ordered by score and then by id. The map is the only source of truth. The method set stores the score in the map and pushes an entry, unless the item already has exactly that score, because the heap then already holds a matching entry. The method remove deletes the id from the map and touches no heap entry.

An entry is valid exactly when the map holds its id with its exact score. The method lowestId removes invalid roots until the root is valid, then returns the id of the root without removing it. A removed item, a replaced score and a duplicate entry all fail or pass this one test correctly. An old entry that becomes valid again after the same pair is set once more is a harmless duplicate, because both entries name the same truth. The invariant is that every current pair has at least one entry in the heap, so the first valid root is the minimum.

The program compares the board with a scan over the map and asserts that the heap never holds more entries than the number of set calls.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) amortized per call, because every entry is pushed once and removed at most once, and each push or removal costs O(log n).
  • Space is O(n) for n set calls, because stale entries stay until a query reaches them.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ScoreBoardBoundary {
    static final class ScoreBoard {
        private record Entry(int score, int id) {}

        private final Map<Integer, Integer> scores = new HashMap<>();
        private final PriorityQueue<Entry> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) ->
            x.score() != y.score() ? Integer.compare(x.score(), y.score()) : Integer.compare(x.id(), y.id()));
        int setCalls;

        /**
         * Gives an item a score, replacing an earlier score.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: every current pair has an entry in the heap.
         */
        void set(int id, int score) {
            setCalls++;
            // The map is the truth, so the replaced score needs no heap search.
            Integer old = scores.put(id, score);
            // An identical pair already has its entry, so pushing again would only add a duplicate.
            if (old == null || old != score) heap.add(new Entry(score, id));
        }

        /**
         * Deletes an item if it exists.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: heap entries of the item become invalid because the map no longer holds the id.
         */
        void remove(int id) {
            // Only the map changes, and the heap entries are discarded when they reach the root.
            scores.remove(id);
        }

        /**
         * Returns the id with the smallest score, ties to the smaller id, or -1 when empty.
         * Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: after the loop the root is valid, so it is the minimum.
         */
        int lowestId() {
            // A root is stale when the map lacks its id or holds another score.
            while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
                Entry top = heap.peek();
                Integer now = scores.get(top.id());
                if (now != null && now == top.score()) break;
                // Each stale entry leaves the heap once, which pays for the check.
                heap.poll();
            }
            // An empty heap means no item exists.
            return heap.isEmpty() ? -1 : heap.peek().id();
        }

        int heapSize() { return heap.size(); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a tie, a replaced score, a removal and an empty board.
        ScoreBoard a = new ScoreBoard();
        a.set(7, 4); a.set(3, 4);
        if (a.lowestId() != 3) throw new AssertionError("tie");
        a.set(3, 9);
        if (a.lowestId() != 7) throw new AssertionError("replaced");
        a.set(3, 4); a.remove(7);
        if (a.lowestId() != 3) throw new AssertionError("after remove");
        a.remove(3);
        if (a.lowestId() != -1) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // Example 2: empty start, repeated pair, remove then set the same pair again.
        ScoreBoard b = new ScoreBoard();
        if (b.lowestId() != -1) throw new AssertionError("empty start");
        b.set(2, 5); b.set(2, 5); b.remove(2); b.set(2, 5);
        if (b.lowestId() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 2 first");
        b.set(9, 5);
        if (b.lowestId() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 2 tie");
        b.set(2, 8);
        if (b.lowestId() != 9) throw new AssertionError("example 2 last");
        // Removing an absent id changes nothing.
        b.remove(12345);
        if (b.lowestId() != 9) throw new AssertionError("absent remove");
        // Random sequences compare with a scan and check the heap bound.
        Random rnd = new Random(3803L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            ScoreBoard real = new ScoreBoard();
            Map<Integer, Integer> model = new HashMap<>();
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(30); step > 0; step--) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3), id = rnd.nextInt(5), sc = rnd.nextInt(4) - 1;
                if (op == 0) { real.set(id, sc); model.put(id, sc); }
                else if (op == 1) { real.remove(id); model.remove(id); }
                int want = -1, best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
                for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : model.entrySet()) {
                    if (e.getValue() < best || (e.getValue() == best && e.getKey() < want)) { best = e.getValue(); want = e.getKey(); }
                }
                if (real.lowestId() != want) throw new AssertionError("lowestId");
                if (real.heapSize() > real.setCalls) throw new AssertionError("heap bound");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Trimmed Mean Of Recent Values

LeetCode 1825

Problem Specification. Design the class MKAverage with a constructor that receives integers m and k. The method addElement(num) appends num to the stream. The method calculateMKAverage() looks at the last m values. When fewer than m values exist, it returns -1. Otherwise it removes the k smallest and the k largest of those m values, with duplicates counted separately, and returns the average of the remaining values rounded down.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Parameters satisfy 3 <= m <= 10^5 and 1 <= k and 2k < m.
  • Values are int values from 0 to 10^5.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Sums reach 10^10, so the sum needs long.

Example 1. Input calls ["MKAverage","addElement","addElement","addElement","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage"] with arguments [[5,1],[6],[6],[1],[6],[],[6],[],[50],[],[0],[],[6],[]], output [null,null,null,null,null,-1,null,6,null,6,null,4,null,6].

Example 2.
