Input calls ["MKAverage","addElement","addElement","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage","addElement","calculateMKAverage"] with arguments [[3,1],[10],[0],[10],[],[0],[],[0],[],[0],[]], output [null,null,null,null,10,null,0,null,0,null,0].

Prerequisites. The Stock Price Corrections exercise above. This exercise adds expiry: the stream is expiring, because only the last m values count.

What This Exercise Changes. Three regions, a queue and a running sum of the middle region replace the two heaps. The oldest value leaves a region by value, and the region sizes return to k, m - 2k and k.

Hint

When the oldest value leaves the window, which of the three regions contains it, and which region boundary must be repaired afterward?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class holds the last m values in a queue and also in three ordered multisets named low, mid and high. Each multiset is a TreeMap from value to count with a size and a sum. Every stored value belongs to exactly one region. Every value in low is at most every value in mid, and every value in mid is at most every value in high. When the window holds m values, low and high hold k values each and mid holds the remaining m - 2k values.

An addition places the new value by comparing it with the top of low and the bottom of high. When the queue then exceeds m values, the oldest value leaves whichever region holds a copy of it. Equal values in different regions are interchangeable, so removing any copy keeps the order between regions. A repair step then moves boundary values: it first shrinks a region that holds more than k values, and then refills low and high from the nearest end of mid. The answer is the sum of mid, held as a long, divided by m - 2k with integer division, which rounds down because all values are non-negative.

The invariant after each addition is the order between regions and the sizes k, m - 2k, k once m values exist. Each addition causes a constant number of tree operations. The program compares the class with sorting the last m values on random streams with many duplicates.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log m) per addElement, because it performs a constant number of insertions, removals and boundary moves in ordered maps. Each calculateMKAverage costs O(1), because it divides two stored numbers.
  • Space is O(m), because the queue and the three regions together hold the last m values.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class TrimmedMeanRecognize {
    /** Ordered multiset of ints that tracks its size and its sum. */
    static final class Region {
        final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> map = new TreeMap<>();
        int size;
        long sum;

        void add(int v) { map.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum); size++; sum += v; }

        boolean remove(int v) {
            // Absent values report false so the caller can try another region.
            Integer c = map.get(v);
            if (c == null) return false;
            if (c == 1) map.remove(v); else map.put(v, c - 1);
            size--; sum -= v;
            return true;
        }

        int min() { return map.firstKey(); }
        int max() { return map.lastKey(); }
    }

    static final class MKAverage {
        private final int m, k;
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> window = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private final Region low = new Region(), mid = new Region(), high = new Region();

        MKAverage(int m, int k) { this.m = m; this.k = k; }

        /**
         * Appends a value and expires the oldest one when more than m values exist.
         * Time: O(log m). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: regions are ordered, and sizes are k, m - 2k, k once the window is full.
         */
        void addElement(int num) {
            window.addLast(num);
            // Place the value by the region boundaries, which keeps the order between regions.
            if (low.size > 0 && num <= low.max()) low.add(num);
            else if (high.size > 0 && num >= high.min()) high.add(num);
            else mid.add(num);
            if (window.size() > m) {
                // The oldest value leaves one region that holds a copy of it.
                int old = window.pollFirst();
                if (!low.remove(old) && !mid.remove(old)) high.remove(old);
            }
            // Shrink oversized outer regions first, so mid never lacks a value to lend.
            while (low.size > k) { int v = low.max(); low.remove(v); mid.add(v); }
            while (high.size > k) { int v = high.min(); high.remove(v); mid.add(v); }
            // Refill the outer regions from the nearest end of mid.
            while (low.size < k && mid.size > 0) { int v = mid.min(); mid.remove(v); low.add(v); }
            while (high.size < k && mid.size > 0) { int v = mid.max(); mid.remove(v); high.add(v); }
        }

        /**
         * Returns the rounded-down mean of the middle region, or -1 before m values exist.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: mid.sum is the sum of the m - 2k values between the trimmed ends.
         */
        int calculateMKAverage() {
            // Fewer than m values means the window is not full.
            if (window.size() < m) return -1;
            // A long sum divided by the middle size, rounded down for non-negative values.
            return (int) (mid.sum / (m - 2 * k));
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: m = 5, k = 1.
        MKAverage a = new MKAverage(5, 1);
        int[] in1 = {6, 6, 1, 6};
        for (int v : in1) a.addElement(v);
        if (a.calculateMKAverage() != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 1 before full");
        a.addElement(6);
        if (a.calculateMKAverage() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first");
        a.addElement(50);
        if (a.calculateMKAverage() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second");
        a.addElement(0);
        if (a.calculateMKAverage() != 4) throw new AssertionError("example 1 third");
        a.addElement(6);
        if (a.calculateMKAverage() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 fourth");
        // Example 2: m = 3, k = 1, zeros and duplicates.
        MKAverage b = new MKAverage(3, 1);
        b.addElement(10); b.addElement(0); b.addElement(10);
        if (b.calculateMKAverage() != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 2 first");
        b.addElement(0);
        if (b.calculateMKAverage() != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2 second");
        // A full window of 10^5 values of 10^5 sums to 10^10, which exceeds the int range.
        MKAverage big = new MKAverage(100000, 1);
        for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) big.addElement(100000);
        if (big.calculateMKAverage() != 100000) throw new AssertionError("wide window");
        // Random streams compare with sorting the last m values.
        Random rnd = new Random(3804L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 1500; t++) {
            int m = 3 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt((m - 1) / 2);
            MKAverage real = new MKAverage(m, k);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 1 + rnd.nextInt(40); step > 0; step--) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(7);
                real.addElement(v);
                all.add(v);
                int want = -1;
                if (all.size() >= m) {
                    List<Integer> w = new ArrayList<>(all.subList(all.size() - m, all.size()));
                    java.util.Collections.sort(w);
                    long s = 0;
                    for (int i = k; i < m - k; i++) s += w.get(i);
                    want = (int) (s / (m - 2 * k));
                }
                if (real.calculateMKAverage() != want) throw new AssertionError("mk average");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Frequency Streams

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Dashboard Lags Behind Its Data

A support team watches a dashboard that shows the first error code that has appeared exactly once since the last deploy. Each incoming log line carries one code. The dashboard service stores every code in a list and rescans the whole list whenever someone opens the page. During a quiet hour this is instant. During an outage the list grows by thousands of lines per second, and the page that should help the team takes longer to load than the outage takes to change. The numbers on screen describe the system as it was a minute ago.

A second service has the opposite flaw. It tracks how many users have performed exactly three failed logins, so it can flag them. It keeps a map from each user to a login count and a separate figure for how many users have three. When a user's record is deleted, the map shrinks but the separate figure does not, and the page flags a user who no longer exists. Both services answer questions about multiplicity. How many times has a value appeared? Is any value present exactly f times? Which value is still unique? Were the last k inputs all the same? This lesson asks how to answer such questions after every update without rereading the stream and without letting two pieces of bookkeeping disagree.

The stream matters before the structure does. A stream is append-only when values only arrive, correctable when earlier arrivals can be withdrawn, and expiring when values leave on their own after a time. The first two problems in this lesson are append-only. The tracker in the third is correctable. No problem here expires, because expiry is the topic of other lessons in this chapter.

Simple approach

Keep Every Value And Recount

The direct design stores each arriving value in a list. A query rebuilds the multiplicities from scratch with a hash map and then answers from the result. The class below supports add, a check whether any value occurs exactly f times, and a lookup of the first value that occurs once.

final class RecountingLog {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Integer> seen = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    void add(int v) { seen.add(v); }

    boolean hasFrequency(int f) {
        java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new java.util.HashMap<>();
        for (int v : seen) counts.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);     // recounts the whole history
        for (int c : counts.values()) if (c == f) return true;   // scans every distinct value
        return false;
    }

    int firstOnce() {
        java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new java.util.HashMap<>();
        for (int v : seen) counts.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);     // recounts again
        for (int v : seen) if (counts.get(v) == 1) return v;     // walks in arrival order
        return -1;                                               // nothing is unique
    }
}

After the values 4, 7, 4, the call hasFrequency(2) returns true and firstOnce() returns 7. Both answers are correct, and for a short stream the class is fast enough.

Predict first: A monitor adds 100000 values to this log and calls hasFrequency after every single add. Roughly how many hash-map insertions happen in total, and what changes if the caller asks only once at the end?

The call after the k-th add rebuilds a map from k stored values, so the total is 1 + 2 + ... + 100000, which is about 5 * 10^9 insertions. The program spends almost all of its time repeating work it already did on the previous call. If the caller asks once at the end, the cost is only 100000 insertions and the design is fine. The cost depends on how often the question is asked, and an online interface asks after every update.

Bottleneck

Every Query Repeats The Whole Count

Each query of the log costs O(n) time for a history of n values, and it also keeps O(n) memory for the list. A stream of n updates with a query after each one therefore costs O(n^2) time. For n near 10^5 this is about 5 * 10^9 hash-map operations, which is far beyond a time limit. The memory grows without bound too, since the list keeps every value even after the answer no longer depends on its position.

The repeated work is visible in the code. The multiplicity of one value changes by exactly 1 when one update arrives, yet the query rebuilds the multiplicity of every value. The set of answers changes by at most two entries per update: one value leaves its old frequency and enters a new one. A design that stores the counts and moves only those two entries answers each update in constant time.

A second cost hides in the first-unique query. The scan walks the list from the front each time, so a front that has stayed non-unique for a long time is rechecked on every call. A correct design needs three properties. Each update touches a bounded number of entries. Every stored summary agrees with the counts after every update, including the update that brings a count to zero. Anything that the design keeps without a guarantee is checked before it is shown to the caller.

Key idea

Counts Plus A Second Map

One Map Is The Source Of Truth

A map from value to its current count is the only place that stores how often a value occurs. Every other structure either summarizes it or is checked against it. When the map says a value has count 0, the entry is removed, so the key set of the map equals the set of values that are present.

A Tally Summarizes The Counts

Questions of the form "is any value present exactly f times" ask about counts and not about values. A tally is a second map from a count c to the number of values whose count is c. When a value moves from count c to count c + 1, the update subtracts 1 from tally[c] and adds 1 to tally[c + 1]. The query then reads tally[f] > 0 in constant time. The two maps agree when every update changes both, and the zero count is the case that breaks agreement. A value whose count is 0 is not present, so no tally[0] entry exists. The update skips the increment when the new count is 0 and skips the decrement when the old count is 0.

A Queue May Hold Stale Entries

For first-unique queries the design keeps a queue of values in arrival order, a candidate being any value in the queue that could be the answer. A value enters the queue once, when its count first becomes 1. Later arrivals of the same value raise its count and leave the queue alone, so the queue now holds a stale entry, a value whose count is no longer 1. Removing that entry from the middle of the queue would cost O(n). The design leaves it there. Before the query exposes the front, it compares the front's count with 1 and pops while the front is stale. Each entry is popped at most once, so the pops are paid for by the pushes.

A Streak Replaces The Stream

Some questions need no history. "Are the last k values all equal to a target" depends only on the streak, the number of consecutive target values at the end of the stream. A matching value adds 1 to the streak and any other value sets it to 0. One integer replaces a window of k values.

The invariant of this lesson is that a container may hold stale values only when a count check filters them before the caller sees them. The maps never hold stale data, the queue may, and the streak is always exact.

State

State Kept By Each Structure

The lesson builds three classes: FrequencyBook for counts, UniqueReader for first-unique queries and RunCounter for runs of one value. The traces follow FrequencyBook and UniqueReader, and RunCounter keeps one integer.

  • counts maps a value to its current number of occurrences, and it holds no entry with count 0.
  • tally maps a count to the number of values that currently have it, and it holds no entry for count 0.
  • order is the queue of values in the order their count first reached 1.
  • target and k are the fixed value and the length that the run recognizer compares against.
  • streak is the number of consecutive target values at the end of the input, and it is a long because the stream can be long.

The invariant is that tally[c] equals the number of values with counts[v] == c for every c >= 1, and every value whose count is 1 appears in order.

Walkthrough

Tracing Updates And Queries

The Tracker With Corrections

The first trace applies nine operations to the counts and the tally. The pointer op marks the operation just applied. After add 4, add 7 and add 4, the map holds 4:2 and 7:1, so the tally holds 1:1 and 2:1. The query has 2 reads tally[2], finds 1 and answers true. The operation removeOne 4 lowers the count of 4 from 2 to 1, so the tally moves one entry from 2 to 1 and tally[2] disappears. The next has 2 answers false. The operation removeOne 7 takes the count of 7 from 1 to 0, so the map removes the key. The tally lowers tally[1] from 2 to 1 and gains no entry for 0. The second removeOne 7 finds no key and changes nothing. The final has 1 answers true because the value 4 still has count 1.

First Unique With A Lazy Queue

The second trace feeds values to the queue order. The pointer op marks the operation just applied. Adding 5 and 3 pushes both. The second add 5 raises the count of 5 to 2 and pushes nothing, so the front of the queue, 5, is now stale. The query first sees count 2 at the front, pops 5, then sees count 1 at 3 and returns it. After add 2 and add 3, the front 3 is stale again, and the next first pops it and returns 2. When add 2 follows, the query pops 2 and finds an empty queue, so the answer is -1. Every pop happens inside a query, and no entry is ever searched for in the middle of the queue.

Code

Three Small Classes With Defined Updates

The Tracker With Counts And Tally

The shift method is the only place that changes a count, so the two maps cannot disagree. It receives the old and new count of one value.

final class FrequencyBook {
    private final java.util.HashMap<Long, Integer> counts = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> tally = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    private void shift(long v, int from, int to) {
        if (from > 0 && tally.merge(from, -1, Integer::sum) == 0)
            tally.remove(from);                                // the value leaves its old count, an empty bucket is dropped
        if (to > 0) tally.merge(to, 1, Integer::sum);          // the value joins its new count
        if (to == 0) counts.remove(v); else counts.put(v, to); // zero means absent
    }

    void add(long v) { int c = counts.getOrDefault(v, 0); shift(v, c, c + 1); }

    void removeOne(long v) { int c = counts.getOrDefault(v, 0); if (c > 0) shift(v, c, c - 1); }

    boolean hasFrequency(int f) { return tally.getOrDefault(f, 0) > 0; }   // constant-time read
}

The First Unique Reader

The reader pushes a value only when its count first becomes 1, and the query removes stale entries from the front.

final class UniqueReader {
    private final java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> order = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();

    void add(int v) {
        int c = counts.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);   // authoritative count
        if (c == 1) order.addLast(v);               // enters the queue once
    }

    int first() {
        while (!order.isEmpty() && counts.get(order.peekFirst()) != 1)
            order.pollFirst();                      // each stale front is popped once
        return order.isEmpty() ? -1 : order.peekFirst();
    }
}

The Suffix Run Counter

The counter stores no values, only the length of the current matching suffix.

final class RunCounter {
    private final int target;
    private final long k;
    private long streak;

    RunCounter(int target, long k) { this.target = target; this.k = k; }

    boolean push(int x) {
        streak = (x == target) ? streak + 1 : 0;   // one comparison decides the whole state
        return streak >= k;                        // true when the last k are all target
    }
}

Cost

  • FrequencyBook costs O(1) expected time per call and O(d) space for d distinct values.
  • UniqueReader costs O(1) amortized time per call, because each value is pushed once and popped at most once, and O(d) space.
  • RunCounter costs O(1) time per call and O(1) space.
When to use it

When To Track Counts Online

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when each update changes the multiplicity of one value and the queries ask about multiplicities, uniqueness or a run of equal values. Before choosing structures, write whether the stream is append-only, correctable or expiring. A correctable stream needs the tally to move in both directions. An append-only stream may skip the downward move. Name the summary of every count you keep, and decide what each update does at count 0. The invariant to keep is that a stale container entry is rejected by a count check before the caller sees it.

A False Friend

A frequency map alone is a false friend of "is any value present exactly f times". It answers how often one value occurs but forces a scan of all values to answer the question about f. A second false friend is a sorted structure of counts, which looks natural for ranking questions and costs O(log d) per update when a constant-time map suffices. A third is a queue that removes a duplicate from the middle on every arrival, which turns an amortized O(1) design into O(n).

When The Method Does Not Apply

If values expire after a time, a count that only rises or falls by explicit updates is wrong, because counts must also fall when time passes. Use the time-based methods of the neighboring lessons. If the query asks for the k-th most frequent value or for a rank, a tally of counts is not enough and an ordered structure is required. If the queue of candidates would need removal from the middle for correctness and not only for speed, the lazy check is also not enough.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Pair Sum Lookup

LeetCode 170

Problem Specification. Design the class PairSumStore. The method add(x) appends the integer x to a multiset. The method find(target) returns true when two different stored elements, counted by position of arrival, have the sum target. An element may not pair with itself, but two equal values stored at different positions may pair.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values from -10^9 to 10^9.
  • Target is a long value from -4 * 10^9 to 4 * 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object, and find may be called on an empty store.
  • Stream is append-only, so no call removes a stored value.

Example 1. Input calls ["PairSumStore","add","add","add","find","find","find","find"] with arguments [[],[3],[7],[3],[6],[10],[14],[9]], output [null,null,null,null,true,true,false,false].

Example 2. Input calls ["PairSumStore","add","find","add","find","add","find"] with arguments [[],[-5],[-10],[-5],[-10],[1000000000],[999999995]], output [null,null,false,null,true,null,true].

Prerequisites. The count map and the append-only definition from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the map stores counts and not positions, and the target is a long so that the partner target - x never overflows.

Hint

When the partner of x equals x itself, which extra fact about the count of x must hold?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The store keeps a hash map from each value to the number of times it was added. A query walks the distinct values. For each value x it computes the partner target - x in long arithmetic, so the subtraction cannot overflow int. If the partner differs from x, the query needs the partner to be in the map. If the partner equals x, the query needs a count of at least 2, because one stored element cannot pair with itself. The invariant is that counts equals the multiset of added values, and add is the only writer.

The program compares the store with a double loop over a list of all added values. Random streams use small values, so equal partners occur often. Separate checks cover targets that need long arithmetic.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected for add and O(d) for find, where d is the number of distinct values, because find visits each key once with a constant-time lookup.
  • Space is O(d), because the map stores one entry per distinct value.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class PairSumStoreBuild {
    static final class PairSumStore {
        private final HashMap<Long, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Appends one value to the multiset.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: counts holds the multiplicity of every added value.
         */
        void add(int x) {
            // merge raises the stored multiplicity by one in a single hash operation.
            counts.merge((long) x, 1, Integer::sum);
        }

        /**
         * Tells whether two different stored positions sum to target.
         * Time: O(d) expected for d distinct values. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a value pairs with itself only when its count is at least 2.
         */
        boolean find(long target) {
            // Each distinct value is tried once as the first element of a pair.
            for (java.util.Map.Entry<Long, Integer> e : counts.entrySet()) {
                // long arithmetic keeps target - x exact for every int x.
                long partner = target - e.getKey();
                if (partner == e.getKey()) {
                    // A value pairs with itself only through two separate copies.
                    if (e.getValue() >= 2) return true;
                } else if (counts.containsKey(partner)) {
                    // A different partner needs only one stored copy.
                    return true;
                }
            }
            // No distinct value found a partner.
            return false;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: equal values pair through two copies, and a single 7 cannot pair with itself.
        PairSumStore s = new PairSumStore();
        s.add(3); s.add(7); s.add(3);
        if (!s.find(6) || !s.find(10) || s.find(14) || s.find(9)) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a long target works, and a single value needs a second copy.
        PairSumStore t = new PairSumStore();
        t.add(-5);
        if (t.find(-10)) throw new AssertionError("one copy cannot pair with itself");
        t.add(-5);
        if (!t.find(-10)) throw new AssertionError("two copies pair");
        t.add(1000000000);
        if (!t.find(999999995L)) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // An empty store has no pair, and a target beyond int range stays exact.
        if (new PairSumStore().find(0)) throw new AssertionError("empty store");
        PairSumStore big = new PairSumStore();
        big.add(1000000000); big.add(1000000000); big.add(-1000000000);
        if (!big.find(2000000000L) || big.find(4000000000L) || !big.find(0)) throw new AssertionError("long targets");
        // Random streams compare every answer with a double loop over positions.
        Random rnd = new Random(3805L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            PairSumStore real = new PairSumStore();
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(9) - 4;
                    real.add(v); all.add(v);
                } else {
                    long target = rnd.nextInt(21) - 10;
                    boolean expect = false;
                    for (int i = 0; i < all.size(); i++)
                        for (int j = i + 1; j < all.size(); j++)
                            if ((long) all.get(i) + all.get(j) == target) expect = true;
                    if (real.find(target) != expect) throw new AssertionError("find " + target + " in " + all);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

First Unique Reader

LeetCode 1429

Problem Specification. Design the class UniqueReader with a constructor that receives an initial int[]. The method append(v) adds v to the end of the stream. The method first() returns the earliest-arriving value that occurs exactly once in the whole stream so far, or -1 when none exists. The method uniqueCount() returns the number of values that currently occur exactly once.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values from 0 to 10^9, so -1 is never a stream value.
  • Initial length is from 0 to 10^5.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Cost per call must not depend on the length of the stream.

Example 1. Input calls ["UniqueReader","first","append","first","append","first","uniqueCount","append","first"] with arguments [[[7,7,4]],[],[4],[],[9],[],[],[9],[]], output [null,4,null,-1,null,9,1,null,-1].

Example 2. Input calls ["UniqueReader","first","append","uniqueCount","append","uniqueCount","first"] with arguments [[[]],[],[6],[],[6],[],[]], output [null,-1,null,1,null,0,-1].

Prerequisites. The Pair Sum Lookup exercise above. This exercise changes the question from a pair to the arrival order of values that occur once.

What This Exercise Changes. The queue gains entries on first arrival only and loses them lazily at the front. A new counter, uniqueCount, moves when a value goes from 1 to 2.

Hint

What does the queue hold after a value arrives a second time, and who removes that entry?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The reader keeps a count map and a queue order of values in the order their count first reached 1. It also keeps the integer unique, the number of values with count exactly 1. An append raises the count of its value. When the new count is 1, the value joins the queue and unique grows. When the new count is 2, the value leaves the unique set, so unique shrinks, and the queue entry becomes stale. When the count is above 2, nothing else changes. The method first pops from the front while the front's count differs from 1, then reads the front.

The invariant is that every value with count 1 is in the queue in arrival order. Any other entry in the queue is stale and is removed before the queue is read. A value is pushed once because its count passes through 1 once, so each entry is popped at most once. The program compares all three methods with a recount over the full stream.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per append and uniqueCount, and O(1) amortized for first, because the total number of pops never exceeds the number of pushes.
  • Space is O(d) for d distinct values, since the map and the queue each hold at most one entry per value.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class UniqueReaderVary {
    static final class UniqueReader {
        private final HashMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> order = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private int unique;

        UniqueReader(int[] initial) {
            // Initial values go through the same path as later appends.
            for (int v : initial) append(v);
        }

        /**
         * Adds one value to the end of the stream.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: counts is exact, and order holds every value of count 1.
         */
        void append(int v) {
            // The authoritative count rises by one.
            int c = counts.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);
            if (c == 1) {
                // First arrival: the value becomes a candidate and a unique value.
                order.addLast(v);
                unique++;
            } else if (c == 2) {
                // Second arrival: the value stops being unique, its queue entry is now stale.
                unique--;
            }
        }

        /**
         * Returns the earliest value that occurs once, or -1.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a stale front is removed before the front is read.
         */
        int first() {
            // Each stale entry is popped once, and its push paid for the pop.
            while (!order.isEmpty() && counts.get(order.peekFirst()) != 1) order.pollFirst();
            // After the loop the front, if any, has count exactly 1.
            return order.isEmpty() ? -1 : order.peekFirst();
        }

        /**
         * Counts the values that occur exactly once.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         */
        int uniqueCount() {
            // The counter was maintained by append, so no scan is needed.
            return unique;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a 4 arrives twice, then a 9 arrives twice.
        UniqueReader a = new UniqueReader(new int[]{7, 7, 4});
        if (a.first() != 4) throw new AssertionError("e1 a");
        a.append(4);
        if (a.first() != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 b");
        a.append(9);
        if (a.first() != 9 || a.uniqueCount() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e1 c");
        a.append(9);
        if (a.first() != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 d");
        // Example 2: an empty reader grows one value and loses uniqueness.
        UniqueReader b = new UniqueReader(new int[0]);
        if (b.first() != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 a");
        b.append(6);
        if (b.uniqueCount() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 b");
        b.append(6);
        if (b.uniqueCount() != 0 || b.first() != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 c");
        // Random streams compare each answer with a full recount of the history.
        Random rnd = new Random(3806L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            int n0 = rnd.nextInt(6);
            int[] init = new int[n0];
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < n0; i++) { init[i] = rnd.nextInt(5); all.add(init[i]); }
            UniqueReader real = new UniqueReader(init);
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) { int v = rnd.nextInt(5); real.append(v); all.add(v); }
                else {
                    int expectFirst = -1, expectCount = 0;
                    for (int v : all) {
                        int occurrences = 0;
                        for (int w : all) if (w == v) occurrences++;
                        if (occurrences == 1) { if (expectFirst == -1) expectFirst = v; expectCount++; }
                    }
                    if (op == 1 && real.first() != expectFirst) throw new AssertionError("first of " + all);
                    if (op == 2 && real.uniqueCount() != expectCount) throw new AssertionError("uniqueCount of " + all);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Count Of Counts

LeetCode 2671

Problem Specification. Design the class FrequencyTracker. The method add(x) stores one more copy of x. The method removeOne(x) removes one copy of x and does nothing when x is absent. The method removeAll(x) removes every copy of x. The method hasFrequency(f) returns true when some value is stored exactly f times.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values from 0 to 10^9.
  • Frequency f satisfies 1 <= f <= 10^5, so no query asks about count 0.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Removal of an absent value changes no state.

Example 1. Input calls ["FrequencyTracker","add","add","add","hasFrequency","removeOne","hasFrequency","hasFrequency","removeAll","hasFrequency","hasFrequency"] with arguments [[],[5],[5],[8],[2],[5],[2],[1],[8],[1],[2]], output [null,null,null,null,true,null,false,true,null,true,false].

Example 2. Input calls ["FrequencyTracker","removeOne","hasFrequency","add","add","add","removeAll","hasFrequency","hasFrequency"] with arguments [[],[4],[1],[4],[4],[4],[4],[3],[1]], output [null,null,false,null,null,null,null,false,false].

Prerequisites. The Pair Sum Lookup and First Unique Reader exercises above. This exercise is a transfer prompt: the stream is correctable, so the tally must also move downward.

What This Exercise Changes. A count of zero is the boundary. The tally entry for the old count shrinks and no entry for count 0 appears. The new removeAll moves a value across many counts in one call.

Hint

When a count reaches 0, which of the two maps must lose an entry and which must receive none?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The tracker keeps counts, the number of copies of each value, and tally, the number of values that have each count. All changes pass through one private method, shift, which receives a value with its old and new count. It lowers tally[old] when the old count is positive and raises tally[new] when the new count is positive. It then writes the value into counts, or removes it when the new count is 0. Because every count of 0 is skipped on the tally side and removed on the map side, absent values leave no trace in either map.

The invariant is that tally[c] equals the number of values with count c for every c >= 1, and neither map contains a zero entry. removeOne on an absent value reads count 0 and does nothing. removeAll calls shift once with the full old count and a new count of 0, so the tally entry for the old count drops by exactly 1. The program compares the tracker with a recount of a plain map after every operation and checks that the tally map never holds an entry that is zero or negative.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, since shift performs a fixed number of hash operations and hasFrequency performs one lookup.
  • Space is O(d) for d distinct stored values, because the tally never has more entries than the map has values.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class FrequencyTrackerBoundary {
    static final class FrequencyTracker {
        private final HashMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
        private final HashMap<Integer, Integer> tally = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Moves one value from count "from" to count "to" in both maps.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterwards tally agrees with counts, and neither holds zero.
         */
        private void shift(int v, int from, int to) {
            // The old bucket loses the value, and an emptied bucket is removed.
            if (from > 0 && tally.merge(from, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) tally.remove(from);
            // The new bucket gains the value, except for count 0 which means absent.
            if (to > 0) tally.merge(to, 1, Integer::sum);
            // The authoritative map drops the key at zero and stores the count otherwise.
            if (to == 0) counts.remove(v); else counts.put(v, to);
        }

        /** Stores one more copy of x. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
        void add(int x) {
            // Read the old count, defaulting to 0 for a new value.
            int c = counts.getOrDefault(x, 0);
            // The value moves up by one.
            shift(x, c, c + 1);
        }

        /** Removes one copy of x if present. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
        void removeOne(int x) {
            // An absent value has count 0 and changes nothing.
            int c = counts.getOrDefault(x, 0);
            if (c > 0) shift(x, c, c - 1);
        }

        /** Removes every copy of x. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
        void removeAll(int x) {
            // One shift moves the value from its full count straight to absent.
            int c = counts.getOrDefault(x, 0);
            if (c > 0) shift(x, c, 0);
        }

        /** Tells whether some value occurs exactly f times. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
        boolean hasFrequency(int f) {
            // A single lookup in the tally answers the question.
            return tally.getOrDefault(f, 0) > 0;
        }

        /** Test hook: confirms the tally equals a recount of the counts. */
        boolean consistent() {
            // Recount the buckets from the authoritative map.
            HashMap<Integer, Integer> rec = new HashMap<>();
            for (int c : counts.values()) {
                if (c <= 0) return false;
                rec.merge(c, 1, Integer::sum);
            }
            // Equal maps imply no zero or negative tally entries either.
            return rec.equals(tally);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing one copy of 5 turns frequency 2 into frequency 1.
        FrequencyTracker a = new FrequencyTracker();
        a.add(5); a.add(5); a.add(8);
        if (!a.hasFrequency(2)) throw new AssertionError("e1 a");
        a.removeOne(5);
        if (a.hasFrequency(2) || !a.hasFrequency(1)) throw new AssertionError("e1 b");
        a.removeAll(8);
        if (!a.hasFrequency(1) || a.hasFrequency(2)) throw new AssertionError("e1 c");
        // Example 2: removing an absent value, then removing all copies, leaves no frequency.
        FrequencyTracker b = new FrequencyTracker();
        b.removeOne(4);
        if (b.hasFrequency(1) || !b.consistent()) throw new AssertionError("e2 a");
        b.add(4); b.add(4); b.add(4);
        b.removeAll(4);
        if (b.hasFrequency(3) || b.hasFrequency(1) || !b.consistent()) throw new AssertionError("e2 b");
        // Random operations compare with a recount, and the maps stay consistent after each call.
        Random rnd = new Random(3807L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            FrequencyTracker real = new FrequencyTracker();
            Map<Integer, Integer> model = new HashMap<>();
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(30); step > 0; step--) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(4);
                int v = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (op == 0) { real.add(v); model.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum); }
                else if (op == 1) { real.removeOne(v); if (model.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) <= 0) model.remove(v); }
                else if (op == 2) { real.removeAll(v); model.remove(v); }
                else {
                    int f = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
                    if (real.hasFrequency(f) != model.containsValue(f)) throw new AssertionError("hasFrequency " + f + " in " + model);
                }
                if (!real.consistent()) throw new AssertionError("tally disagrees with counts");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Suffix Run Counter

LeetCode 2526

Problem Specification. Design the class SuffixRun with a constructor that receives an int named target and a long named k. The method push(x) appends x to the stream and returns true when at least k values have been pushed and the last k are all equal to target. The method currentRun() returns the number of consecutive values equal to target at the end of the stream.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values and target are int values from 0 to 10^9.
  • Length k is a long from 1 to 10^12.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Memory must not depend on k or on the number of calls.

Example 1. Input calls ["SuffixRun","push","push","push","push","push","push","push"] with arguments [[6,3],[6],[6],[2],[6],[6],[6],[6]], output [null,false,false,false,false,false,true,true].

Example 2. Input calls ["SuffixRun","push","push","push","currentRun"] with arguments [[0,1],[0],[5],[0],[]], output [null,true,false,true,1].

Prerequisites. The state list of this lesson. This exercise is a transfer prompt: the stream needs one integer, not a stored window.

What This Exercise Changes. The summary of the stream is one integer that either grows by 1 or resets to 0. Memory is O(1) whatever k is.

Hint

Which single number must change when a non-matching value arrives, and does it ever need to know the earlier values?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The answer to every call depends on one number, the length of the run of target values at the end of the stream. A pushed value equal to target extends the run by 1, and any other value ends it, so the run becomes 0. The method push returns whether the run is at least k. Because a run of at least k includes at least k pushed values, the case "fewer than k values pushed" needs no separate test. The run uses long, since k can reach 10^12.

The invariant is that run equals the number of consecutive target values ending at the last pushed value. Nothing stored depends on k, so memory is constant. The program compares the class with a check of the last k entries of the full history, and it uses a large k with a direct assertion.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because each call performs one comparison and one assignment.
  • Space is O(1), as the object stores target, k and run and no history.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SuffixRunRecognize {
    static final class SuffixRun {
        private final int target;
        private final long k;
        private long run;

        SuffixRun(int target, long k) {
            // Only two constants and one counter are stored.
            this.target = target;
            this.k = k;
        }

        /**
         * Appends x and tells whether the last k values all equal target.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: run is the length of the target suffix of the stream.
         */
        boolean push(int x) {
            // A match extends the suffix, and anything else ends it.
            run = (x == target) ? run + 1 : 0;
            // A suffix of length k or more contains k pushed values, all equal to target.
            return run >= k;
        }

        /** Returns the current suffix length. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        long currentRun() {
            // The counter is the answer itself.
            return run;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the run of 6 breaks at 2 and then reaches 3 at the sixth push.
        SuffixRun a = new SuffixRun(6, 3);
        int[] in = {6, 6, 2, 6, 6, 6, 6};
        boolean[] want = {false, false, false, false, false, true, true};
        for (int i = 0; i < in.length; i++)
            if (a.push(in[i]) != want[i]) throw new AssertionError("e1 at " + i);
        // Example 2: with k equal to 1, every target value answers true at once.
        SuffixRun b = new SuffixRun(0, 1);
        if (!b.push(0) || b.push(5) || !b.push(0) || b.currentRun() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // A huge k needs no memory and stays false, and the run counter does not overflow int arithmetic.
        SuffixRun big = new SuffixRun(1, 1_000_000_000_000L);
        for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) if (big.push(1)) throw new AssertionError("big k");
        if (big.currentRun() != 1000) throw new AssertionError("big run");
        // Random streams compare each answer with a direct check of the last k stored values.
        Random rnd = new Random(3808L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            int target = rnd.nextInt(3);
            int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            SuffixRun real = new SuffixRun(target, k);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = rnd.nextInt(25); step > 0; step--) {
                int x = rnd.nextInt(3);
                all.add(x);
                boolean expect = all.size() >= k;
                for (int i = all.size() - k; expect && i < all.size(); i++) if (all.get(i) != target) expect = false;
                if (real.push(x) != expect) throw new AssertionError("push on " + all);
                int suffix = 0;
                for (int i = all.size() - 1; i >= 0 && all.get(i) == target; i--) suffix++;
                if (real.currentRun() != suffix) throw new AssertionError("currentRun on " + all);
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Temporal Indexes

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Date Search Misses A Day

A support engineer opens the log viewer and searches for errors from 2024-03-01 to 2024-03-05. The viewer stores each line with a full timestamp such as 2024-03-05 14:02:11. It compares the stored text with the two search strings. The text 2024-03-05 14:02:11 sorts after 2024-03-05, so every line from the last day falls outside the answer. The engineer sees four days of errors and closes the ticket while the incident is still running.

A second tool from the same team draws a chart of requests per second. It allocates one counter for every second between the two chart ends. A one-year chart needs about 31 million counters for every series, and the service runs out of memory when ten series are open. Both failures have one cause. The events had no agreed rule for where a query range ends, and no way to jump to the matching events. This lesson asks how to store each event once and answer questions about time ranges, granularity and empty stretches of time.

The stream in this lesson is append-only. Events arrive with non-decreasing timestamps, and nothing is corrected or expires afterward. Later parts mention what changes when arrivals come in any order.

Simple approach

Scanning Every Event For Every Query

The direct design keeps all event times in an array and answers each question with a full scan. A query for a time range counts the events that lie between two times. A query for counts per interval allocates one counter for every interval of fixed length between the two ends and adds each event to its counter.

final class NaiveEventLog {
    private long[] times = new long[16];
    private int n;

    void append(long t) {
        if (n == times.length) times = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(times, n * 2);
        times[n++] = t;
    }

    int countBetween(long from, long to) {          // both ends inclusive
        int c = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)                 // visits every stored event
            if (times[i] >= from && times[i] <= to) c++;
        return c;
    }

    int[] countsPerInterval(long from, long to, long size) {
        int[] counts = new int[(int) ((to - from) / size + 1)];   // one counter for every interval
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)                 // visits every stored event again
            if (times[i] >= from && times[i] <= to)
                counts[(int) ((times[i] - from) / size)]++;
        return counts;
    }
}

With events at times 2, 2, 9, 31, 33 and 58, the call countsPerInterval(0, 59, 10) returns {3, 0, 0, 2, 0, 1}, and countBetween(9, 33) returns 3. Both answers are correct.

Predict first: A caller appends 3 events and then calls countsPerInterval(0, 1000000000000L, 1). What happens, and which counters hold a nonzero value?

The cast of 10^12 + 1 to int produces a negative number, so the array allocation throws NegativeArraySizeException. If the span were smaller, such as 3 * 10^9 intervals, the cast would still be wrong, and a span of 10^9 would ask for 4 GB of counters. At most 3 counters could ever be nonzero. The method spends all of its memory on counters that stay at zero.

Bottleneck

Cost That Ignores The Query

Work Per Call Depends On The Whole Stream

Each query visits all n stored events, even when the matching events are a handful. With q queries the total cost is O(n * q). A dashboard that refreshes 50 panels every second over a log of 10^8 events performs 5 * 10^9 comparisons per second to read a few thousand events. The events are already in time order. Every event before the range and every event after it can be ruled out without a comparison.

Memory Depends On The Span Of Time

The counters array has one entry for each interval between the two ends. If the span is S and the interval length is w, the method uses O(S / w) memory and O(S / w + n) time, whatever the number of events. A quiet week at one-second resolution needs 604800 counters for a few hundred events. A sparse series can hold 3 events and still span 10^12 time units.

What A Correct Design Needs

A better design must satisfy three conditions. It must jump to the first matching event in O(log n) time and stop at the first event after the range. It must group events into intervals while it walks only over events, so empty intervals cost nothing. It must define, in one place, whether a range includes its ends, because that single choice decided the missing day in the log viewer.

Key idea

Order Plus A Fixed Boundary Rule

Events Live In A Sorted Index

A sorted index is a structure that keeps the stored events in timestamp order and can find the first event at or after any time by binary search. For an append-only stream with non-decreasing arrivals, a plain growable array is a sorted index, because each append goes at the end and keeps the order. The search costs O(log n). Everything between two search results is a contiguous run of the array, so a range count is a subtraction of two positions.

When events arrive in any order, an array can no longer append in O(1). A balanced tree map keeps the same search cost at O(log n) per insert, and its range view plays the role of the array slice.

A Half-Open Range Settles The Ends

A half-open range [a, b) includes a and excludes b. Two such ranges that share an end never share an event, and the length of [a, b) is exactly b - a. The search for the first event at or after a and the search for the first event at or after b mark the two ends of the answer. A query with an inclusive end [a, b] converts to [a, b + 1) for integer timestamps. The conversion happens once, at the entry of the method. This conversion is the fix for the missing day in the log viewer.

Buckets And Prefix Keys Group Events

A bucket is the set of timestamps that map to the same integer (t - from) / size for a query that starts at from with interval length size. Bucket b covers the half-open range [from + b * size, from + (b + 1) * size). Because the index is sorted, all events of one bucket sit next to each other. The walk counts one bucket and then moves to the next event with a larger bucket number. Buckets with no events are never visited, so a gap in time costs nothing.

A prefix key is the leading part of a fixed-width timestamp text, such as the first 10 characters of 2021:05:07:09:30:15, which name the day. When every field has a fixed number of digits, text order equals time order. Cutting both query ends to the same prefix length selects a granularity, and an inclusive comparison of prefixes then matches every event inside the chosen day, hour or minute.

The invariant of a temporal index is that timestamp order and bucket meaning agree with one stated boundary rule. Every event then belongs to exactly one bucket and one search position.

State

State Of The Index And The Query

The index and one query share these quantities, and the traces below follow them.

  • times is the long array of event timestamps in non-decreasing order.
  • n is the number of stored events, so valid positions run from 0 to n - 1.
  • lo and hi are the ends of the binary-search window, and the answer position lies between them.
  • from, to and size are the query ends and the bucket width, with from <= to and size >= 1.
  • b is the bucket number of the event under the pointer, computed as (times[i] - from) / size.
  • i is the position of the event the walk is looking at, from lowerBound(from) up to n.

The invariant for the search is that every position below lo holds a time smaller than the target. Every position at or after hi holds a time at least as large.

Walkthrough

Tracing A Search And A Bucket Walk

Two Binary Searches Mark A Range

The first trace stores the times 3, 3, 8, 14, 15, 15, 27 and 40, and asks for the events in [8, 28). The search for 8 starts with the window [0, 8). The middle position 4 holds 15, which is at least 8, so hi moves to 4. Position 2 holds 8, so hi moves to 2. Position 1 holds 3, which is below 8, so lo moves to 2 and the window closes at position 2. The second search, for 28, closes at position 7, because 27 sits at position 6 and 40 sits at position 7. The count is 7 minus 2, which is 5. The pointer mid shows which position each step compares, and the value -1 means that no comparison is running.

A Walk Skips Empty Buckets

The second trace stores the times 2, 2, 9, 31, 33 and 58, and asks for counts with width 10 over the inclusive range 0 to 59. That range holds 6 buckets. One search finds the first event at or after 0. The walk then takes the events 2, 2 and 9, which all have bucket number 0, and records the pair of bucket start 0 and count 3. The next event, 31, has bucket number 3, so the walk jumps over buckets 1 and 2 without touching them. Events 31 and 33 form the pair 30 and 2. The event 58 forms the pair 50 and 1. The result lists only three pairs, and the three missing buckets have count 0 by definition.

Code

A Sorted Array With A Boundary Rule

The Index And Its Search

The class keeps the array and the size. The search method returns the first position whose time is at least the target, and the range count uses two such positions. The method countBetween takes an inclusive end and converts it once.

final class TimeIndex {
    private long[] times = new long[16];
    private int n;

    void append(long t) {
        if (n > 0 && t < times[n - 1]) throw new IllegalArgumentException("arrivals must not go backward");  // keeps the array sorted
        if (n == times.length) times = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(times, n * 2);                                 // amortized O(1) growth
        times[n++] = t;
    }

    private int lowerBound(long target) {
        int lo = 0, hi = n;                          // window [lo, hi) holds the answer position
        while (lo < hi) {                            // each pass halves the window, O(log n) passes
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            if (times[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;   // everything up to mid is too early
            else hi = mid;                           // mid could be the answer
        }
        return lo;
    }

    int countBetween(long from, long to) {           // inclusive ends, to < Long.MAX_VALUE
        return lowerBound(to + 1) - lowerBound(from);   // half-open [from, to + 1), a subtraction of two positions
    }
}

The Bucket Walk

The walk starts at the first event of the query. It reads the bucket number of the current event, advances over every event with the same number, and records one pair. Pairs are stored as the bucket start and the count, and only nonempty buckets appear. The method takes the sorted array and its length, so it repeats the same binary search inline.

final class BucketWalk {
    static java.util.List<long[]> nonEmpty(long[] times, int n, long from, long to, long size) {
        java.util.List<long[]> out = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        int lo = 0, hi = n;                                         // binary search for the first event at or after from
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            if (times[mid] < from) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
        }
        int i = lo;                                                 // first event inside the query
        while (i < n && times[i] <= to) {                           // stops at the first event after the query
            long b = (times[i] - from) / size;                      // bucket number of this event
            int start = i;
            while (i < n && times[i] <= to && (times[i] - from) / size == b) i++;   // same bucket, same pair
            out.add(new long[]{from + b * size, i - start});        // bucket start and its count
        }
        return out;                                                 // empty buckets are absent, so their count is 0
    }
}

Cutting A Timestamp To A Granularity

For fixed-width text, the granularity is a prefix length. The format YYYY:MM:DD:hh:mm:ss gives each unit a fixed length that includes the colons before it.

  • Year keeps 4 characters.
  • Month keeps 7 characters.
  • Day keeps 10 characters.
  • Hour keeps 13 characters.
  • Minute keeps 16 characters.
  • Second keeps 19 characters, the whole text.

The two query ends are cut to the unit length and compared inclusively with each stored text cut to the same length.

final class PrefixKeys {
    static int length(String unit) {
        return switch (unit) {
            case "Year" -> 4;  case "Month" -> 7;  case "Day" -> 10;       // lengths for YYYY:MM:DD:hh:mm:ss
            case "Hour" -> 13; case "Minute" -> 16; case "Second" -> 19;
            default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException(unit);
        };
    }

    static String prefixKey(String timestamp, int digits) {
        return timestamp.substring(0, digits);       // fixed width, so text order equals time order
    }

    static boolean inRange(String stamp, String start, String end, String unit) {
        int len = length(unit);
        String key = prefixKey(stamp, len);                                   // the stored text cut to the unit
        return key.compareTo(prefixKey(start, len)) >= 0                      // inclusive lower end
            && key.compareTo(prefixKey(end, len)) <= 0;                       // inclusive upper end
    }
}

Cost

  • append costs O(1) amortized time and O(1) extra space per event.
  • countBetween costs O(log n) time and O(1) extra space.
  • BucketWalk.nonEmpty costs O(log n + m) time, where m is the number of events inside the query, and O(k) space for the k nonempty buckets it returns.
When to use it

When To Index By Time

Recognizing The Pattern

Use a temporal index when events are stored once and later read by a time range, a granularity or an interval grid. Phrases such as "between two times", "per minute", "every hour" and "inclusive" in a requirement point to it. Before coding, state whether the stream is append-only, correctable or expiring, and write the one boundary rule in a comment next to the entry method. The invariant to protect is that timestamp order and bucket meaning agree with that rule, so a range query and a bucket query on the same data never disagree.

A False Friend

A hash map keyed by the raw timestamp is a false friend of the sorted index. It answers "how many events at exactly time 90" in O(1). It cannot answer a range, because a hash has no order and a range scan visits every key. A fixed array with one counter for every possible interval is a second false friend. It passes small tests and fails on a sparse series that spans a long time.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If events expire, change or disappear, a plain sorted array no longer shows the current state. The earlier lessons of this chapter on expiring state and on corrected values apply first. If timestamp text has variable width, such as 2021:5:7 next to 2021:05:07, text order and time order differ, and the text must be parsed into numbers before it is indexed. If the interval length changes from query to query and the query ends are always the stream ends, a running prefix total may be simpler than any index.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Log Storage System

LeetCode 635

Problem Specification. Design the class LogStore. The method store(id, timestamp) saves a log with an integer id and a timestamp text of the form YYYY:MM:DD:hh:mm:ss. The method retrieve(start, end, unit) takes two timestamp texts and a unit, which is one of Year, Month, Day, Hour, Minute or Second. It returns the ids of all stored logs whose timestamp, cut to the unit, lies between start and end cut to the unit, both ends included. The ids come back in ascending order.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 500 per object.
  • Ids are distinct integers from 1 to 500.
  • Timestamps have fixed width, with years from 2000 to 2099, and may repeat across logs.
  • Order of start and end is guaranteed so that start is not later than end.

Example 1. Input calls ["LogStore","store","store","store","store","retrieve","retrieve"] with arguments [[],[1,"2021:05:07:09:30:15"],[2,"2021:05:07:23:59:59"],[3,"2021:05:08:00:00:00"],[4,"2021:06:01:12:00:00"],["2021:05:07:12:00:00","2021:05:08:00:00:00","Day"],["2021:05:07:12:00:00","2021:05:08:00:00:00","Hour"]], output [null,null,null,null,null,[1,2,3],[2,3]].

Example 2. Input calls ["LogStore","store","store","store","retrieve","retrieve"] with arguments [[],[7,"2019:12:31:23:59:59"],[8,"2020:01:01:00:00:00"],[9,"2020:01:01:00:00:01"],["2019:01:01:00:00:00","2020:01:01:00:00:00","Year"],["2020:01:01:00:00:00","2020:01:01:00:00:00","Second"]], output [null,null,null,null,[7,8,9],[8]].

Prerequisites. The prefix key and the sorted index from this lesson. This exercise reads the granularity from a text argument and selects events by a prefix comparison.

What This Exercise Changes. The query end is no longer a time but a prefix. Both ends are cut to the unit first, and then the comparison is inclusive on the cut texts, which is the rule that fixes the log viewer failure.

Hint

What does the pair of cut texts 2021:05:07 and 2021:05:08 select, and why does the original start text 2021:05:07:12:00:00 not matter for the unit Day?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The store keeps a TreeMap from the full timestamp text to the ids stored at that text. Because every field has a fixed width, the text order of two timestamps equals their time order, and String.compareTo compares the characters left to right. A retrieval first cuts the start and the end to the prefix length of the unit. It then pads each cut text back to full width. The start is padded with the lowest filler 0000:00:00:00:00:00 and the end with the highest filler 9999:99:99:99:99:99. Every stored timestamp whose prefix equals the cut start is at least the padded start, and every one whose prefix equals the cut end is at most the padded end. A single subMap call with both ends included therefore returns exactly the logs whose cut texts lie between the cut query ends. The ids are collected and sorted.

The invariant is that the map holds each timestamp once, in text order, and that a padded end is never smaller than any real timestamp with the same prefix. The program compares the class with a direct scan that cuts every stored text on random data. It also asserts the claim behind the log viewer failure, that a date without a time sorts before a full timestamp of that date.

Complexity.

  • Time for store is O(log n) for the map insert. A retrieval costs O(log n + r log r) for r returned ids, because one search finds the range view and a sort orders the ids.
  • Space is O(n) for the map, plus O(r) for one answer.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class LogStorageBuild {
    static final class LogStore {
        private static final String LOW = "0000:00:00:00:00:00";    // lowest text of full width
        private static final String HIGH = "9999:99:99:99:99:99";   // highest text of full width
        private final TreeMap<String, List<Integer>> byTime = new TreeMap<>();

        /**
         * Maps a unit name to the length of its prefix.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the lengths 4, 7, 10, 13, 16 and 19 end right before a separator or at the end.
         */
        static int digits(String unit) {
            // Each case returns the number of characters that name the unit and everything above it.
            switch (unit) {
                case "Year": return 4;
                case "Month": return 7;
                case "Day": return 10;
                case "Hour": return 13;
                case "Minute": return 16;
                default: return 19;
            }
        }

        /**
         * Saves one log.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: the map stays ordered by timestamp text.
         */
        void store(int id, String timestamp) {
            // One map insert places the id under its timestamp, and equal timestamps share a list.
            byTime.computeIfAbsent(timestamp, k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(id);
        }

        /**
         * Returns the ids whose timestamp, cut to the unit, lies between the cut ends, both included.
         * Time: O(log n + r log r). Space: O(r).
         * Invariant: the padded start is the lowest text with the cut start prefix, the padded end the highest.
         */
        List<Integer> retrieve(String start, String end, String unit) {
            int d = digits(unit);
            // Cut each end to the unit and pad it to full width, so both are valid map keys.
            String lo = start.substring(0, d) + LOW.substring(d);
            String hi = end.substring(0, d) + HIGH.substring(d);
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            // The range view visits only the stored timestamps between the two padded ends.
            for (Map.Entry<String, List<Integer>> e : byTime.subMap(lo, true, hi, true).entrySet()) out.addAll(e.getValue());
            // Ascending ids give a deterministic answer.
            Collections.sort(out);
            return out;
        }
    }

    static final String[] UNITS = {"Year", "Month", "Day", "Hour", "Minute", "Second"};

    static String stamp(Random rnd) {
        // Small field ranges force many equal prefixes and boundary cases.
        return String.format("%04d:%02d:%02d:%02d:%02d:%02d", 2019 + rnd.nextInt(2), 1 + rnd.nextInt(2),
                1 + rnd.nextInt(2), rnd.nextInt(2), rnd.nextInt(2), rnd.nextInt(3));
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The failure from the lesson: a date alone sorts before a timestamp of that date.
        if ("2021:05:07:12:00:00".compareTo("2021:05:07") <= 0) throw new AssertionError("text order");
        // Example 1: day and hour units.
        LogStore a = new LogStore();
        a.store(1, "2021:05:07:09:30:15");
        a.store(2, "2021:05:07:23:59:59");
        a.store(3, "2021:05:08:00:00:00");
        a.store(4, "2021:06:01:12:00:00");
        if (!a.retrieve("2021:05:07:12:00:00", "2021:05:08:00:00:00", "Day").equals(List.of(1, 2, 3))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 day");
        if (!a.retrieve("2021:05:07:12:00:00", "2021:05:08:00:00:00", "Hour").equals(List.of(2, 3))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 hour");
        // Example 2: year unit and an exact second.
        LogStore b = new LogStore();
        b.store(7, "2019:12:31:23:59:59");
        b.store(8, "2020:01:01:00:00:00");
        b.store(9, "2020:01:01:00:00:01");
        if (!b.retrieve("2019:01:01:00:00:00", "2020:01:01:00:00:00", "Year").equals(List.of(7, 8, 9))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 year");
        if (!b.retrieve("2020:01:01:00:00:00", "2020:01:01:00:00:00", "Second").equals(List.of(8))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 second");
        // Random data is compared with a scan that cuts every stored text.
        Random rnd = new Random(3806L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            LogStore real = new LogStore();
            List<String> stamps = new ArrayList<>();
            int n = rnd.nextInt(12);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { String s = stamp(rnd); stamps.add(s); real.store(i + 1, s); }
            for (int q = 0; q < 6; q++) {
                String x = stamp(rnd), y = stamp(rnd);
                String s = x.compareTo(y) <= 0 ? x : y, e = x.compareTo(y) <= 0 ? y : x;
                String unit = UNITS[rnd.nextInt(UNITS.length)];
                int d = LogStore.digits(unit);
                List<Integer> expect = new ArrayList<>();
                for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                    String cut = stamps.get(i).substring(0, d);
                    if (cut.compareTo(s.substring(0, d)) >= 0 && cut.compareTo(e.substring(0, d)) <= 0) expect.add(i + 1);
                }
                if (!real.retrieve(s, e, unit).equals(expect)) throw new AssertionError("random " + unit);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Tweet Counts Per Frequency

LeetCode 1348

Problem Specification. Design the class ReadingCounter. The method record(sensor, time) stores one reading of a named sensor at an integer second time. The method counts(sensor, unit, from, to) takes unit equal to minute, hour or day, with widths 60, 3600 and 86400 seconds. It returns a list in which entry b counts the readings of that sensor with time in [from + b * width, from + (b + 1) * width). The last bucket ends at to and may be shorter. The list has exactly (to - from) / width + 1 entries.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Times are long values from 0 to 10^12, and readings may arrive in any order.
  • Range satisfies from <= to and to - from <= 10^7.
  • Sensor names have 1 to 10 lowercase letters, and an unknown sensor gives a list of zeros.

Example 1. Input calls ["ReadingCounter","record","record","record","record","record","record","counts","counts"] with arguments [[],["a",10],["a",70],["a",75],["a",130],["a",3700],["a",3700],["a","minute",10,200],["a","hour",0,7200]], output [null,null,null,null,null,null,null,[1,2,1,0],[4,2,0]].

Example 2. Input calls ["ReadingCounter","record","record","record","counts","counts","counts"] with arguments [[],["b",5],["b",5],["b",120],["b","minute",0,119],["b","minute",0,120],["c","day",0,0]], output [null,null,null,null,[2,0],[2,0,1],[0]].

Prerequisites. The Log Storage System exercise above. This exercise keeps one sorted index for each key and turns a time range into a list of bucket counts.

What This Exercise Changes. Arrivals come in any order, so the array becomes a sorted map from time to count. The answer list is dense and has one entry for every bucket, and its length is limited by the constraint on the range.

Hint

Which tree view returns the readings of one sensor inside a time range, and how does the bucket number of each reading find its slot in the answer list?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Each sensor owns a TreeMap from time to the number of readings at that time. Readings may arrive in any order, so a tree map keeps the order that an array cannot keep with O(1) appends. A query computes the number of buckets as (to - from) / width + 1 and allocates one int slot per bucket. The constraint on the range bounds that number. The query then reads only the entries of the sensor between from and to, both included, and adds each count to the slot (time - from) / width. The last bucket is shorter automatically, because no entry beyond to is read.

The invariant is that every reading inside the range is added to exactly one slot, and no reading outside the range is read. The program compares the class with a list of all readings that is scanned for each query, using random times with many duplicates.

Complexity.

  • Time for record is O(log n). A query costs O(log n + m + B) for m readings in the range and B buckets, because the answer list has B entries.
  • Space is O(n) for the stored readings and O(B) for one answer.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class CountPerIntervalVary {
    static final class ReadingCounter {
        private final Map<String, TreeMap<Long, Integer>> bySensor = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Returns the bucket width of a unit name in seconds.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the widths are 60, 3600 and 86400.
         */
        static long width(String unit) {
            // The three allowed units map to fixed widths.
            return unit.equals("minute") ? 60L : unit.equals("hour") ? 3600L : 86400L;
        }

        /**
         * Stores one reading.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: the map holds the number of readings at each distinct time.
         */
        void record(String sensor, long time) {
            // merge adds 1 to the count at this time, or creates the entry with 1.
            bySensor.computeIfAbsent(sensor, k -> new TreeMap<>()).merge(time, 1, Integer::sum);
        }

        /**
         * Counts readings per bucket over the inclusive range [from, to].
         * Time: O(log n + m + B). Space: O(B).
         * Invariant: each reading adds to the slot (time - from) / width exactly once.
         */
        List<Integer> counts(String sensor, String unit, long from, long to) {
            long w = width(unit);
            // The range constraint keeps the number of buckets small enough for an int.
            int buckets = (int) ((to - from) / w + 1);
            int[] slots = new int[buckets];
            TreeMap<Long, Integer> m = bySensor.get(sensor);
            // An unknown sensor skips the loop and returns zeros.
            if (m != null)
                // The range view visits only readings with from <= time <= to.
                for (Map.Entry<Long, Integer> e : m.subMap(from, true, to, true).entrySet())
                    slots[(int) ((e.getKey() - from) / w)] += e.getValue();
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>(buckets);
            // Copy the slots into the answer list.
            for (int c : slots) out.add(c);
            return out;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: minute buckets with a short last bucket, then hour buckets.
        ReadingCounter a = new ReadingCounter();
        for (long t : new long[]{10, 70, 75, 130, 3700, 3700}) a.record("a", t);
        if (!a.counts("a", "minute", 10, 200).equals(List.of(1, 2, 1, 0))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 minute");
        if (!a.counts("a", "hour", 0, 7200).equals(List.of(4, 2, 0))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 hour");
        // Example 2: duplicates, an inclusive end that opens a new bucket, and an unknown sensor.
        ReadingCounter b = new ReadingCounter();
        b.record("b", 5); b.record("b", 5); b.record("b", 120);
        if (!b.counts("b", "minute", 0, 119).equals(List.of(2, 0))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 a");
        if (!b.counts("b", "minute", 0, 120).equals(List.of(2, 0, 1))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 b");
        if (!b.counts("c", "day", 0, 0).equals(List.of(0))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 c");
        // Random readings are compared with a scan of all readings for every query.
        Random rnd = new Random(3807L);
        String[] units = {"minute", "hour", "day"};
        String[] names = {"p", "q"};
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            ReadingCounter real = new ReadingCounter();
            List<long[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            List<String> who = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = rnd.nextInt(15); i > 0; i--) {
                long time = rnd.nextInt(400);
                String s = names[rnd.nextInt(2)];
                real.record(s, time); all.add(new long[]{time}); who.add(s);
            }
            for (int q = 0; q < 5; q++) {
                long from = rnd.nextInt(300), to = from + rnd.nextInt(300);
                String unit = rnd.nextInt(2) == 0 ? "minute" : units[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                String s = names[rnd.nextInt(2)];
                long w = ReadingCounter.width(unit);
                int[] expect = new int[(int) ((to - from) / w + 1)];
                for (int i = 0; i < all.size(); i++) {
                    long time = all.get(i)[0];
