                    if (who.get(i).equals(s) && time >= from && time <= to) expect[(int) ((time - from) / w)]++;
                }
                List<Integer> got = real.counts(s, unit, from, to);
                if (got.size() != expect.length) throw new AssertionError("size");
                for (int i = 0; i < expect.length; i++) if (got.get(i) != expect[i]) throw new AssertionError("count");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Sparse Empty Buckets

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class SparseSeries. The method record(time) appends one event, and times never decrease from call to call. Bucket b of the inclusive range [from, to] starts at from + b * size. The method nonEmpty(from, to, size) returns a pair {start, count} for each bucket that holds an event, in ascending order of start. The method countAt(from, to, size, index) returns the count of bucket index counted from 0, and it returns 0 for an empty bucket. It returns -1 when index is not a bucket of the range. The last bucket ends at to.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Times and ends are long values from 0 to 10^15, with from <= to.
  • Size is a long from 1 to 10^15.
  • Index is a long from 0 to 10^15.
  • Memory must not depend on the number of buckets in the range.

Example 1. Input calls ["SparseSeries","record","record","record","record","record","record","nonEmpty","countAt","countAt","countAt"] with arguments [[],[2],[2],[9],[31],[33],[58],[0,59,10],[0,59,10,1],[0,59,10,5],[0,59,10,6]], output [null,null,null,null,null,null,null,[[0,3],[30,2],[50,1]],0,1,-1].

Example 2. Input calls ["SparseSeries","record","record","nonEmpty","countAt","countAt"] with arguments [[],[0],[1000000000000000],[0,1000000000000000,7],[0,1000000000000000,7,142857142857142],[0,1000000000000000,7,142857142857143]], output [null,null,null,[[0,1],[999999999999994,1]],1,-1]. The last bucket starts at 999999999999994 and holds the event at 10^15.

Prerequisites. The Tweet Counts Per Frequency exercise above. This exercise changes the output so that empty buckets are never listed, and the range can be too long to list.

What This Exercise Changes. The answer is no longer a dense list. The walk visits events and never buckets, and every number that involves a bucket count, an index or a start uses long arithmetic.

Hint

How many buckets does the second example have, and which type holds that number? Which two binary searches count the events of one bucket?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Events arrive in non-decreasing order, so a growable long array is a sorted index. Two helper searches return the first position whose time is at least a target. The method nonEmpty searches for from once, then walks the events up to to. For each event it computes the bucket number (time - from) / size. It advances over all events with the same number and emits the bucket start with their count. Empty buckets never appear, so the cost depends on the events in the range and not on the number of buckets. The method countAt first computes the number of buckets (to - from) / size + 1, which reaches 10^15 and so needs a long. It returns -1 for an index outside that count. Otherwise it takes the bucket start from + index * size and the bucket end min(start + size - 1, to). The count is the difference of two searches.

The invariant is that a pair covers one run of events with one bucket number. The product index * size never exceeds to - from, so it fits in a long. The program also checks the Java facts that the lesson relies on. The cast of 10^12 + 1 to int is negative, and the allocation with that size throws NegativeArraySizeException. Random small ranges are checked against a dense array that counts every bucket.

Complexity.

  • Time for record is O(1) amortized. The method nonEmpty costs O(log n + m) for m events in the range, and countAt costs O(log n).
  • Space is O(n) for the events and O(k) for the k pairs of one answer, and no space depends on the number of buckets.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SparseEmptyBucketsBoundary {
    static final class SparseSeries {
        private long[] times = new long[16];
        private int n;

        /**
         * Appends one event.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: the array stays in non-decreasing order.
         */
        void record(long time) {
            // A smaller time would break the order, so it is rejected.
            if (n > 0 && time < times[n - 1]) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time went backward");
            // Doubling the array keeps the append cost O(1) amortized.
            if (n == times.length) times = Arrays.copyOf(times, n * 2);
            times[n++] = time;
        }

        /**
         * Finds the first position whose time is at least the target.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: every position below lo is smaller than target, every position from hi on is not.
         */
        private int lowerBound(long target) {
            int lo = 0, hi = n;
            // Each pass halves the window, so there are O(log n) passes.
            while (lo < hi) {
                int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
                // A time below the target lies left of the answer.
                if (times[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
                else hi = mid;
            }
            return lo;
        }

        /**
         * Lists the nonempty buckets of [from, to] in ascending order.
         * Time: O(log n + m). Space: O(k).
         * Invariant: each pair covers one maximal run of events with equal bucket number.
         */
        List<long[]> nonEmpty(long from, long to, long size) {
            List<long[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
            // One search finds the first event of the range.
            int i = lowerBound(from);
            // The walk stops at the first event after the range.
            while (i < n && times[i] <= to) {
                long b = (times[i] - from) / size;
                int start = i;
                // Advance over every event with the same bucket number.
                while (i < n && times[i] <= to && (times[i] - from) / size == b) i++;
                // The pair holds the bucket start and the event count.
                out.add(new long[]{from + b * size, i - start});
            }
            return out;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the count of one bucket, 0 if empty, -1 if the index is outside the range.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: index * size stays at most to - from, so no overflow occurs.
         */
        long countAt(long from, long to, long size, long index) {
            long buckets = (to - from) / size + 1;
            // An index outside the bucket count is not a bucket.
            if (index < 0 || index >= buckets) return -1;
            long start = from + index * size;
            // The last bucket ends at the inclusive end of the range.
            long end = Math.min(start + size - 1, to);
            // Two searches count the events with start <= time <= end.
            return lowerBound(end + 1) - lowerBound(start);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The lesson claim: casting 10^12 + 1 to int gives a negative size, and allocation fails.
        if ((int) (1_000_000_000_000L + 1) >= 0) throw new AssertionError("cast should be negative");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { int[] x = new int[(int) (1_000_000_000_000L + 1)]; } catch (NegativeArraySizeException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("negative size must throw");
        // Example 1: three nonempty buckets among six.
        SparseSeries a = new SparseSeries();
        for (long t : new long[]{2, 2, 9, 31, 33, 58}) a.record(t);
        List<long[]> got = a.nonEmpty(0, 59, 10);
        long[][] want = {{0, 3}, {30, 2}, {50, 1}};
        if (got.size() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1 size");
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) if (!Arrays.equals(got.get(i), want[i])) throw new AssertionError("example 1 pair");
        if (a.countAt(0, 59, 10, 1) != 0 || a.countAt(0, 59, 10, 5) != 1 || a.countAt(0, 59, 10, 6) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 1 countAt");
        // Example 2: two events 10^15 apart, memory does not depend on the bucket count.
        SparseSeries b = new SparseSeries();
        b.record(0); b.record(1_000_000_000_000_000L);
        List<long[]> far = b.nonEmpty(0, 1_000_000_000_000_000L, 7);
        if (far.size() != 2 || far.get(0)[0] != 0 || far.get(0)[1] != 1 || far.get(1)[0] != 999_999_999_999_994L || far.get(1)[1] != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 pairs");
        if (b.countAt(0, 1_000_000_000_000_000L, 7, 142_857_142_857_142L) != 1 || b.countAt(0, 1_000_000_000_000_000L, 7, 142_857_142_857_143L) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 countAt");
        // Random small ranges are compared with a dense array of every bucket.
        Random rnd = new Random(3808L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            SparseSeries real = new SparseSeries();
            List<Long> ev = new ArrayList<>();
            long cur = rnd.nextInt(5);
            for (int i = rnd.nextInt(12); i > 0; i--) { cur += rnd.nextInt(9); ev.add(cur); real.record(cur); }
            long from = rnd.nextInt(10), to = from + rnd.nextInt(80), size = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            int nb = (int) ((to - from) / size + 1);
            long[] dense = new long[nb];
            for (long x : ev) if (x >= from && x <= to) dense[(int) ((x - from) / size)]++;
            List<long[]> pairs = real.nonEmpty(from, to, size);
            int p = 0;
            for (int k = 0; k < nb; k++) {
                if (dense[k] > 0) {
                    long[] pr = pairs.get(p++);
                    if (pr[0] != from + (long) k * size || pr[1] != dense[k]) throw new AssertionError("pair");
                }
                if (real.countAt(from, to, size, k) != dense[k]) throw new AssertionError("countAt");
            }
            if (p != pairs.size() || real.countAt(from, to, size, nb) != -1) throw new AssertionError("extra pairs");
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design Exam Scores Tracker

LeetCode 3709

Problem Specification. Design the class ScoreLedger. The method add(time, score) stores one exam result, and the times of successive calls strictly increase. The method total(from, to) returns the sum of the scores with a time in the inclusive range [from, to]. It returns 0 when from > to or when no result lies inside the range.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Times are long values from 1 to 10^9, strictly increasing across add calls.
  • Scores are int values from 1 to 10^9.
  • Sums can exceed Integer.MAX_VALUE, so the return type is long.

Example 1. Input calls ["ScoreLedger","add","add","add","total","total","total"] with arguments [[],[5,900000000],[8,800000000],[20,700000000],[5,20],[6,19],[9,10]], output [null,null,null,null,2400000000,800000000,0].

Example 2. Input calls ["ScoreLedger","add","add","total","total","total"] with arguments [[],[3,10],[4,20],[4,3],[1,1000000000],[3,3]], output [null,null,null,0,30,10].

Prerequisites. The Sparse Empty Buckets exercise above. Here every query is a time range, and a running total replaces the walk.

What This Exercise Changes. Each result carries a value, so a count by subtraction of positions becomes a sum by subtraction of running totals. The index is still a sorted array, but the second array of long totals answers every range in O(log n).

Hint

Which array holds the sum of all scores up to each position, and which two positions does a range need?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The ledger keeps two parallel long arrays. The array times holds the result times, which strictly increase, so it is a sorted index. The array prefix has one more slot than times, and prefix[i] is the sum of the first i scores. A range [from, to] becomes the half-open range [from, to + 1) once, at the entry of total. Two binary searches give the position l of the first result at or after from and the position r of the first result at or after to + 1. The answer is prefix[r] - prefix[l], which is the sum of the scores at the positions l through r - 1. A reversed range gives 0 by an explicit test.

The invariant is that prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + score_i at all times, so any contiguous run sums by one subtraction. The sums use long, because three scores of 10^9 already exceed Integer.MAX_VALUE. The program asserts that fact and compares the class with a loop over all results on random data.

Complexity.

  • Time for add is O(1) amortized, and total costs O(log n) for two searches.
  • Space is O(n) for the two arrays, and a query uses O(1) extra space.
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ExamScoresTrackerRecognize {
    static final class ScoreLedger {
        private long[] times = new long[16];
        private long[] prefix = new long[17];
        private int n;

        /**
         * Stores one result.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: prefix[i + 1] equals prefix[i] plus the score at position i.
         */
        void add(long time, int score) {
            // Times must strictly increase, which keeps the array sorted without duplicates.
            if (n > 0 && time <= times[n - 1]) throw new IllegalArgumentException("times must increase");
            // Both arrays double together, so one growth test is enough.
            if (n == times.length) { times = Arrays.copyOf(times, n * 2); prefix = Arrays.copyOf(prefix, n * 2 + 1); }
            times[n] = time;
            // The running total adds the score as a long, so large sums do not wrap.
            prefix[n + 1] = prefix[n] + score;
            n++;
        }

        /**
         * Finds the first position whose time is at least the target.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: every position below lo is smaller than target, every position from hi on is not.
         */
        private int lowerBound(long target) {
            int lo = 0, hi = n;
            // The window halves on every pass.
            while (lo < hi) {
                int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
                // A time below the target lies left of the answer.
                if (times[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
                else hi = mid;
            }
            return lo;
        }

        /**
         * Sums the scores with from <= time <= to.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the half-open range [from, to + 1) selects positions l through r - 1.
         */
        long total(long from, long to) {
            // A reversed range holds no result.
            if (from > to) return 0;
            int l = lowerBound(from);
            int r = lowerBound(to + 1);
            // One subtraction of running totals sums the contiguous run.
            return prefix[r] - prefix[l];
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The lesson claim: three scores of 10^9 exceed the int range.
        if (3L * 1_000_000_000 <= Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("int overflow claim");
        // Example 1: a sum above the int range, an inner range, and an empty range.
        ScoreLedger a = new ScoreLedger();
        a.add(5, 900_000_000); a.add(8, 800_000_000); a.add(20, 700_000_000);
        if (a.total(5, 20) != 2_400_000_000L || a.total(6, 19) != 800_000_000L || a.total(9, 10) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a reversed range, the full span, and a single point.
        ScoreLedger b = new ScoreLedger();
        b.add(3, 10); b.add(4, 20);
        if (b.total(4, 3) != 0 || b.total(1, 1_000_000_000) != 30 || b.total(3, 3) != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Random ledgers are compared with a loop over every result.
        Random rnd = new Random(3809L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            ScoreLedger real = new ScoreLedger();
            int n = rnd.nextInt(40);
            long[] ts = new long[n];
            long[] sc = new long[n];
            long cur = 0;
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                cur += 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
                ts[i] = cur;
                sc[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000);
                real.add(ts[i], (int) sc[i]);
            }
            for (int q = 0; q < 8; q++) {
                long from = rnd.nextInt(80) - 3, to = rnd.nextInt(80) - 3;
                long expect = 0;
                for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (ts[i] >= from && ts[i] <= to) expect += sc[i];
                if (real.total(from, to) != expect) throw new AssertionError("total");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Randomized Evolution

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Fair Shuffle Is Not Fair

A published security analysis of an online poker dealer found that its shuffle gave some deck orders a higher chance than others. The routine walked down the deck and swapped each card with a card at a random position anywhere in the deck. The loop looks balanced and runs in linear time. Players who knew which orders were favored could narrow down the hidden cards. A music player shows a quieter version of the same family of bugs. Its "restore order" button returns the list that the shuffle button already rearranged, because both buttons share one array.

Both failures sit in a collection that changes between calls and then returns random output. This lesson treats the collection as correctable. The set of values never grows and never expires, but every call to shuffle overwrites the arrangement, and a reset call restores the original one. The lesson asks two questions. How does a shuffle give every arrangement the same chance, and which copy of the data must stay untouched so that reset is still possible?

Simple approach

Swapping With Any Position

The direct design walks the array from left to right and swaps position i with a random position from the whole array. A second field keeps the array that reset should return. The class below uses an injected IntUnaryOperator called pick as its source of randomness, so a test can script the draws. A call pick.applyAsInt(b) returns an integer from 0 to b - 1.

final class NaiveShuffleBox {
    private final int[] original;
    private final int[] array;
    private final java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator pick;

    NaiveShuffleBox(int[] nums, java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator pick) {
        this.original = nums;      // remembers the input
        this.array = nums;         // shuffles the input
        this.pick = pick;
    }

    int[] shuffle() {
        int n = array.length;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int j = pick.applyAsInt(n);                 // any position from 0 to n - 1
            int t = array[i]; array[i] = array[j]; array[j] = t;
        }
        return array;
    }

    int[] reset() { return original; }
}

On random draws the class returns arrays that look shuffled, and a quick visual check passes. For a different approach, a second design lists all n! orders and returns one chosen at random. That design is correct, and it is far too large for any array with more than a dozen elements.

Predict first: A caller builds NaiveShuffleBox on {1, 2, 3}, calls shuffle() once, and then calls reset(). What does reset() return? Separately, the loop makes 3 draws from 3 values, so it has 27 equally likely draw sequences. Do the six orders of {1, 2, 3} each receive the same number of those sequences?

reset() returns the shuffled order, because the constructor stores the same array object twice and the shuffle rewrote it. The orders do not share the 27 sequences evenly. Three orders each receive 5 sequences and the other three receive 4, so the original order 1, 2, 3 has probability 4/27 and the order 2, 3, 1 has probability 5/27. Six orders cannot split 27 sequences into equal groups.

Bottleneck

Counting Draws Against Orders

The loop makes n draws of n values each, so it has n^n equally likely draw sequences, and each sequence produces exactly one order. A fair shuffle needs every one of the n! orders to receive the same number of sequences. That needs n! to divide n^n. For every n of 3 or more it does not. The number n - 1 is at least 2 and divides n!. It shares no prime factor with n, so it never divides n^n. The bias is therefore a property of the counting, not of the random number generator, and a better generator cannot repair it. For n = 4 the 256 sequences give some orders 8 sequences and others 15, while a fair split would give each order 10.67.

The enumeration design avoids the bias at a price. It builds n! orders, so it costs O(n * n!) time and O(n * n!) space. The swap loop costs only O(n) time and O(1) extra space, which is the budget a correct design should keep.

The second weak point is the shared array. The class holds one object under two names, so no later call can recover the input. A correct design needs two properties. The number of draw sequences must factor as n * (n - 1) * ... * 1, and the class must keep an array that no shuffle and no caller can touch.

Key idea

Shrinking The Choice Each Step

The Frontier Splits The Array

The frontier is an index i that splits the working array into two parts. Positions 0 to i - 1 hold final values that no later step touches. Positions i to n - 1 hold the values still waiting for a place. Fisher-Yates shuffling moves the frontier from left to right. At each step it draws a position j from i to n - 1, swaps a[i] with a[j], and moves the frontier one step right. The draw ranges over n - i values, so the first draw has n options, the second has n - 1, and the last useful draw has 2.

Why Every Order Is Equally Likely

The product of the draw sizes is n * (n - 1) * ... * 2, and it equals n!. Different draw sequences produce different orders. Two sequences that differ first at step i place different values at position i, and no later step changes that position. So the map from draw sequences to orders is one to one, and both sides have n! members. Each order therefore has probability 1 / n!, and the shuffle is uniform, which means every order has the same probability. A short form of the argument: each value is equally likely to land at position i, whatever the earlier steps chose.

The Snapshot Protects Reset

A snapshot is a private copy of the input that the class never hands out and never shuffles. The class makes the snapshot in the constructor, so a later change to the caller's array cannot reach it. Reset copies the snapshot into the working array, and every array returned to a caller is a fresh copy, so a caller who writes into a returned array changes nothing inside the class.

The invariant of the design is that the working array always holds a permutation of the snapshot, the positions left of the frontier are final, and the snapshot never changes.

State

State And Draw Ranges

The five names below cover the class fields and the loop locals.

  • snapshot is the private copy of the input array, written once in the constructor.
  • work is the working array. It always holds the same multiset of values as snapshot.
  • pick is the injected source of randomness. A call with bound b returns an integer from 0 to b - 1.
  • i is the frontier, from 0 to n - 1. Positions below i are final.
  • j is the position chosen at step i. It equals i + pick.applyAsInt(n - i), so it lies in [i, n - 1].

The array may contain equal values. The argument above counts draw sequences, not distinct-looking arrays, so equal values change how arrays look and never change the counting.

Walkthrough

Tracing Shuffles With Scripted Draws

One Shuffle Of Four Letters

The first trace shuffles K L M N with scripted draws. The pointer i marks the frontier and the pointer j marks the chosen position. At i = 0 the draw has bound 4 and returns 2, so j = 2 and K swaps with M. The frontier moves right. At i = 1 the bound is 3 and the draw returns 0, so j = 1 and the letter L stays in place. A draw may choose the frontier position itself, and that choice is required for fairness. At i = 2 the bound is 2 and the draw returns 1, so j = 3 and K swaps with N. The loop stops because the last position has only one value left. The result is M L N K.

Reset, Then Shuffle Again

The second trace uses a class built on 7 2 9 2 with a repeated value. The first shuffle runs three steps with scripted draws and leaves the working array in a new order, while the snapshot stays 7 2 9 2. The reset step copies the snapshot back into the working array. A second shuffle with different draws then gives a different order. The variables column shows the snapshot, and it has the same value in every step. The pointer j equals i in the steps where the draw keeps the frontier value in place.

Code

Shuffler With Private Snapshot

The Class

The class copies the input twice in its constructor, once for the snapshot and once for the working array. The method permute is the Fisher-Yates loop. The public methods return copies.

final class Shuffler {
    private final int[] snapshot;
    private final int[] work;
    private final java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator pick;

    Shuffler(int[] nums, java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator pick) {
        this.snapshot = nums.clone();   // private copy: the caller's later writes cannot reach it
        this.work = nums.clone();       // second copy: shuffles never touch the snapshot
        this.pick = pick;
    }

    private void permute() {
        int n = work.length;
        for (int i = 0; i + 1 < n; i++) {          // frontier i: the last position needs no draw
            int j = i + pick.applyAsInt(n - i);    // uniform position in [i, n - 1]
            int t = work[i]; work[i] = work[j]; work[j] = t;
        }
    }

    int[] shuffle() { permute(); return work.clone(); }              // fresh copy for the caller

    int[] reset() {
        System.arraycopy(snapshot, 0, work, 0, work.length);        // restore without sharing
        return work.clone();
    }
}

Production Use

In production code the caller passes a java.util.Random method reference, for example new Shuffler(nums, new java.util.Random()::nextInt). The call nextInt(bound) returns a value from 0 to bound - 1 without the bias of a remainder operation. Tests pass a scripted source, so a test can drive the loop through every draw sequence.

Cost

  • shuffle costs O(n) time for the loop and O(n) space for the returned copy.
  • reset costs O(n) time and O(n) space for the same reasons.
  • Constructor costs O(n) time and O(n) space for the two copies.
When to use it

When Fair Shuffling Applies

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when public calls change a collection and later return random output. The words "shuffle", "random order" and "reset" in a statement signal it. Before coding, decide which copy must stay fixed and what the draw ranges are at each step. The invariant to keep is that each valid arrangement has probability 1 / n! under the current state, the snapshot never changes, and every returned array is a copy.

False Friends

A swap with a random position from the whole array is a false friend of Fisher-Yates, because the two loops differ by a single bound, n versus n - i, and only one is fair. A sort with a random comparator is a second false friend. Its result depends on the sorting algorithm, and a comparator that contradicts itself breaks the sort contract. For sampling a single item from a stream whose length is unknown, shuffling is also the wrong tool. Chapter 35 covers reservoir sampling, which keeps one item and proves that each of t items seen so far is held with probability 1 / t.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If the output must resist an adversary, as in a card game with money at stake, java.util.Random is a no-go. Its internal state has only 48 bits, and an observer who sees a few outputs can predict the rest. Use java.security.SecureRandom as the source. If the requested distribution is not uniform, for example weighted choices without repeats, the counting argument above no longer holds and the method needs a different proof.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Fisher-Yates One Step

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Write the method int step(int[] a, int i, IntUnaryOperator pick). The method performs one Fisher-Yates step at frontier i. It calls pick.applyAsInt(a.length - i) exactly once and treats the result k as an offset. It swaps a[i] with a[i + k] in place and returns the value now stored at a[i].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Length of a is from 1 to 10^5, and elements are int values.
  • Frontier i satisfies 0 <= i < a.length.
  • Draw k lies from 0 to a.length - i - 1, and a draw outside that range throws IllegalStateException before any write.
  • Mutation touches only positions i and i + k.

Example 1. Input a = [4, 7, 9], i = 1, draw bound 2 with scripted result 1, output 9, and a becomes [4, 9, 7].

Example 2. Input a = [5, 5, 2], i = 0, draw bound 3 with scripted result 2, output 2, and a becomes [2, 5, 5].

Prerequisites. The frontier and the draw range from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: the draw range shrinks to the unplaced suffix, and the position under the frontier may swap with itself.

Hint

What must the argument to pick be so that the chosen position can equal i itself?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method computes the bound a.length - i, which is the number of values still waiting at and after the frontier. It asks pick for one offset k below that bound, checks the range, and swaps a[i] with a[i + k]. The offset 0 is legal and swaps a position with itself, so the value under the frontier keeps its own chance of staying. The check happens before the swap, so an out-of-range draw leaves the array unchanged. The invariant is that positions below i are never read or written, and the multiset of values never changes.

The program tries every legal offset for random arrays and frontiers, and it compares the result with a model that builds the expected array by copying and rotating a list. It also records the bound that pick receives and checks the error path.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1), because the method makes one draw and one swap.
  • Space is O(1), because the method keeps three local integers.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator;

public final class FisherYatesStepBuild {
    /**
     * Performs one Fisher-Yates step at frontier i.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: positions below i stay untouched and the multiset of values is preserved.
     */
    static int step(int[] a, int i, IntUnaryOperator pick) {
        // The unplaced suffix has a.length - i values, so the draw bound equals that size.
        int bound = a.length - i;
        // One draw per step: the offset selects a position inside the suffix.
        int k = pick.applyAsInt(bound);
        // A bad draw is rejected before any write, so the array stays unchanged.
        if (k < 0 || k >= bound) throw new IllegalStateException("draw out of range: " + k);
        // Offset k = 0 maps j to i and the swap changes nothing, which keeps fairness.
        int j = i + k;
        // Three assignments swap the two positions in place.
        int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[j]; a[j] = t;
        // The frontier position now holds its final value.
        return a[i];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: draw 1 at frontier 1 swaps positions 1 and 2.
        int[] a = {4, 7, 9};
        if (step(a, 1, b -> { if (b != 2) throw new AssertionError("bound"); return 1; }) != 9
                || !java.util.Arrays.equals(a, new int[]{4, 9, 7})) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: draw 2 at frontier 0 swaps the ends.
        int[] c = {5, 5, 2};
        if (step(c, 0, b -> { if (b != 3) throw new AssertionError("bound"); return 2; }) != 2
                || !java.util.Arrays.equals(c, new int[]{2, 5, 5})) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Out-of-range draws throw and leave the array unchanged.
        int[] d = {1, 2, 3};
        for (int bad : new int[]{-1, 3}) {
            boolean threw = false;
            try { step(d, 0, b -> bad); } catch (IllegalStateException e) { threw = true; }
            if (!threw || !java.util.Arrays.equals(d, new int[]{1, 2, 3})) throw new AssertionError("bad draw " + bad);
        }
        // Random arrays: every legal offset gives the model result, and pick is called once.
        Random rnd = new Random(3807L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int[] arr = new int[n];
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) arr[x] = rnd.nextInt(5) - 2;
            int i = rnd.nextInt(n);
            for (int k = 0; k < n - i; k++) {
                // The model removes the chosen value from the suffix list and appends the old frontier value in its slot.
                List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
                for (int x : arr) model.add(x);
                int chosen = model.get(i + k);
                model.set(i + k, model.get(i));
                model.set(i, chosen);
                int[] work = arr.clone();
                int[] calls = {0};
                final int offset = k;
                final int expectBound = n - i;
                int got = step(work, i, b -> { calls[0]++; if (b != expectBound) throw new AssertionError("bound"); return offset; });
                if (calls[0] != 1 || got != chosen) throw new AssertionError("calls or value");
                for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) if (work[x] != model.get(x)) throw new AssertionError("array mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Shuffle An Array

LeetCode 384

Problem Specification. Design the class ShuffleBox with a constructor that receives an int[] nums and an IntUnaryOperator pick. The method shuffle() returns a new array that holds the elements of nums in an order chosen so that every arrangement of the elements has the same probability. The method reset() returns a new array equal to nums as it was at construction time.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Length of nums is from 0 to 50, and elements are int values that may repeat.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Draws come only from pick, and one shuffle() calls it once per frontier from 0 to n - 2.
  • Isolation means a write into a returned array never changes later results.

Example 1. Input nums = [3, 1, 2] with scripted draws 2, 0 for shuffle(), output [2, 1, 3], and then reset() outputs [3, 1, 2].

Example 2. Input nums = [8, 8, 5, 0] with scripted draws 1, 2, 0 for shuffle(), output [8, 0, 5, 8], and then reset() outputs [8, 8, 5, 0].

Prerequisites. The Fisher-Yates One Step exercise above. This exercise repeats the step over the whole array and adds a restore call.

What This Exercise Changes. The single step becomes a loop over a moving frontier. The class also keeps a private snapshot, so no shuffle writes into it and reset can run at any time.

Hint

Which array does each call start from, and which of the arrays that the class holds may the call write into?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The constructor stores a private copy of nums. Each shuffle() call clones that copy into a fresh array and runs the frontier loop on the clone, and each reset() call returns another clone. No call writes into the stored copy and no caller receives it. The loop runs for i from 0 to n - 2, draws i + pick.applyAsInt(n - i) and swaps. The invariant is that the clone holds a permutation of the stored values, with positions below i final.

Fairness follows from counting: the draw sizes n, n - 1, ..., 2 multiply to n!, and different draw sequences give different arrays when all values are distinct. The program checks this claim directly. For lengths 0 to 6 it feeds every draw sequence through a scripted source and requires that each of the n! orders of distinct values appears exactly once. It then runs random draws on arrays with repeated values, checks that each result is a rearrangement of the input, and checks that writes into returned arrays never change later calls.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) per shuffle() and per reset(), because the loop and the clone each touch every position once.
  • Space is O(n) for the stored copy and for each returned array, and the loop adds O(1) extra.
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.HashSet;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.Set;
import java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator;

public final class ShuffleArrayVary {
    static final class ShuffleBox {
        private final int[] stored;
        private final IntUnaryOperator pick;

        ShuffleBox(int[] nums, IntUnaryOperator pick) {
            // A private copy protects the original order from later writes by the caller.
            this.stored = nums.clone();
            this.pick = pick;
        }

        /**
         * Returns a new array that holds the stored values in a random order.
         * Time: O(n). Space: O(n) for the returned array.
         * Invariant: the clone is always a permutation of the stored values, and positions below i are final.
         */
        int[] shuffle() {
            // The clone keeps the stored copy untouched, so reset stays possible.
            int[] out = stored.clone();
            int n = out.length;
            // Frontier i moves right; the last position needs no draw because one value is left.
            for (int i = 0; i + 1 < n; i++) {
                // The draw covers the n - i unplaced positions, including i itself.
                int j = i + pick.applyAsInt(n - i);
                // Swapping places the chosen value at the frontier, and that position is final.
                int t = out[i]; out[i] = out[j]; out[j] = t;
            }
            return out;
        }

        /**
         * Returns a new array equal to the construction-time input.
         * Time: O(n). Space: O(n).
         * Invariant: the stored copy is never written after construction.
         */
        int[] reset() {
            // A clone, not the stored array, so callers cannot write into the original.
            return stored.clone();
        }
    }

    /** Pick source that replays a fixed list of draws and checks each against its bound. */
    static IntUnaryOperator scripted(int... draws) {
        int[] pos = {0};
        return bound -> {
            int d = draws[pos[0]++];
            if (d < 0 || d >= bound) throw new AssertionError("draw " + d + " for bound " + bound);
            return d;
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: draws 2 then 0 turn [3,1,2] into [2,1,3], and reset restores the input.
        ShuffleBox b1 = new ShuffleBox(new int[]{3, 1, 2}, scripted(2, 0));
        if (!Arrays.equals(b1.shuffle(), new int[]{2, 1, 3})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 shuffle");
        if (!Arrays.equals(b1.reset(), new int[]{3, 1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 reset");
        // Example 2: duplicates stay legal and the draw sequence 1,2,0 gives [8,0,5,8].
        ShuffleBox b2 = new ShuffleBox(new int[]{8, 8, 5, 0}, scripted(1, 2, 0));
        if (!Arrays.equals(b2.shuffle(), new int[]{8, 0, 5, 8})) throw new AssertionError("example 2 shuffle");
        if (!Arrays.equals(b2.reset(), new int[]{8, 8, 5, 0})) throw new AssertionError("example 2 reset");
        // Exhaustive check: every draw sequence yields a different order, so all n! orders appear once.
        for (int n = 0; n <= 6; n++) {
            int[] input = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) input[i] = 10 + i;
            Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
            int[] draws = new int[Math.max(0, n - 1)];
            long total = 1;
            for (int i = 0; i < draws.length; i++) total *= (n - i);
            for (long code = 0; code < total || (n <= 1 && code == 0); code++) {
                // Decode the mixed-radix number into one draw per frontier.
                long rest = code;
                for (int i = 0; i < draws.length; i++) { draws[i] = (int) (rest % (n - i)); rest /= (n - i); }
                seen.add(Arrays.toString(new ShuffleBox(input, scripted(draws)).shuffle()));
                if (n <= 1) break;
            }
            long factorial = 1;
            for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) factorial *= i;
            if (seen.size() != factorial) throw new AssertionError("orders for n=" + n + ": " + seen.size());
        }
        // Random draws on arrays with repeats: results are rearrangements and callers cannot corrupt the box.
        Random rnd = new Random(3808L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(10);
            int[] nums = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) nums[i] = rnd.nextInt(4);
            int[] expected = nums.clone();
            ShuffleBox box = new ShuffleBox(nums, rnd::nextInt);
            // A caller write into the constructor argument must not reach the box.
            if (n > 0) nums[0] = 99;
            for (int round = 0; round < 4; round++) {
                int[] s = box.shuffle();
                int[] sortedS = s.clone(); Arrays.sort(sortedS);
                int[] sortedE = expected.clone(); Arrays.sort(sortedE);
                if (!Arrays.equals(sortedS, sortedE)) throw new AssertionError("not a rearrangement");
                // A caller write into a returned array must not reach the box.
                if (s.length > 0) s[0] = -7;
                int[] r = box.reset();
                if (!Arrays.equals(r, expected)) throw new AssertionError("reset changed");
                if (r.length > 0) r[0] = -8;
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated Reset And Shuffle

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class Deck with a constructor that receives an int[] initial and an IntUnaryOperator pick. The method shuffle() applies one Fisher-Yates pass to the current order and returns a copy of the new order. The method current() returns a copy of the current order. The method reset() sets the current order to the initial order and returns a copy of it. The initial order is the content of initial at construction time.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Length of initial is from 0 to 1000, and elements are int values that may repeat.
  • Calls number at most 10^4 per object.
  • Aliasing means a write by the caller into initial after construction, or into any returned array, never changes the object.
  • Draws use pick once per frontier from 0 to n - 2, so lengths 0 and 1 use none.

Example 1. Input initial = [1, 2, 3]. The call shuffle() with draws 1, 1 outputs [2, 3, 1]. The caller writes 99 into that array, and current() outputs [2, 3, 1]. Then reset() outputs [1, 2, 3], and shuffle() with draws 2, 1 outputs [3, 1, 2].

Example 2. Input initial = [4, 4]. The caller sets initial[0] = 9 after construction, and reset() outputs [4, 4]. For initial = [6], shuffle() outputs [6] and calls pick zero times.

Prerequisites. The Shuffle An Array exercise above. This exercise changes one decision: the class keeps one working array across calls, so shuffles continue from the current order.

What This Exercise Changes. Shuffles now compound, because each pass starts from the order the last call left. The new method current() reads that order, so the class must copy on every read and write and keep the initial order apart.

Hint

How many separate arrays does the class need, and which of them does the caller ever receive?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps two private arrays. The array initial is a copy of the constructor argument and never changes. The array order is the current order, and it starts as another copy. The method shuffle() runs the frontier loop on order in place, so the next shuffle continues from the new order, and it returns a clone. The method current() returns a clone of order. The method reset() copies initial back into order with System.arraycopy and returns a clone.

The invariant is that order is always a permutation of initial, and that no reference to either array leaves the class. Continuing from the current order is still fair. The counting argument does not depend on the starting order, because for distinct values different draw sequences still produce different results, so every order is reachable from every other with probability 1 / n!. The lengths 0 and 1 make the loop run zero times, so they call pick zero times.

The program checks aliasing in both directions, runs the lengths 0 and 1, and compares against a model that keeps its own list. For starting orders other than the identity it enumerates every draw sequence and requires all n! results to be distinct.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) per call, because each call runs one loop or one copy over n positions.
  • Space is O(n) for the two private arrays, plus O(n) for each array returned.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.HashSet;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.Set;
import java.util.function.IntUnaryOperator;

public final class RepeatedResetShuffleBoundary {
    static final class Deck {
        private final int[] initial;
        private final int[] order;
        private final IntUnaryOperator pick;

        Deck(int[] start, IntUnaryOperator pick) {
            // Two independent copies: one never changes, one evolves with each shuffle.
            this.initial = start.clone();
            this.order = start.clone();
            this.pick = pick;
        }

        /**
         * Applies one Fisher-Yates pass to the current order.
         * Time: O(n). Space: O(n) for the returned copy.
         * Invariant: order is a permutation of initial, and the pass never touches initial.
         */
        int[] shuffle() {
            int n = order.length;
            // Lengths 0 and 1 skip the loop, so pick is never called for them.
            for (int i = 0; i + 1 < n; i++) {
                // Draw over the unplaced suffix of size n - i.
                int j = i + pick.applyAsInt(n - i);
                // Swap in place; position i is final for this pass.
                int t = order[i]; order[i] = order[j]; order[j] = t;
            }
            // A clone keeps the caller away from the internal array.
            return order.clone();
        }

        /**
         * Returns a copy of the current order.
         * Time: O(n). Space: O(n).
         * Invariant: the call writes nothing.
         */
        int[] current() {
            // Clone so a write by the caller cannot change later shuffles.
            return order.clone();
        }

        /**
         * Restores the initial order and returns a copy of it.
         * Time: O(n). Space: O(n).
         * Invariant: afterwards order equals initial element by element.
         */
        int[] reset() {
            // Copy the values, never the reference, so the two arrays stay separate.
            System.arraycopy(initial, 0, order, 0, order.length);
            return order.clone();
        }
    }

    static IntUnaryOperator scripted(int... draws) {
        int[] pos = {0};
        return bound -> {
            int d = draws[pos[0]++];
            if (d < 0 || d >= bound) throw new AssertionError("draw " + d + " for bound " + bound);
            return d;
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: shuffle, caller write, current, reset, then a second shuffle after reset.
        int[] start = {1, 2, 3};
        Deck d = new Deck(start, scripted(1, 1, 2, 1));
        int[] s = d.shuffle();
        if (!Arrays.equals(s, new int[]{2, 3, 1})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first shuffle");
        s[0] = 99;
        if (!Arrays.equals(d.current(), new int[]{2, 3, 1})) throw new AssertionError("returned array aliased");
        if (!Arrays.equals(d.reset(), new int[]{1, 2, 3})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 reset");
        if (!Arrays.equals(d.shuffle(), new int[]{3, 1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second shuffle");
        // Example 2: a write to the constructor argument does not reach the deck.
        int[] arg = {4, 4};
        Deck e = new Deck(arg, scripted());
        arg[0] = 9;
        if (!Arrays.equals(e.reset(), new int[]{4, 4})) throw new AssertionError("constructor aliasing");
        // Lengths 1 and 0 call pick zero times; the scripted source with no draws would fail on a call.
        if (!Arrays.equals(new Deck(new int[]{6}, scripted()).shuffle(), new int[]{6})) throw new AssertionError("length 1");
        if (new Deck(new int[0], scripted()).shuffle().length != 0) throw new AssertionError("length 0");
        // Exhaustive fairness from a non-identity starting order of distinct values.
        int[] weird = {30, 10, 40, 20, 50};
        int n = weird.length;
        Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
        long total = 1;
        for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) total *= (n - i);
        int[] draws = new int[n - 1];
        for (long code = 0; code < total; code++) {
            long rest = code;
            for (int i = 0; i < draws.length; i++) { draws[i] = (int) (rest % (n - i)); rest /= (n - i); }
            seen.add(Arrays.toString(new Deck(weird, scripted(draws)).shuffle()));
        }
        if (seen.size() != 120) throw new AssertionError("expected 120 distinct orders, got " + seen.size());
        // Random cycles against a model that keeps its own copy of the initial order.
        Random rnd = new Random(3809L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int len = rnd.nextInt(9);
            int[] src = new int[len];
            List<Integer> modelInitial = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) { src[i] = rnd.nextInt(5); modelInitial.add(src[i]); }
            Deck deck = new Deck(src, rnd::nextInt);
            List<Integer> modelCurrent = new ArrayList<>(modelInitial);
            for (int op = 0; op < 12; op++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                int[] got;
                if (kind == 0) {
                    got = deck.shuffle();
                    // The shuffle keeps the multiset; the model adopts the observed order.
                    List<Integer> a = new ArrayList<>(); for (int x : got) a.add(x);
                    List<Integer> b1 = new ArrayList<>(modelCurrent), b2 = new ArrayList<>(a);
                    Collections.sort(b1); Collections.sort(b2);
                    if (!b1.equals(b2)) throw new AssertionError("shuffle changed the multiset");
                    modelCurrent = a;
                } else if (kind == 1) {
                    got = deck.current();
                    if (!Arrays.equals(got, modelCurrent.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray())) throw new AssertionError("current");
                } else {
                    got = deck.reset();
                    modelCurrent = new ArrayList<>(modelInitial);
                    if (!Arrays.equals(got, modelInitial.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray())) throw new AssertionError("reset");
                }
                // Hostile caller: scribble on the returned array and on the original argument.
                if (got.length > 0) got[0] = -5;
                if (len > 0) src[0] = -6;
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Streaming Sample Review

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design the class StreamPicker with a constructor that receives a LongUnaryOperator pick. The method add(int value) records the next value of the stream. The method pick() returns one of the values added so far, chosen so that each added value has the same probability. The class stores a constant number of values and never keeps the whole stream. Each call to add calls pick.applyAsLong(t) once, where t is the number of values added so far including the new one, and a result of 0 replaces the stored value with the new value.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Stream length is from 0 to 10^12 values, so the counter and the draw bound use long.
  • Values are int values that may repeat.
  • Empty stream means pick() throws NoSuchElementException.
  • Memory is O(1) and does not depend on the number of values added.

Example 1. Input calls add(5), add(8), add(3), pick() with scripted draws 0, 1, 0 for the three add calls, output 3.

Example 2. Input calls pick() on a new object, output NoSuchElementException. Then add(7) with scripted draw 0 and pick() output 7.

Prerequisites. The Repeated Reset And Shuffle exercise above. This exercise changes the kind of state: values arrive one at a time and the set grows, so there is no array to shuffle.

What This Exercise Changes. The represented set grows instead of being rearranged. The class keeps one held value and a count, and the draw bound grows with the count.

Hint

What is the probability that the newest of t values replaces the stored one, and what must it be for the newest value to be held with probability 1 / t?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores one held value and a long count seen. Each add increments the count to t, draws from the bound t, which is a long, and replaces the held value when the draw is 0. The probability that value number t replaces the held one is 1 / t. A value that arrived earlier survives each later step s with probability (s - 1) / s. These factors telescope, so after m additions every value is held with probability 1 / m. The invariant is that, given m values added, the held value is each of them with probability 1 / m, and the count equals m.

The program verifies the claim by enumeration, as in the shuffle exercises. For stream lengths 1 to 7 it feeds every combination of draws to a scripted source. A combination is one draw below t for each step t, which gives m! combinations. It requires that every position of the stream is held in exactly (m - 1)! combinations, and that the held value matches a model that records the last step whose draw was 0. It also checks the empty case and a count beyond the int range.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because add makes one draw and pick reads one field.
  • Space is O(1), because the class stores one value and one long, whatever the stream length.
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.function.LongUnaryOperator;

public final class StreamingSampleReviewRecognize {
    static final class StreamPicker {
        private final LongUnaryOperator pick;
        private int held;
        private long seen;

        StreamPicker(LongUnaryOperator pick) { this.pick = pick; }

        /**
         * Records one value and decides whether it replaces the held value.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: after m calls, each of the m values is held with probability 1/m.
         */
        void add(int value) {
            // The count includes the new value, so t is the stream length after this call.
            seen++;
            // Draw 0 out of t outcomes happens with probability 1/t, the share the newcomer needs.
            if (pick.applyAsLong(seen) == 0) held = value;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the held value.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the call writes nothing.
         */
        int pick() {
            // An empty stream has no value to return.
            if (seen == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException();
            return held;
        }

        long count() { return seen; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: draws 0,1,0 keep 5, ignore 8, then replace by 3.
        int[] script = {0, 1, 0};
        int[] at = {0};
        StreamPicker p = new StreamPicker(b -> script[at[0]++]);
        p.add(5); p.add(8); p.add(3);
        if (p.pick() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: an empty picker throws, and the first value is always kept because the bound is 1.
        StreamPicker q = new StreamPicker(b -> { if (b != 1) throw new AssertionError("bound"); return 0; });
        boolean threw = false;
        try { q.pick(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("empty must throw");
        q.add(7);
        if (q.pick() != 7) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Exhaustive fairness: every position is held in exactly (m-1)! of the m! draw combinations.
        for (int m = 1; m <= 7; m++) {
            long total = 1;
            for (int t = 2; t <= m; t++) total *= t;
            long[] heldCount = new long[m];
            for (long code = 0; code < total; code++) {
                // Decode the mixed-radix number: step t uses a digit in [0, t).
                int[] draws = new int[m];
                long rest = code;
                for (int t = 1; t <= m; t++) { draws[t - 1] = (int) (rest % t); rest /= t; }
                int[] pos = {0};
                StreamPicker sp = new StreamPicker(b -> {
                    int d = draws[pos[0]++];
                    if (b != pos[0]) throw new AssertionError("bound must equal the step number");
                    return d;
                });
                for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) sp.add(100 + i);
                // The model: the held value is the one added at the last step whose draw was 0.
                int last = -1;
                for (int t = 1; t <= m; t++) if (draws[t - 1] == 0) last = t - 1;
                if (sp.pick() != 100 + last) throw new AssertionError("model mismatch");
                heldCount[last]++;
            }
            long expected = total / m;
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) if (heldCount[i] != expected) throw new AssertionError("m=" + m + " position " + i);
        }
        // The count is a long: it passes the int range without overflow.
        StreamPicker big = new StreamPicker(b -> 1);
        big.add(1);
        java.lang.reflect.Field f;
        try {
            f = StreamPicker.class.getDeclaredField("seen");
            f.setAccessible(true);
            f.setLong(big, (long) Integer.MAX_VALUE + 5);
        } catch (ReflectiveOperationException ex) { throw new AssertionError(ex); }
        big.add(2);
        if (big.count() != (long) Integer.MAX_VALUE + 6) throw new AssertionError("count width");
        // Random streams against a model with the same scripted draws.
        Random rnd = new Random(3810L);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int len = 1 + rnd.nextInt(30);
            int[] vals = new int[len], dr = new int[len];
            int[] ix = {0};
            StreamPicker sp = new StreamPicker(b -> dr[ix[0]++]);
            int expectedHeld = 0;
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) {
                vals[i] = rnd.nextInt(6);
                dr[i] = rnd.nextInt(i + 1);
                if (dr[i] == 0) expectedHeld = vals[i];
                sp.add(vals[i]);
            }
            if (sp.pick() != expectedHeld || sp.count() != len) throw new AssertionError("random stream");
        }
    }
}verified

Unlocked Combinations

A monitoring service shows the number of requests in the last minute and the median latency of the last thousand calls. The first number needs a queue that drops entries by age. The second needs two ordered halves that stay balanced while old values leave. The two lessons that close this chapter pair those techniques.

These two lessons combine techniques from lessons 1 to 7 of this chapter.

Pairings Taught In This Chapter

Combination Representative problem
Queue + Time Window LeetCode 933 Number of Recent Calls
Ordered Structures + Stream LeetCode 2034 Stock Price Fluctuation

The lesson Queue Time Window states in its contract whether an entry exactly one span old still counts. The lesson Ordered Stream State requires every active value to belong to one validated ordered region. Reservoir and weighted sampling are covered by chapter 35.

Queue Time Window

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why The Alert Fires Minutes Late

A monitoring service pages the on-call engineer when the alert score of the last 60 seconds exceeds 500. Each failure adds a weight to the score, which is 1 for an ordinary failure and larger for a severe one. A developer implements the check in the obvious way. Every failure is appended to a list with its timestamp and weight, and the check runs after each failure and adds up the entries from the last minute. In the first week of testing, the alert fires within a second of the real spike. In production, traffic runs at 20,000 failures per second during an incident. After an hour the list holds 72 million entries, and the process spends more time summing than the next failure takes to arrive. The check falls behind its input, and the page reaches the engineer several minutes after the outage began. The heap also grows until the garbage collector stalls the whole service.

Nothing in the logic is wrong, because every score is correct. The cost grows with the age of the process and not with the length of the window. This lesson asks how to keep exactly the entries of the last span time units, and how to say precisely which entry on the border belongs to the window.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

The lesson Windowed Streams supplied the count window, where a queue holds the latest k values and a running sum changes by one addition and one subtraction per arrival. It also supplied the habit of keeping the sum in a long, so that a long stream of large values cannot wrap around. This lesson keeps that machinery and replaces the question that decides when the front leaves.

The lesson Expiring State supplied the vocabulary of a deadline and of live and ended entries, and it supplied the rule that equality at the deadline needs one documented answer. Entries that leave in the order they arrived can be removed from the front of one sequence without a search. This lesson uses that property for a stream where every entry has the same lifetime.

The new work is the combination. A count window decides by position, so the queue alone is enough. A time window decides by the clock, so each entry must carry its timestamp, and a rule must say which clock value is the current time. The lesson shows that the queue, the running sum and a half-open border together answer every question about the last span time units in constant amortized time.

Simple approach

Keeping Every Event In A List

The direct design stores every event for the life of the process and scans the whole list on each query. Each entry holds a timestamp and a weight, where a weight counts how severe the event is. The score at time now is the sum of the weights of the entries whose timestamp lies inside the last span time units.

final class NaiveScore {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<long[]> events = new java.util.ArrayList<>(); // {time, weight}
    private final long span;

    NaiveScore(long span) { this.span = span; }

    void add(long now, long weight) { events.add(new long[] {now, weight}); }

    long score(long now) {
        long limit = now - span;                    // events at or before this time are too old
        long sum = 0;
        for (long[] e : events) {                   // visits every event ever added
            if (e[0] > limit) sum += e[1];
        }
        return sum;
    }
}

With span = 10, the calls add(2, 5), add(4, 3) and add(9, 2) followed by score(11) return 10, and score(13) returns 5, because the event at time 2 is then 11 units old and the other two are younger than 10. The class is correct on every small test.

Predict first: The service receives 20000 events per second, the span is 60 seconds, and score is called once per event. After one hour of running, how many entries does one call to score visit, how many of them can still count, and how much memory does the list hold?

The list holds every event of the hour, which is 72 million entries. Each call visits all of them, although at most 1.2 million lie inside the last 60 seconds, so about 98 percent of the visits touch entries that can never count again. Every entry is an array object with two longs plus a reference, roughly 40 bytes, so the list needs close to 3 GB. Both the time per call and the memory grow without limit as the process keeps running.

Bottleneck

Work That Grows With The Process Age

Let n be the number of events added so far and w the number that lie inside the window. One call to score costs O(n) time, and the list costs O(n) memory. The useful work is only O(w). For a service that runs for weeks, n is many orders of magnitude larger than w, and the gap widens with every event.

The repeated work has a clear shape. An event that falls out of the window at time t stays out for every later query, because time never moves backward. The naive scan tests the same expired entry again on every call and rejects it again. Deleting expired entries from the list would stop the memory growth, but ArrayList.remove(0) shifts every remaining element, so each removal costs O(w) and the call is still slow.

A correct design meets three requirements. An expired event must be dropped once and never examined again. The sum must update without a rescan. The border must have one exact definition, because an event that sits exactly span units old is either counted or not counted, and two parts of a program that disagree on this produce an off-by-one error that appears only on the tick where timestamps align.

Key idea

Remove From The Front By Age

The Stream Is Expiring

Before choosing a structure, state the kind of stream. Events here are appended in non-decreasing time order, and they are never corrected. Each event expires after the same span, so the stream is expiring and not correctable, and the oldest event is always the next to expire.

Three Parts That Move Together

The arrival queue holds the live events in the order they arrived, each with its timestamp and weight. Because arrival order equals age order, the oldest live event is at the front, and a new event joins at the back. The cutoff is the time now - span. An event is live when its timestamp is strictly greater than the cutoff, so the window is the half-open range from the cutoff, excluded, to now, included. The running sum is the total weight of the arrival queue, kept as a long and adjusted by one addition for each arrival and one subtraction for each removal.

Every operation that receives a time first computes the cutoff. It then removes events from the front while the oldest timestamp is at or before the cutoff, and it subtracts each removed weight from the running sum. The score is then the running sum, with no further scan, because the queue holds exactly the live events. An add performs the same removal first and then appends the new event. The removal on add is what keeps memory bounded even when nobody asks for a score.

Why Equality Is Settled By The Cutoff

The rule "at or before the cutoff leaves" is one comparison, and both operations use it through the same code. An event at time 4 with span = 10 is live at time 13, because 4 is greater than 3. It is gone at time 14, because 4 is not greater than 4. The window therefore always spans exactly span distinct integer times. A program that tested the border in two places, with < in one and <= in another, would count an event in add that score has already rejected. Time arrives as an argument, so a test can drive the clock to the exact border value.

State

Fields Of The Alert Score

The score object stores four fields besides the argument now that each call receives. The caller supplies now, so no method reads the system clock, and a test can choose every timestamp.

  • span is the window length, a long fixed at construction, with a value of at least 1.
  • queue is the arrival queue of {time, weight} pairs, oldest first, and it contains only live events after any call.
  • sum is the running sum of the weights in queue, as a long.
  • last is the latest time passed to any call, used to reject a time that moves backward.

In the traces, the pointer head marks the index of the oldest live event among the cells, and tail marks the index of the newest event added. The queue holds tail - head + 1 events, and an empty queue has head equal to tail + 1.

The invariant is that after every call, queue holds exactly the events with a timestamp greater than last - span, in non-decreasing time order, and sum equals the sum of their weights.

Walkthrough

Tracing Adds And Scores

Eviction On Each Call

The span is 10. Six events arrive over time, and the cells below list them in arrival order. The pointer head marks the index of the oldest live event, and the pointer tail marks the newest event added so far. When the queue is empty, head equals tail + 1. The first three adds all fit inside the window, so head stays at the first cell and the running sum grows to 10. A score at time 11 has the cutoff 1, so nothing leaves. The add at time 12 has the cutoff 2, and the event at time 2 is at or before it, so it leaves before the new event joins. Later calls repeat the pattern. A score at time 20 removes the event at time 9 without any add, and the last add removes the event at time 12.

Border, Equal Times And Bad Input

The second trace uses the span 5. Two events arrive at the same time 3, which the queue stores as separate entries. A score at time 8 has the cutoff 3, so both entries at time 3 are at or before it and leave together. This is the exact border: the events are five units old, and the half-open range excludes them. A late score finds the queue empty, and the sum is 0. An add with a time smaller than the latest time is rejected, and it changes neither the queue nor the sum.

Code

The Alert Score With Injected Time

The Class

The class AlertScore stores each event as a two-element array of time and weight, and it takes the time as an argument. Both public methods call the private method evict, so the border rule exists in one place. The method add rejects a backward time before it changes anything.

An ordinary failure passes weight 1, so the running sum counts failures and the alert fires when score exceeds 500. A severe failure passes a larger weight and moves the score faster. The invariant is that the queue is sorted by time and holds exactly the events inside the half-open window, and that the sum matches it after every call.

final class AlertScore {
    private final long span;
    private final java.util.ArrayDeque<long[]> queue = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    private long sum;
    private long last = Long.MIN_VALUE;

    AlertScore(long span) {
        if (span < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("span must be at least 1");
        this.span = span;
    }

    private void evict(long now) {
        if (now < last) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time moved backward");   // checked before any change
        last = now;
        long cutoff = now - span;                                  // an event at or before this time is too old
        while (!queue.isEmpty() && queue.peekFirst()[0] <= cutoff) {
            sum -= queue.pollFirst()[1];                           // each event leaves once
        }
    }

    void add(long now, long weight) {
        evict(now);                                                // drop expired events first
        queue.addLast(new long[] {now, weight});                   // the newest event is the youngest
        sum += weight;
    }

    long score(long now) {
        evict(now);                                                // a quiet stream still expires
        return sum;
    }
}

Array With A Head Index

The Counts Over Several Window Lengths exercise needs a binary search over the live events. An ArrayDeque has no indexed access, so that exercise keeps an array and a head index. The live events sit in positions head to tail - 1, and eviction only moves head forward.

final class IndexedWindow {
    private long[] times = new long[16];
    private int head, tail;                              // live events occupy [head, tail)

    void record(long now, long maxSpan) {
        while (head < tail && times[head] <= now - maxSpan) head++;   // evict by the widest border
        if (tail == times.length) {                                   // compact the live part to the front
            times = java.util.Arrays.copyOfRange(times, head, head + Math.max(16, 2 * (tail - head)));
            tail -= head; head = 0;
        }
        times[tail++] = now;
    }

    int count(long now, long span) {
        int lo = head, hi = tail;                        // search the first live event newer than now - span
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            if (times[mid] <= now - span) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
        }
        return tail - lo;                                // a narrower window is a suffix of the live part
    }
}

Cost

  • add costs O(1) amortized time, because each event is appended once and removed once.
  • score costs O(1) amortized time, and a single call that removes many events pays for them all at once.
  • Memory is O(w) for the w events inside the window, and it does not depend on the age of the process.
When to use it

When A Queue Fits A Time Window

Recognizing The Pattern

Use this method when every event has the same lifetime, events arrive in non-decreasing time order, and the answer is a sum, a count or an average over the live events. Check three points before coding. State whether the border is inclusive or exclusive, and write it once in the code. Decide where time comes from, and pass it as an argument. Decide what happens to an entry when the stream is corrected. If the lifetimes differ by entry, or an entry can renew, the expiry order no longer matches the arrival order, and the exercises below show how to keep a second order for it. The invariant to protect in all variants is that the structure holds exactly the live entries and the running aggregate matches them.

False Friends

A cleanup task that runs every second looks like eviction, and it is a false friend. Between two runs, a score still includes events that expired, so the answer depends on when the task last ran. A second false friend is a window by event count used for a time question. The last 100 events cover one second at peak traffic and an hour at night, so the count cannot tell how recent they are.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If events can arrive out of time order, the front of the queue is no longer the oldest event, and the method gives wrong counts. The input then needs a buffer that waits for stragglers, or a sorted structure keyed by time. If the question asks for the maximum or the median of the live events, a sum cannot be adjusted by subtraction, and a monotonic deque or two ordered halves is needed instead.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Smoothed Reading With Two Limits

LeetCode 346

Problem Specification. Design the class CappedAverage with a constructor that receives an int called maxCount and a long called span. The method next(time, value) records one reading and returns a double. The returned number is the average of the readings that are among the latest maxCount readings and also have a timestamp greater than time - span. The new reading always counts. The stream is expiring, and a reading leaves the window by either rule.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • maxCount is an int from 1 to 100000, and span is a long from 1 to 10^12.
  • time is a long from 0 to 10^15 that does not decrease, and equal times are allowed.
  • value is an int from -10^9 to 10^9, and calls number at most 100000.
  • Decreasing time throws IllegalArgumentException and changes nothing.

Example 1. Input: with maxCount = 3 and span = 10, calls next(1,4), next(3,8), next(5,6), next(6,10), next(20,2), next(21,7); output [4.0, 6.0, 6.0, 8.0, 2.0, 4.5].

Example 2. Input: with maxCount = 2 and span = 5, calls next(4,1000000000), next(4,1000000000), next(4,7), next(9,3), next(9,5); output [1000000000.0, 1000000000.0, 500000003.5, 3.0, 4.0].

Prerequisites. The count window from the lesson Windowed Streams and the cutoff from this lesson. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 346: the average also drops readings that are too old.

What This Exercise Changes. The single size condition of a count window becomes two conditions in one eviction loop. The sum must be a long, because 10^5 values of 10^9 exceed the int range.

Hint

Which end of the queue does each of the two limits remove from, and can one loop test both conditions?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The window is the intersection of two bounds, so the queue needs two eviction conditions and the running sum needs one update rule. Each call first checks that the time did not move backward, then appends the new reading and adds its value to the long sum. The new reading has time > time - span, so the age test never removes it, and maxCount >= 1 means the size test never removes it either. A loop then removes from the front while the queue holds more than maxCount readings or the oldest reading is at or before time - span, and each removal subtracts its value from the sum. The average is the sum divided by the queue size.

The invariant is that the queue holds exactly the readings among the latest maxCount whose time is greater than time - span, and the sum equals their values. Both bounds remove from the front only, because readings arrive in time order, so the oldest reading is always the first candidate for either rule. The oracle recomputes the window from the full history on each call and compares the result exactly, since the sum and the count are the same integers.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because each reading enters the queue once and leaves it once.
  • Space is O(min(maxCount, readings inside the span)), the size of the queue at its peak.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class CappedAverageBuild {
    static final class CappedAverage {
        private final int maxCount;
        private final long span;
        private final ArrayDeque<long[]> window = new ArrayDeque<>(); // entries {time, value}, oldest first
        private long sum;                                             // long: 10^5 values of 10^9 overflow int
        private long last = Long.MIN_VALUE;                           // latest time seen

        CappedAverage(int maxCount, long span) {
            this.maxCount = maxCount;
            this.span = span;
        }

        /**
         * Records a reading and returns the average over the bounded window.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(window size).
         * Invariant: window holds the latest maxCount readings with time greater than time - span.
         */
        double next(long time, int value) {
