            // Time may repeat but may not decrease, or the front would not be the oldest reading.
            if (time < last) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time moved backward");
            last = time;
            // Append the new reading and add it to the sum in one step.
            window.addLast(new long[] {time, value});
            sum += value;
            long cutoff = time - span; // a reading with time <= cutoff is too old
            // Remove from the front while either bound is broken; the new reading is never removed.
            while (window.size() > maxCount || window.peekFirst()[0] <= cutoff) {
                // Each removal subtracts the reading's value from the sum.
                sum -= window.pollFirst()[1];
            }
            // Exact integer sum over a positive count.
            return (double) sum / window.size();
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: rescans the full history for the latest maxCount readings inside the span. */
    static double oracle(List<long[]> history, int maxCount, long span) {
        long time = history.get(history.size() - 1)[0];
        long sum = 0;
        int count = 0;
        // Only the latest maxCount history entries can qualify.
        for (int i = Math.max(0, history.size() - maxCount); i < history.size(); i++) {
            // The age rule keeps time greater than time - span.
            if (history.get(i)[0] > time - span) {
                sum += history.get(i)[1];
                count++;
            }
        }
        return (double) sum / count;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: both bounds act, the count bound at 5 and the age bound at 20 and 21.
        CappedAverage a = new CappedAverage(3, 10);
        long[][] r1 = {{1, 4}, {3, 8}, {5, 6}, {6, 10}, {20, 2}, {21, 7}};
        double[] e1 = {4.0, 6.0, 6.0, 8.0, 2.0, 4.5};
        for (int i = 0; i < r1.length; i++) {
            if (a.next(r1[i][0], (int) r1[i][1]) != e1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 step " + i);
        }
        // Example 2: equal times, values near the int limit, and a sum above Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        CappedAverage b = new CappedAverage(2, 5);
        long[][] r2 = {{4, 1_000_000_000}, {4, 1_000_000_000}, {4, 7}, {9, 3}, {9, 5}};
        double[] e2 = {1_000_000_000.0, 1_000_000_000.0, 500_000_003.5, 3.0, 4.0};
        for (int i = 0; i < r2.length; i++) {
            if (b.next(r2[i][0], (int) r2[i][1]) != e2[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 2 step " + i);
        }
        // A backward time is rejected and the structure is unchanged.
        boolean threw = false;
        try { b.next(8, 1); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || b.next(9, 5) != 5.0) throw new AssertionError("backward time must leave the window unchanged");
        // Random streams against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(38);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            int maxCount = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            long span = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            CappedAverage c = new CappedAverage(maxCount, span);
            List<long[]> history = new ArrayList<>();
            long time = rnd.nextInt(5);
            for (int step = 0; step < 40; step++) {
                time += rnd.nextInt(4); // zero gaps give equal times
                int value = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 1_000_000_000 - rnd.nextInt(3) : rnd.nextInt(41) - 20; // some values near the int limit
                history.add(new long[] {time, value});
                if (c.next(time, value) != oracle(history, maxCount, span)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Counts Over Several Window Lengths

LeetCode 933

Problem Specification. Design the class MultiSpanCounter with a constructor that receives a long called maxSpan. The method record(time) stores one event. The method count(time, span) returns the number of recorded events with a timestamp greater than time - span, for a span from 1 to maxSpan. Every call carries the current time, and the times of all calls do not decrease.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • maxSpan is a long from 1 to 10^9, and span is a long from 1 to maxSpan.
  • time is a long from 0 to 10^15, and calls number at most 100000.
  • Invalid input means a span outside its range or a time that decreases throws IllegalArgumentException.
  • Memory must depend on the events inside the widest window, not on the history.

Example 1. Input: with maxSpan = 10, calls record(1), record(2), record(5), record(5), count(6,3), count(6,10), record(9), count(12,4), count(12,10), count(20,10); output [null, null, null, null, 2, 4, null, 1, 3, 0].

Example 2. Input: with maxSpan = 4, calls count(3,4), record(3), record(3), count(3,1), count(7,4), count(7,1); output [0, null, null, 2, 0, 0].

Prerequisites. The Smoothed Reading exercise above. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 933: each query chooses its own window length, so the eviction border and the query border differ.

What This Exercise Changes. Eviction uses the widest cutoff and never a query's cutoff, because a later query may be wider than an earlier one. A narrower query counts a suffix of the live events, found by binary search.

Hint

Which cutoff may the structure use to drop events for good, and how can a narrower query find its first live event without scanning? Binary search needs indexed access, so keep an array and a head index.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stored list is bounded by the largest span a caller may ask for, maxSpan, so eviction uses that fixed bound and never a query's span. Every call first moves the clock to time and advances a head index past the entries with entry <= time - maxSpan. These entries cannot belong to any permitted query again, because later calls have a time that is at least as large. The list between the head and the end is sorted, so a count call finds the first entry greater than time - span by binary search and returns the number of entries from that position to the end. Equal times occupy separate entries, and the search for "first greater" places the boundary after all of them when they sit exactly on the cutoff.

The invariant is that every entry at or after the head is inside the widest window, and the entries are in non-decreasing order, so the entries inside any narrower window form a suffix of the live part. Removing entries by moving a head index avoids shifting the list. The program compacts the list when the head passes half its length, which keeps memory proportional to the live entries.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log w) per count call for w live entries, and O(1) amortized for record and for eviction.
  • Space is O(w), because compaction runs whenever dead entries outnumber live ones.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class MultiSpanCounterVary {
    static final class MultiSpanCounter {
        private final long maxSpan;
        private final ArrayList<Long> times = new ArrayList<>(); // non-decreasing event times
        private int head;                                         // first entry that can still be counted
        private long last = Long.MIN_VALUE;                       // latest time seen by any call

        MultiSpanCounter(long maxSpan) { this.maxSpan = maxSpan; }

        /**
         * Moves the clock to time and drops entries outside the widest window.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(live entries) after compaction.
         * Invariant: every entry at or after head has a time greater than time - maxSpan.
         */
        private void advance(long time) {
            // A smaller time would break the sorted order that binary search relies on.
            if (time < last) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time moved backward");
            last = time;
            // Skip entries at or before the widest cutoff; no later query can count them.
            while (head < times.size() && times.get(head) <= time - maxSpan) head++;
            // Compact when dead entries are at least half the list, so memory tracks live entries.
            if (head * 2 > times.size()) {
                times.subList(0, head).clear();
                head = 0;
            }
        }

        /**
         * Records one event at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per event.
         * Invariant: the new entry is the largest, so the list stays sorted.
         */
        void record(long time) {
            advance(time);
            times.add(time);
        }

        /**
         * Counts events with a time greater than time - span.
         * Time: O(log w). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the counted events are exactly the suffix after the cutoff.
         */
        int count(long time, long span) {
            // A span above maxSpan would need entries that were already dropped.
            if (span < 1 || span > maxSpan) throw new IllegalArgumentException("span out of range");
            advance(time);
            long cutoff = time - span;
            int lo = head, hi = times.size(); // search the live part for the first entry > cutoff
            // Binary search: the loop keeps all entries before lo at or below the cutoff.
            while (lo < hi) {
                int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
                if (times.get(mid) <= cutoff) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
            }
            // Everything from lo to the end lies inside the window.
            return times.size() - lo;
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: keeps every event and filters on each query. */
    static int oracle(List<Long> all, long time, long span) {
        int n = 0;
        // The window is half-open: a time equal to time - span is outside.
        for (long t : all) if (t > time - span) n++;
        return n;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: one stream, three different query spans, an event exactly on a cutoff.
        MultiSpanCounter a = new MultiSpanCounter(10);
        a.record(1); a.record(2); a.record(5); a.record(5);
        int[] got = new int[5];
        got[0] = a.count(6, 3);   // 5, 5 are inside (3, 6]
        got[1] = a.count(6, 10);  // 1, 2, 5, 5 are inside (-4, 6]
        a.record(9);
        got[2] = a.count(12, 4);  // only 9 is inside (8, 12]
        got[3] = a.count(12, 10); // 5, 5, 9 are inside (2, 12]; the event at 2 is on the cutoff
        got[4] = a.count(20, 10); // everything is at or before 10
        int[] e1 = {2, 4, 1, 3, 0};
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) if (got[i] != e1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 query " + i);
        // Example 2: an empty counter, then equal times, then a span of 1.
        MultiSpanCounter b = new MultiSpanCounter(4);
        if (b.count(3, 4) != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        b.record(3); b.record(3);
        if (b.count(3, 1) != 2 || b.count(7, 4) != 0 || b.count(7, 1) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Out-of-range span and backward time are rejected.
        boolean s = false, t = false;
        try { b.count(8, 5); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { s = true; }
        try { b.record(2); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { t = true; }
        if (!s || !t) throw new AssertionError("argument checks");
        // Random operation mixes against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(933);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            long maxSpan = 1 + rnd.nextInt(15);
            MultiSpanCounter c = new MultiSpanCounter(maxSpan);
            List<Long> all = new ArrayList<>();
            long time = rnd.nextInt(4);
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                time += rnd.nextInt(4); // zero gaps give equal times
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    c.record(time);
                    all.add(time);
                } else {
                    long span = 1 + rnd.nextInt((int) maxSpan);
                    if (c.count(time, span) != oracle(all, time, span)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Correct The Newest Reading

LeetCode 362

Problem Specification. Design the class ByteMeter with a constructor that receives a long called span. The method add(time, bytes) appends an entry. The method total(time) returns the sum of bytes over the entries with a timestamp greater than time - span. The method amend(bytes) replaces the byte count of the most recently added entry. It throws IllegalStateException when no entry was added, or when that entry is outside the window at the latest time given to add or total.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • span is a long from 1 to 10^12, and bytes is a long from 0 to 10^12.
  • time is a long from 0 to 10^15 that does not decrease across add and total, with equal times allowed.
  • Calls number at most 100000, and totals can exceed the int range.
  • amend takes no time and does not move the clock.

Example 1. Input: with span = 5, calls amend(1), add(3,10), total(7), amend(4), total(8), amend(9), add(8,2), add(8,0), amend(5), total(8); output ["IllegalStateException", null, 10, null, 0, "IllegalStateException", null, null, null, 7].

Example 2. Input: with span = 10, calls add(2,50), add(6,30), total(8), amend(45), total(8), total(12), amend(99), total(16), amend(1); output [null, null, 80, null, 95, 45, null, 0, "IllegalStateException"].

Prerequisites. The Counts Over Several Window Lengths exercise above. The stream is now correctable at its newest entry, and the exact border decides whether a correction is allowed. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 362: events carry a weight, and the newest one can be replaced.

What This Exercise Changes. The queue gains one more operation, a replacement at the back, and the running sum changes by the difference. The border now decides a correction as well as a count.

Hint

Which element of the queue holds the newest entry, and what does an empty queue say about whether that entry is still live?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stream is correctable at its newest entry only, so the queue of entries stays in time order and the correction touches the last element. The meter keeps a queue of {time, bytes} entries, a long total, and seen, the latest time given to add or total. Every call that carries a time first checks that it does not go backward, sets seen and removes from the front the entries with time <= seen - span, which is the half-open rule: an entry exactly span old is outside. An add then appends and increases the total.

The method amend needs no time argument. The newest entry is the last element of the queue, and front eviction removes it only after every older entry. If the queue is empty, the newest entry has expired, or nothing was added, and amend throws IllegalStateException. Otherwise the method replaces the stored bytes and changes the total by the difference between new and old. The invariant is that the total equals the bytes of the queue, and the queue equals the entries inside the window at seen. Because amend does not move the clock, an entry that total has already evicted cannot come back.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized for add, total and amend, since each entry is added and removed once and a correction touches one element.
  • Space is O(entries inside the window).
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ByteMeterBoundary {
    static final class ByteMeter {
        private final long span;
        private final ArrayDeque<long[]> queue = new ArrayDeque<>(); // entries {time, bytes}, oldest first
        private long sum;                                            // bytes inside the window, as long
        private long seen = Long.MIN_VALUE;                          // latest time given to add or total

        ByteMeter(long span) { this.span = span; }

        /**
         * Moves the clock and evicts entries at or before seen - span.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(window).
         * Invariant: the queue holds exactly the entries inside (seen - span, seen].
         */
        private void advance(long time) {
            // Time repeats are fine; a decrease would make the front stop being the oldest entry.
            if (time < seen) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time moved backward");
            seen = time;
            // Half-open window: an entry exactly span old is outside.
            while (!queue.isEmpty() && queue.peekFirst()[0] <= time - span) sum -= queue.pollFirst()[1];
        }

        /**
         * Adds an entry at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per entry.
         * Invariant: the new entry is the newest and sits at the back.
         */
        void add(long time, long bytes) {
            advance(time);
            queue.addLast(new long[] {time, bytes});
            sum += bytes;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the bytes inside the window that ends at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the returned value equals the sum over the queue.
         */
        long total(long time) {
            advance(time);
            return sum;
        }

        /**
         * Replaces the bytes of the newest entry.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: sum changes by exactly the difference, and the clock does not move.
         */
        void amend(long bytes) {
            // An empty queue means no entry was added, or the newest one already left the window.
            if (queue.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("newest entry is not live");
            long[] newest = queue.peekLast(); // the newest entry is always the last one
            sum += bytes - newest[1];
            newest[1] = bytes;
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: keeps every entry and the latest seen time, and filters on demand. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final long span;
        final List<long[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
        long seen = Long.MIN_VALUE;
        Oracle(long span) { this.span = span; }
        void add(long t, long b) { seen = t; all.add(new long[] {t, b}); }
        long total(long t) {
            seen = t;
            long s = 0;
            for (long[] e : all) if (e[0] > t - span) s += e[1];
            return s;
        }
        boolean amend(long b) {
            // Live means the newest entry is inside the window at the latest seen time.
            if (all.isEmpty() || all.get(all.size() - 1)[0] <= seen - span) return false;
            all.get(all.size() - 1)[1] = b;
            return true;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a correction while live, one after the entry expired through total.
        ByteMeter m = new ByteMeter(5);
        boolean first = false;
        try { m.amend(1); } catch (IllegalStateException e) { first = true; }
        m.add(3, 10);
        if (!first || m.total(7) != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 1 start");
        m.amend(4);
        if (m.total(8) != 0) throw new AssertionError("exactly span old is outside");
        boolean second = false;
        try { m.amend(9); } catch (IllegalStateException e) { second = true; }
        if (!second) throw new AssertionError("expired entry must not be amendable");
        m.add(8, 2); m.add(8, 0); m.amend(5);
        if (m.total(8) != 7) throw new AssertionError("equal times, newest only");
        // Example 2: corrections move the total, and the final amend sees an evicted entry.
        ByteMeter n = new ByteMeter(10);
        n.add(2, 50); n.add(6, 30);
        if (n.total(8) != 80) throw new AssertionError("before amend");
        n.amend(45);
        if (n.total(8) != 95 || n.total(12) != 45) throw new AssertionError("after amend and eviction");
        n.amend(99);
        if (n.total(16) != 0) throw new AssertionError("window empties");
        boolean third = false;
        try { n.amend(1); } catch (IllegalStateException e) { third = true; }
        if (!third) throw new AssertionError("third");
        // Large values stay exact in long.
        ByteMeter big = new ByteMeter(100);
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) big.add(i, 1_000_000_000_000L);
        if (big.total(4) != 5_000_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("long total");
        // Random operation mixes against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(362);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            long span = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            ByteMeter a = new ByteMeter(span);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(span);
            long time = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int step = 0; step < 50; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                time += rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    long b = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    a.add(time, b);
                    o.add(time, b);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (a.total(time) != o.total(time)) throw new AssertionError("total, trial " + trial);
                } else {
                    long b = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    boolean ok = true;
                    try { a.amend(b); } catch (IllegalStateException e) { ok = false; }
                    if (ok != o.amend(b)) throw new AssertionError("amend, trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Sessions With Idle And Hard Expiry

LeetCode 1797

Problem Specification. Design the class SessionStore with a constructor that receives two long values, idle and maxLife. The method open(key, time) starts a session for a String key and replaces any earlier session of that key. The method renew(key, time) returns true and records the touch if the session is live, and returns false and changes nothing otherwise. The method liveCount(time) returns the number of live sessions. A session started at s and last touched at l is live at time when time - l < idle and time - s < maxLife.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • idle and maxLife are long values from 1 to 10^12.
  • time is a long from 0 to 10^15 that does not decrease across all calls.
  • key is a nonempty String, and calls number at most 100000.
  • Renewal changes the last touch and never the start.

Example 1. Input: with idle = 5 and maxLife = 12, calls open("a",0), open("b",2), renew("a",4), liveCount(4), renew("a",8), renew("b",8), liveCount(8), renew("a",12), liveCount(12), renew("a",12); output [null, null, true, 2, true, false, 1, false, 0, false].

Example 2. Input: with idle = 3 and maxLife = 3, calls open("x",1), renew("x",3), liveCount(3), renew("x",4), open("x",4), liveCount(6), liveCount(7); output [null, true, 1, false, null, 1, 0].

Prerequisites. The Correct The Newest Reading exercise above and the expiring entries from the lesson Expiring State. A renewal moves an entry in one order and leaves it in another. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 1797: a session ends by idleness or by a maximum age, whichever comes first.

What This Exercise Changes. One queue becomes two ordered collections over the same keys, one by last touch and one by start. A key that fails either rule leaves both.

Hint

A renewal changes the order of expiry by idleness. Which second order keeps the expiry by age sorted? Two insertion-ordered maps over the same keys give one order by last touch and one by start.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The two expiry rules sort the sessions differently, so one queue cannot serve both. The idle rule depends on the last touch, and the hard rule depends on the start. The store therefore keeps two insertion-ordered maps over the same keys. The map byTouch holds each key's last touch and moves a key to the back on every renewal by removing and putting it again. The map byStart holds each key's start and never reorders. Both maps are sorted ascending by their value, because time never decreases.

Every call first sweeps. It removes keys from the front of byTouch while time - touch >= idle, and from the front of byStart while time - start >= maxLife. Each removal deletes the key from both maps, so a key that fails either rule disappears entirely. After the sweep, every remaining session satisfies both rules, because the front of each map holds its minimum value. renew then succeeds exactly when the key is present. open removes any old entry for the key and inserts a fresh session. The live count is the map size.

The invariant is that after a sweep, the two maps hold the same key set, which is exactly the set of live sessions, and each map is sorted by its own value.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because each session is inserted once per open and removed once, and a renewal is one removal and one insertion in a hash-based linked map.
  • Space is O(live sessions) for each of the two maps.
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.LinkedHashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SessionStoreRecognize {
    static final class SessionStore {
        private final long idle;
        private final long maxLife;
        private final LinkedHashMap<String, Long> byTouch = new LinkedHashMap<>(); // key to last touch, oldest touch first
        private final LinkedHashMap<String, Long> byStart = new LinkedHashMap<>(); // key to start, oldest start first
        private long last = Long.MIN_VALUE;                                         // latest time seen

        SessionStore(long idle, long maxLife) {
            this.idle = idle;
            this.maxLife = maxLife;
        }

        /**
         * Removes every session that fails the idle rule or the hard rule at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterward both maps hold the same keys, all of them live.
         */
        private void sweep(long time) {
            // Time never decreases, which keeps both maps sorted by their own value.
            if (time < last) throw new IllegalArgumentException("time moved backward");
            last = time;
            // Idle rule: the front of byTouch has the oldest touch, so stop at the first live one.
            for (Iterator<Map.Entry<String, Long>> it = byTouch.entrySet().iterator();
                 it.hasNext(); ) {
                Map.Entry<String, Long> e = it.next();
                if (time - e.getValue() < idle) break; // this and every later session passes the idle rule
                it.remove();                           // expired by idleness
                byStart.remove(e.getKey());            // keep the two maps on one key set
            }
            // Hard rule: the front of byStart has the oldest start.
            for (Iterator<Map.Entry<String, Long>> it = byStart.entrySet().iterator();
                 it.hasNext(); ) {
                Map.Entry<String, Long> e = it.next();
                if (time - e.getValue() < maxLife) break; // this and every later session passes the hard rule
                it.remove();                              // expired by age
                byTouch.remove(e.getKey());               // keep the two maps on one key set
            }
        }

        /**
         * Starts a session for key, replacing any earlier one.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per session.
         * Invariant: the new session sits at the back of both maps.
         */
        void open(String key, long time) {
            sweep(time);
            // Remove first, so a re-inserted key moves to the back instead of keeping its old place.
            byTouch.remove(key);
            byStart.remove(key);
            byTouch.put(key, time);
            byStart.put(key, time);
        }

        /**
         * Renews the session if it is live; returns whether it was.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a renewal changes the touch order only, never the start order.
         */
        boolean renew(String key, long time) {
            sweep(time);
            // After the sweep, presence in byTouch means the session is live.
            if (!byTouch.containsKey(key)) return false;
            byTouch.remove(key);       // leave the old position
            byTouch.put(key, time);    // re-enter at the back with the new touch
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Counts live sessions at the given time.
         * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the size equals the number of live sessions after the sweep.
         */
        int liveCount(long time) {
            sweep(time);
            return byTouch.size();
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: keeps every session and tests the two rules directly. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final long idle, maxLife;
        final Map<String, long[]> sessions = new HashMap<>(); // key to {start, touch}
        Oracle(long idle, long maxLife) { this.idle = idle; this.maxLife = maxLife; }
        boolean live(long[] s, long t) { return t - s[1] < idle && t - s[0] < maxLife; }
        void open(String k, long t) { sessions.put(k, new long[] {t, t}); }
        boolean renew(String k, long t) {
            long[] s = sessions.get(k);
            if (s == null || !live(s, t)) return false;
            s[1] = t;
            return true;
        }
        int count(long t) {
            int n = 0;
            for (long[] s : sessions.values()) if (live(s, t)) n++;
            return n;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the hard limit ends a session that keeps renewing within the idle limit.
        SessionStore a = new SessionStore(5, 12);
        a.open("a", 0); a.open("b", 2);
        boolean r1 = a.renew("a", 4);
        int c1 = a.liveCount(4);
        boolean r2 = a.renew("a", 8), r3 = a.renew("b", 8);
        int c2 = a.liveCount(8);
        boolean r4 = a.renew("a", 12);
        int c3 = a.liveCount(12);
        boolean r5 = a.renew("a", 12);
        if (!r1 || c1 != 2 || !r2 || r3 || c2 != 1 || r4 || c3 != 0 || r5) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: both limits equal, and a replaced session starts a new life.
        SessionStore b = new SessionStore(3, 3);
        b.open("x", 1);
        boolean q1 = b.renew("x", 3);
        int d1 = b.liveCount(3);
        boolean q2 = b.renew("x", 4);
        b.open("x", 4);
        int d2 = b.liveCount(6), d3 = b.liveCount(7);
        if (!q1 || d1 != 1 || q2 || d2 != 1 || d3 != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Random operation mixes against the oracle, with few keys so renewals and replacements collide.
        Random rnd = new Random(1797);
        String[] keys = {"a", "b", "c", "d", "e"};
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 4000; trial++) {
            long idle = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6), maxLife = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            SessionStore s = new SessionStore(idle, maxLife);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(idle, maxLife);
            long time = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                time += rnd.nextInt(3); // zero gaps give equal times
                String k = keys[rnd.nextInt(keys.length)];
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    s.open(k, time);
                    o.open(k, time);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (s.renew(k, time) != o.renew(k, time)) throw new AssertionError("renew, trial " + trial);
                } else {
                    if (s.liveCount(time) != o.count(time)) throw new AssertionError("count, trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Ordered Stream State

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why The Daily High Never Went Down

A market dashboard shows the highest price of each instrument for the current day. The service keeps every price in a max-heap, and the root is the answer. At 10:02 a feed sends a tick with price 9100 for an instrument that trades near 910. At 10:05 the exchange sends a correction for that timestamp with price 910. The service writes the new price into its table of timestamps and prices, but the old tick still sits inside the heap. The dashboard shows 9100 as the daily high until the service restarts.

Nothing crashed, and the heap did exactly what a heap does. The bug lies in the contract. The stream is correctable, and the structure fits only a stream that grows. The question of this lesson is how to keep an ordered answer correct. The answer can be a maximum, a k-th largest value or a median, and values arrive, change, disappear and expire.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

The heap lessons supplied the bounded heap. A min-heap of size k keeps the k largest values, and its root is the k-th largest. The lessons on ordered sets supplied the count map, where a TreeMap from value to count gives the smallest and the largest value in O(log n). The design chapter supplied the habit of keeping one table that holds the truth about every key, so that a second structure is only an index over it. The first lessons of this chapter supplied the window queue, where the oldest value expires from the front. They also supplied the rule that an API states its time and its inclusive edges.

This lesson joins them. The new work is a way to decide which structure fits each kind of stream. An append-only fixed-rank stream needs a bounded heap. A correctable stream needs an index that can be wrong in a controlled way. An expiring stream needs both a queue of arrival order and an ordered split of the live values. The Java rule that goes with all three is simple: PriorityQueue.remove(Object) scans the whole heap, so a hot path never calls it.

Simple approach

Keep Everything And Sort On Demand

The direct design stores every value in a list and sorts a copy whenever a question arrives. A correction overwrites the stored value, so the list is always the truth. The class below answers the k-th largest value and the maximum.

final class SortingBoard {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> values = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    // Appends a value to the stream.
    void add(long v) { values.add(v); }

    // Replaces the value at a position after a correction.
    void correct(int index, long v) { values.set(index, v); }

    // Returns the k-th largest value, or null when fewer than k values exist.
    Long kth(int k) {
    if (values.size() < k) return null;
    java.util.ArrayList<Long> copy = new java.util.ArrayList<>(values);
    copy.sort(java.util.Collections.reverseOrder());
    return copy.get(k - 1);
    }
}

Every answer is right, including the answers after corrections, and a test with ten values passes at once. The list also serves as a trusted reference for faster designs later in this lesson.

Predict first: A feed adds 100000 values, and a user asks for the 3rd largest after each addition. About how many comparisons does the sorting design perform in total?

Each question sorts a copy of m values, which costs about m log m comparisons. The sum over m from 1 to 100000 is about n squared over 2 times log n, which is roughly 8 times 10^10 comparisons. The service falls behind within seconds, and almost all of the work repeats an order that did not change since the previous question.

Bottleneck

Re-Sorting Values That Did Not Move

Each question pays O(n log n) to rebuild an order of which almost every pair was already known. After one new value arrives, the order of the old values stays the same, and only the place of the new value is open. A stream of n values with a question after each value costs O(n^2 log n) in total, and the copy of the list adds O(n) memory traffic per question.

A faster design has to answer from a structure that changes a little per event. Three kinds of event change the structure in different ways.

An append adds one value. A correction replaces one value, so an old value must stop counting somewhere inside the structure. An expiry removes the oldest value, and the oldest value can sit anywhere in the order. A binary heap supports the first event in O(log n) and has no fast way to find a value inside it. A call to PriorityQueue.remove(Object) takes O(n) time, so n corrections cost O(n^2). A design is therefore good only if it meets two conditions. First, every answer comes from the roots of a few heaps or from the ends of an ordered map. Second, a correction or an expiry costs O(log n) amortized and never searches the inside of a heap.

Key idea

Split The Values And Check The Roots

Say Which Kind Of Stream It Is

State the stream kind before choosing a structure. An append-only stream never changes a value. A correctable stream replaces or deletes a value by key. An expiring stream drops the oldest value when the window moves. The kind decides how much of the data a structure must keep. A heap of size k is enough only for append-only streams with a fixed k, because every discarded value can never matter again.

Give Every Value Exactly One Home

A region is a group of live values that one structure holds, and the regions together cover every live value exactly once. The lower half of a median finder is a region, and so is the set of the k largest values. The regions are ordered: every value of one region is at most every value of the next. The order between regions is what makes the answer readable from the roots. The median is the largest value of the lower region, and the k-th largest is the smallest value of the top region.

Compare Against The Edge Between Regions

The boundary is the pair of values where two regions meet, such as the largest value of the lower region and the smallest value of the upper region. The code routes a new value by comparing it with the boundary. A value above the boundary joins the upper region. If a region then holds too many values, its extreme value crosses the boundary into the next region. One move repairs the sizes, because only the extreme value can cross without breaking the order.

Let Wrong Entries Wait Inside The Heap

A stale entry is a heap item that no longer matches the truth. The item can hold a deleted key, an old price or a timestamp that left the window. The design never searches for a stale entry. It keeps a table of truth, and it discards a heap root when the table disagrees with it. The design creates each entry once and discards it at most once, so n events cost O(n log n) in total. An entry buried below a good root costs nothing until it reaches the root, and a rebuild from the table clears buried entries when they outnumber the live ones.

The three ideas combine into one invariant. Every live value belongs to exactly one region. The regions are ordered across the boundary. The code checks every root against the truth before it reads the root.

State

State In The Ordered Structures

The structures below are small, and each piece of state has one owner and one rule for when it changes.

  • truth is the map from key to live value, and every correction and deletion changes it first.
  • top is the min-heap of the k largest values. Its root is the boundary and the k-th largest value.
  • rest is the max-heap of the other values, used by the rank sketch when k changes.
  • heap is the lazy max-heap of entries, and an entry stays in it after the truth moves on.
  • k is the rank. In the first example it never changes. The exercises let it change.

Values in the lazy heap use long, so a comparison never overflows. The state changes in one order: first update the truth, then push at most one new entry, then repair the sizes, and only then read a root.

Walkthrough

Admitting Values And Discarding Stale Roots

Keeping The Three Largest Of An Append-Only Stream

The stream is append-only, and the question is always the third largest value. The pointer i marks the value that just arrived. The heap top holds at most three values, and its root is the boundary. The first three values fill the heap without any comparison. The value 20 then beats the boundary 4, so it replaces the root. The value 1 loses to the boundary and leaves the heap unchanged. The value 7 loses against the new boundary 9, and 15 beats it. The answer after the last value is the root 12.

Correcting And Deleting With A Lazy Heap

The stream is correctable. The pointer op marks the operation that was just applied. The table truth is always right, and the heap heap may contain stale entries. The correction of key b to 20 leaves the entry 50 in the heap. The next maximum query meets that entry at the root, finds that the truth for b is 20, and discards it. The root is then 30, which matches the truth for key a, so the answer is 30. The deletion of a leaves its entry stale in the same way. The next query discards it and returns 20. A new value 25 for key a then arrives, and its new entry becomes the root. The final query discards nothing and returns 25.

Code

A Bounded Heap And A Lazy Heap

Keeping The Largest K Values

The class TopKHeap handles the append-only stream with a fixed rank. The heap never holds more than k values, so memory is O(k). Each addition costs O(log k), and the query costs O(1).

final class TopKHeap {
    private final int k;
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Integer> top = new java.util.PriorityQueue<>();

    TopKHeap(int k) {
        if (k < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("k must be at least 1");
        this.k = k;
    }

    void add(int v) {
        if (top.size() < k) top.add(v);                    // the heap is not full, so every value is admitted
        else if (v > top.peek()) { top.poll(); top.add(v); } // the value beats the boundary and replaces the root
    }

    Integer kth() {
        return top.size() == k ? top.peek() : null;        // the root is the k-th largest once k values exist
    }
}

Checking Roots Against The Truth

The class LazyMaxHeap handles a correctable stream. The map truth holds each live value. A heap entry records the key and the value at the time of the push. The method max discards roots that the truth contradicts, so a correction or a deletion costs one map operation.

final class LazyMaxHeap {
    private record Entry(String key, long value) {}

    private final java.util.HashMap<String, Long> truth = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Entry> heap =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(b.value(), a.value()));

    void set(String key, long value) {
        truth.put(key, value);                             // the truth changes first
        heap.add(new Entry(key, value));                   // one new entry; older entries for the key become stale
    }

    void erase(String key) {
        truth.remove(key);                                 // no heap search; the old entries become stale
    }

    java.util.OptionalLong max() {
        while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
            Entry top = heap.peek();
            Long live = truth.get(top.key());
            if (live != null && live == top.value()) return java.util.OptionalLong.of(top.value()); // the root agrees with the truth
            heap.poll();                                   // the root is stale; each entry leaves at most once
        }
        return java.util.OptionalLong.empty();             // nothing live remains
    }
}

Moving The Boundary For A New Rank

The Kth Largest With Changing Rank exercise keeps two heaps. The min-heap top holds the k largest values, and the max-heap rest holds all others. A change of rank moves values across the boundary until top has k values again.

final class RankSketch {
    private int k;
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Integer> top = new java.util.PriorityQueue<>();   // k largest, root is the boundary
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Integer> rest =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>(java.util.Comparator.reverseOrder());                 // all other values

    RankSketch(int k) { this.k = k; }

    void add(int v) { top.add(v); repair(); }
    void setRank(int newK) { k = newK; repair(); }
    Integer kth() { return top.size() == k ? top.peek() : null; }

    private void repair() {
        while (top.size() > k) rest.add(top.poll());                       // the smallest of top crosses down
        while (top.size() < k && !rest.isEmpty()) top.add(rest.poll());    // the largest of rest crosses up
    }
}

A change of rank by d moves d values, so it costs O(d log n).

Dead Marks For A Window Median

The Median Of The Last W Values exercise keeps a lower max-heap and an upper min-heap, as in the median lesson. The oldest value may sit deep inside either heap, so the code marks its entry dead and lowers the live count of its side. A dead entry waits until it reaches a root. Each side keeps a live count, and the balance rule uses live counts only.

final class WindowMedianSketch {
    private static final class E {
        final long value; boolean inLow, dead;
        E(long value) { this.value = value; }
    }

    private final int w;
    private int lowLive, highLive;                                         // live counts decide the balance
    private final java.util.ArrayDeque<E> window = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();   // arrival order
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<E> low =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(b.value, a.value));
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<E> high =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a.value, b.value));

    WindowMedianSketch(int w) { this.w = w; }

    private E liveLow() { while (!low.isEmpty() && low.peek().dead) low.poll(); return low.peek(); }
    private E liveHigh() { while (!high.isEmpty() && high.peek().dead) high.poll(); return high.peek(); }

    void add(long x) {
        E e = new E(x);
        window.addLast(e);
        if (lowLive == 0 || x <= liveLow().value) { e.inLow = true; low.add(e); lowLive++; }
        else { high.add(e); highLive++; }
        if (window.size() > w) {                                           // the oldest value expires by a mark
            E old = window.pollFirst();
            old.dead = true;
            if (old.inLow) lowLive--; else highLive--;
        }
        while (lowLive > highLive + 1) { E r = liveLow(); low.poll(); r.inLow = false; high.add(r); lowLive--; highLive++; }
        while (highLive > lowLive) { E r = liveHigh(); high.poll(); r.inLow = true; low.add(r); highLive--; lowLive++; }
    }

    long median() {
        if (lowLive == 0) throw new IllegalStateException();
        return liveLow().value;                                            // the lower root is the lower median
    }
}

Cost And Java Details

  • TopKHeap costs O(log k) per addition and O(k) space.
  • LazyMaxHeap costs O(log n) amortized per event, because each entry enters the heap once and leaves it at most once.
  • Memory of LazyMaxHeap grows with the number of events unless a rebuild from truth runs when the heap holds more than twice the live count.

The comparison live == top.value() unboxes the Long before it compares, so the value 1000 does not fail the way two boxed Long objects can under ==. The comparator uses Long.compare, since the difference of two large long values can overflow.

When to use it

When Ordered Regions Fit

Recognizing The Pattern

Look for a question about an extreme or a rank over data that changes. Examples are the largest value so far and the middle value of the last hundred. Then ask which kind of event the stream contains. The invariant to state has three parts. Every live value has one home, the regions are ordered, and the code trusts a heap root only after the truth confirms it. Equal values are the usual trap, so the routing rule must say which side a tie takes and the test must contain ties.

The Nearest False Friend

A bounded heap that keeps the k largest values is a false friend for the median and for any rank that grows with the count. It answers a fixed rank and discards the values on the other side of the boundary. A TreeSet is a second false friend, because it merges equal values into one item, so the count of a rank drifts after the first duplicate. A TreeMap from value to count fixes the duplicates.

When Not To Use It

If the questions ask for arbitrary ranks, such as the 10th and the 90th percentile together, two regions with one boundary are not enough. A tree of counts over the sorted values answers any rank in O(log n), and chapter 31 covers it. If the values are small integers, a counting array does the same without a heap. If corrections are rare and the stream is short, the sorted list of the first design is the clearest code. A lazy heap then adds complexity without a gain.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Kth Largest With Changing Rank

LeetCode 703

Problem Specification. Design the class RankTracker. The constructor takes the initial rank k. The method add(v) appends the integer v to the stream. The method setRank(newK) replaces the rank. The method kth() returns the k-th largest value among all added values, where equal values count separately, or null when fewer than k values exist.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Rank is an integer from 1 to 10^5 at every moment.
  • Values are int values from -10^9 to 10^9, and duplicates occur.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Cost of setRank is proportional to the change in rank times a logarithm.

Example 1. Input calls ["RankTracker","add","add","kth","add","kth","setRank","kth","add","kth","setRank","kth"] with arguments [[2],[6],[11],[],[8],[],[4],[],[3],[],[1],[]], output [null,null,null,6,null,8,null,null,null,3,null,11].

Example 2. Input calls ["RankTracker","kth","add","add","kth","add","kth","setRank","kth","setRank","kth","add","add","kth"] with arguments [[3],[],[-5],[-5],[],[-9],[],[2],[],[5],[],[0],[7],[]], output [null,null,null,null,null,null,-9,null,-5,null,null,null,null,-9].

Prerequisites. The bounded heap from the code stage. The stream is append-only, but the rank can change. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 703: a bounded heap of fixed size loses the values that a larger rank needs.

What This Exercise Changes. A single bounded heap is no longer enough. The structure keeps two ordered regions, the top k values and all others, and a change of rank moves values across the boundary.

Hint

When the rank grows, which values must the structure still hold, and from which heap does the next value come?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stream is append-only, but the rank can grow, so no value can be thrown away. The tracker keeps two ordered regions: a min-heap top that holds the k largest values, and a max-heap rest that holds every other value. The root of top is the boundary and the answer. A new value that beats the boundary enters top, and the old boundary drops into rest. A value that ties or loses goes to rest directly.

A change of rank moves values across the boundary. A smaller rank pushes roots of top into rest, and a larger rank pulls roots of rest into top. The invariant is that top holds the min(k, count) largest values, and every value in top is at least every value in rest. Each move preserves this order because only the extreme value of a region crosses. A tie that goes to rest is safe, since equal values are interchangeable for a rank.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per addition and O(d log n) for a rank change of d, because each moved value costs one heap operation.
  • Space is O(n), because the structure must keep every value to serve a rank that grows later. A fixed rank would allow O(k).
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class KthWithMovingK {
    /** Min-heap that holds the current top region: the k largest values seen. */
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> top = new PriorityQueue<>();
    /** Max-heap that holds every other value, so a larger k can pull values back. */
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> rest = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
    private int k;

    /**
     * Creates the tracker with a starting rank.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: top holds the min(k, count) largest values, and every value in top is at least every value in rest.
     */
    KthWithMovingK(int k) {
        // A rank below one has no meaning, so the constructor rejects it.
        if (k < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("k must be at least 1");
        this.k = k;
    }

    /**
     * Appends one value to the stream.
     * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1) extra, and the value stays stored.
     */
    void add(int v) {
        if (top.size() < k) {
            // Fewer than k values exist, so rest is empty and the value joins the top region.
            top.add(v);
        } else if (v > top.peek()) {
            // The value beats the boundary: it enters top and the old boundary drops into rest.
            top.add(v);
            rest.add(top.poll());
        } else {
            // The value ties or loses against the boundary, so it belongs to rest.
            rest.add(v);
        }
    }

    /**
     * Changes the rank, moving |newK - oldK| values between the two heaps at most.
     * Time: O(d log n) for a change of d. Space: O(1).
     */
    void setRank(int newK) {
        if (newK < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("k must be at least 1");
        k = newK;
        // A smaller k pushes the smallest values of top down into rest.
        while (top.size() > k) rest.add(top.poll());
        // A larger k pulls the largest values of rest up into top.
        while (top.size() < k && !rest.isEmpty()) top.add(rest.poll());
    }

    /**
     * Returns the k-th largest value, or null when fewer than k values exist.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     */
    Integer kth() {
        // The root of top is the smallest of the k largest, which is the k-th largest overall.
        return top.size() == k ? top.peek() : null;
    }

    private static Integer oracle(List<Integer> all, int k) {
        // The reference sorts a copy in descending order and indexes it.
        List<Integer> copy = new ArrayList<>(all);
        copy.sort(Collections.reverseOrder());
        return copy.size() >= k ? copy.get(k - 1) : null;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the rank grows to 4 with three values, which gives no answer, and then shrinks to 1.
        KthWithMovingK t = new KthWithMovingK(2);
        t.add(6); t.add(11);
        if (t.kth() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first");
        t.add(8);
        if (t.kth() != 8) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second");
        t.setRank(4);
        if (t.kth() != null) throw new AssertionError("example 1 short");
        t.add(3);
        if (t.kth() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1 fourth");
        t.setRank(1);
        if (t.kth() != 11) throw new AssertionError("example 1 max");
        // Example 2: negative duplicates, then a rank beyond the count.
        KthWithMovingK u = new KthWithMovingK(3);
        if (u.kth() != null) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        u.add(-5); u.add(-5);
        if (u.kth() != null) throw new AssertionError("two of three");
        u.add(-9);
        if (u.kth() != -9) throw new AssertionError("third");
        u.setRank(2);
        if (u.kth() != -5) throw new AssertionError("second");
        u.setRank(5);
        if (u.kth() != null) throw new AssertionError("five");
        u.add(0); u.add(7);
        if (u.kth() != -9) throw new AssertionError("fifth");
        // A rank below one is rejected.
        try { new KthWithMovingK(0); throw new AssertionError("accepted zero"); } catch (IllegalArgumentException ok) { }
        // Random operation sequences are compared with the sorting reference after every step.
        Random rnd = new Random(3801L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 20000; trial++) {
            int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            KthWithMovingK fast = new KthWithMovingK(k);
            List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 25; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) { int v = rnd.nextInt(7) - 3; fast.add(v); all.add(v); }
                else if (op == 1) { k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6); fast.setRank(k); }
                // Compare after every operation, including answers that are null.
                Integer got = fast.kth();
                Integer want = oracle(all, k);
                if (want == null ? got != null : !want.equals(got)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial + " step " + step);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Median Of The Last W Values

LeetCode 295

Problem Specification. Design the class WindowMedian. The constructor takes the window size w. The method add(x) appends the integer x; when more than w values exist, the oldest one leaves the window. The method median() returns the lower median of the values in the window. For c values in the window, this is the value at zero-based position (c - 1) / 2 in sorted order. It throws IllegalStateException when the window is empty.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Window size w is an integer from 1 to 10^5.
  • Values are int values from -10^9 to 10^9, and duplicates occur.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5 per object.
  • Memory stays O(w) however long the stream runs.

Example 1. Input w = 3. Add 8, 1, 5, 9, 2, 2 one at a time and query after each. The output is 8, 1, 5, 5, 5, 2.

Example 2. Input w = 2. Add -4, -4, 7, -1, -1, 0 and query after each. The output is -4, -4, -4, -1, -1, -1.

Prerequisites. The first exercise above and the stale-entry idea from the insight stage. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 295: the stream expires, so the median covers only the newest values.

What This Exercise Changes. The oldest value expires and the two halves must stay balanced by live count. Expiry marks a value dead, and the code discards a dead root when it reaches the top.

Hint

The oldest value may sit deep inside either heap. How can the live counts stay correct while its entry stays where it is?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stream expires, so the median covers only the newest w values. The structure keeps the two halves of the median finder: a max-heap low and a min-heap high, plus a queue of items in arrival order. When the window is full, the item at the front of the queue expires. Its heap entry is not searched for. The code marks the item dead and lowers the live count of its half. The dead entry waits in its heap until it reaches the root.

Every read of a root first discards dead roots. The invariant has two parts. The live values of low are at most the live values of high, and the live count of low exceeds that of high by zero or one. The routine repairs the halves after an expiry and before the new value is routed. Otherwise a half with no live value would misroute it. Dead items buried below live roots do not disturb the answer. They would grow the memory, so a rebuild from the live items runs when the heaps hold more than twice the window plus a constant. The query returns the root of low, which is the lower median.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log w) amortized per addition and per query. Each event moves a bounded number of items between heaps, and each item leaves a heap at most once. A rebuild costs O(w) and follows at least w additions.
  • Space is O(w), since the rebuild keeps the stored items, live and dead together, within about twice the window.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class WindowMedian {
    /** One stream value. It sits in exactly one heap and is marked dead when it expires. */
    private static final class Item {
        final int value;
        boolean dead;
        boolean inLow;
        Item(int value) { this.value = value; }
    }

    private final int w;
    private final ArrayDeque<Item> window = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final PriorityQueue<Item> low = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(b.value, a.value));
    private final PriorityQueue<Item> high = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a.value, b.value));
    private int lowLive, highLive;

    /**
     * Creates a tracker for the last w values.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(w) live items plus a bounded number of dead items.
     * Invariant: every live value in low is at most every live value in high, and lowLive - highLive is 0 or 1.
     */
    WindowMedian(int w) {
        if (w < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("window must hold at least one value");
        this.w = w;
    }

    /**
     * Appends a value and expires the oldest value when the window is full.
     * Time: O(log w) amortized. Space: O(1) extra.
     */
    void add(int x) {
        // The oldest value expires first, and the halves are repaired before the new value is routed.
        if (window.size() == w) { expire(window.pollFirst()); rebalance(); }
        Item item = new Item(x);
        window.addLast(item);
        // Route by the largest live value of the lower half.
        clean(low);
        if (low.isEmpty() || x <= low.peek().value) { item.inLow = true; low.add(item); lowLive++; }
        else { high.add(item); highLive++; }
        rebalance();
        compact();
    }

    /**
     * Returns the lower median of the live values: the smaller of the two middle values for an even count.
     * Time: O(log w) amortized. Space: O(1).
     */
    int median() {
        if (window.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("no values");
        // Dead roots are discarded first; after that the root of low is the lower median.
        clean(low);
        return low.peek().value;
    }

    private void expire(Item old) {
        // The item stays inside its heap; it only changes the live count of its side.
        old.dead = true;
        if (old.inLow) lowLive--; else highLive--;
    }

    private void clean(PriorityQueue<Item> heap) {
        // A dead root is stale and leaves the heap; each item leaves at most once.
        while (!heap.isEmpty() && heap.peek().dead) heap.poll();
    }

    private void rebalance() {
        // The lower half is too large: its live root moves up.
        while (lowLive > highLive + 1) {
            clean(low);
            Item m = low.poll();
            m.inLow = false; high.add(m);
            lowLive--; highLive++;
        }
        // The upper half is larger: its live root moves down.
        while (highLive > lowLive) {
            clean(high);
            Item m = high.poll();
            m.inLow = true; low.add(m);
            highLive--; lowLive++;
        }
    }

    private void compact() {
        // Dead items buried below live ones are removed when they outnumber the live items.
        if (low.size() + high.size() <= 2 * window.size() + 16) return;
        List<Item> keepLow = new ArrayList<>(), keepHigh = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Item i : low) if (!i.dead) keepLow.add(i);
        for (Item i : high) if (!i.dead) keepHigh.add(i);
        low.clear(); high.clear();
        low.addAll(keepLow); high.addAll(keepHigh);
    }

    int stored() { return low.size() + high.size(); }

    private static int oracle(List<Integer> win) {
        // Reference: sort the window and take the lower middle element.
        List<Integer> s = new ArrayList<>(win);
        Collections.sort(s);
        return s.get((s.size() - 1) / 2);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: window of 3; the median follows the last three values only.
        WindowMedian a = new WindowMedian(3);
        int[] in1 = {8, 1, 5, 9, 2, 2};
        int[] out1 = {8, 1, 5, 5, 5, 2};
        for (int i = 0; i < in1.length; i++) {
            a.add(in1[i]);
            if (a.median() != out1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        }
        // Example 2: window of 2 returns the smaller of two middle values; negative numbers work.
        WindowMedian b = new WindowMedian(2);
        int[] in2 = {-4, -4, 7, -1, -1, 0};
        int[] out2 = {-4, -4, -4, -1, -1, -1};
        for (int i = 0; i < in2.length; i++) {
            b.add(in2[i]);
            if (b.median() != out2[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 2 at " + i);
        }
        // An empty tracker and a zero window are rejected.
        try { new WindowMedian(3).median(); throw new AssertionError("empty"); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
        try { new WindowMedian(0); throw new AssertionError("zero"); } catch (IllegalArgumentException ok) { }
        // A window of one always returns the newest value.
        WindowMedian one = new WindowMedian(1);
        for (int v : new int[] {3, -2, 9}) { one.add(v); if (one.median() != v) throw new AssertionError("window one"); }
        // Random streams are compared with the sorting reference after every add.
        Random rnd = new Random(3802L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 20000; trial++) {
            int w = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            WindowMedian fast = new WindowMedian(w);
            List<Integer> win = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 30; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(7) - 3;
                fast.add(v);
                win.add(v);
                if (win.size() > w) win.remove(0);
                if (fast.median() != oracle(win)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial + " step " + step);
            }
        }
        // Memory stays proportional to the window even for a long increasing stream.
        WindowMedian longRun = new WindowMedian(4);
        for (int i = 0; i < 200000; i++) longRun.add(i);
        if (longRun.stored() > 2 * 4 + 16 + 2) throw new AssertionError("stored " + longRun.stored());
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Price Timeline With Deletions

LeetCode 2034

Problem Specification. Design the class PriceTimeline. The method update(t, p) sets the price at timestamp t to p and replaces an earlier price at t. The method remove(t) deletes the record at t and returns true when a record existed. The methods current(), maximum() and minimum() return the price at the largest live timestamp, the highest live price and the lowest live price. Each throws IllegalStateException when no record is live.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Timestamps are long values from 1 to 10^12, and arrive in any order.
  • Prices are long values from 0 to 10^12.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5 per object.
  • Memory of the heaps stays within a constant factor of the live record count plus a fixed number of entries.

Example 1. Input calls update(10,40), update(20,25), update(15,60), then maximum(), minimum(), current(), remove(20), current(), remove(20), update(15,5), maximum(), minimum(), output 60, 25, 25, true, 60, false, 40, 5.

Example 2. Input calls update(7,30), update(7,30), update(3,30), remove(7), current(), maximum(), remove(3), then minimum(), output true, 30, 30, true, and an IllegalStateException.

Prerequisites. The lazy heap from the code stage and the second exercise above. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 2034: records can be deleted, and the heaps must stay small.

What This Exercise Changes. Deletion makes an entry stale without replacing it, so validity means the timestamp is live with the same price. A rebuild from the table keeps memory proportional to the live records.

Hint

Which structure gives the largest live timestamp after the latest record disappears, and when does a heap entry still count?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stream is correctable. An ordered map from timestamp to price is the truth, and it also answers current through its last key, even after the latest record is removed. Two lazy heaps hold entries of price and timestamp, a max-heap and a min-heap. An update changes the truth and pushes one entry into each heap. A removal changes only the truth, because the entries of the removed record stay behind as stale entries.

A query for the maximum or the minimum discards roots whose timestamp is missing from the truth or holds a different price, and then returns the first root that agrees. An entry that repeats the same price is still valid, and a duplicate valid entry is harmless. The invariant is that every live record has at least one valid entry in each heap. The rebuild from the map runs when the heap holds more than twice the live count plus a constant, and it keeps memory proportional to the live records. The test also checks the queries on an empty timeline, which throw.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) amortized per operation, because each entry is polled at most once and a rebuild of O(n) follows at least n pushes or removals.
  • Space is O(n) for the live records, plus a bounded number of stale entries because of the rebuild.
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class StockTimeline {
    /** Truth: the live price of each timestamp, ordered by timestamp. */
    private final TreeMap<Long, Long> prices = new TreeMap<>();
    /** Lazy max-heap of entries {price, timestamp}; an entry is live only when it equals the truth. */
    private final PriorityQueue<long[]> maxHeap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(b[0], a[0]));
    /** Lazy min-heap of entries {price, timestamp} with the same liveness rule. */
    private final PriorityQueue<long[]> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));

    /**
     * Sets the price at a timestamp, replacing any earlier price there.
     * Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1) amortized, because rebuilds cap the heap size.
     * Invariant: every live price has at least one entry in each heap.
     */
    void update(long timestamp, long price) {
        prices.put(timestamp, price);
        // The old entries stay in the heaps; they now disagree with the truth and will be discarded later.
        maxHeap.add(new long[] {price, timestamp});
        minHeap.add(new long[] {price, timestamp});
        compact();
    }

    /**
     * Deletes the record at a timestamp. Returns true when a record existed.
     * Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1).
     */
    boolean remove(long timestamp) {
        // Only the truth changes; the heaps keep entries that are now stale.
        boolean existed = prices.remove(timestamp) != null;
        compact();
        return existed;
    }

    /** Returns the price at the largest timestamp. Time: O(log n). Space: O(1). */
    long current() {
        if (prices.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("no records");
        // The ordered map gives the latest timestamp directly, even after the latest record was removed.
        return prices.lastEntry().getValue();
    }

    /** Returns the highest live price. Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1). */
    long maximum() { return live(maxHeap); }

    /** Returns the lowest live price. Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1). */
    long minimum() { return live(minHeap); }

    private long live(PriorityQueue<long[]> heap) {
        if (prices.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("no records");
        while (true) {
            long[] top = heap.peek();
            Long truth = prices.get(top[1]);
            // The root is live when the truth for its timestamp still holds the same price.
            if (truth != null && truth == top[0]) return top[0];
            // Otherwise the root is stale and leaves the heap; each entry leaves at most once.
            heap.poll();
        }
    }

    private void compact() {
        // When stale entries outnumber live ones, both heaps are rebuilt from the truth in O(n).
        if (maxHeap.size() > 2 * prices.size() + 16) {
            maxHeap.clear();
            minHeap.clear();
            for (var e : prices.entrySet()) {
                maxHeap.add(new long[] {e.getValue(), e.getKey()});
                minHeap.add(new long[] {e.getValue(), e.getKey()});
            }
        }
    }

    int heapSize() { return maxHeap.size(); }

    private static void same(StockTimeline fast, TreeMap<Long, Long> ref, String where) {
        // Compare every query with a direct scan of the reference map.
        if (ref.isEmpty()) {
            try { fast.maximum(); throw new AssertionError("empty max " + where); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
            try { fast.current(); throw new AssertionError("empty current " + where); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
            return;
        }
        long max = Long.MIN_VALUE, min = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (long p : ref.values()) { max = Math.max(max, p); min = Math.min(min, p); }
        if (fast.maximum() != max || fast.minimum() != min || fast.current() != ref.lastEntry().getValue()) {
            throw new AssertionError("mismatch " + where);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing the latest record moves current back to the previous timestamp.
        StockTimeline s = new StockTimeline();
        s.update(10, 40); s.update(20, 25); s.update(15, 60);
        if (s.maximum() != 60 || s.minimum() != 25 || s.current() != 25) throw new AssertionError("example 1 a");
        if (!s.remove(20) || s.current() != 60) throw new AssertionError("example 1 b");
        if (s.remove(20)) throw new AssertionError("second removal");
        s.update(15, 5);
        if (s.maximum() != 40 || s.minimum() != 5) throw new AssertionError("example 1 c");
        if (!s.remove(15) || !s.remove(10)) throw new AssertionError("example 1 d");
        try { s.maximum(); throw new AssertionError("empty after removals"); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
        // Example 2: duplicate prices, a repeated update, and a remove-then-reinsert at the same timestamp.
        StockTimeline t = new StockTimeline();
        try { t.current(); throw new AssertionError("empty"); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
        t.update(7, 30); t.update(7, 30); t.update(3, 30);
        if (!t.remove(7) || t.current() != 30 || t.maximum() != 30) throw new AssertionError("example 2 a");
        if (!t.remove(3)) throw new AssertionError("example 2 b");
        try { t.minimum(); throw new AssertionError("empty min"); } catch (IllegalStateException ok) { }
        // Random operations are compared with a plain map after every step.
        Random rnd = new Random(3803L);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            StockTimeline fast = new StockTimeline();
            TreeMap<Long, Long> ref = new TreeMap<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                long ts = rnd.nextInt(8);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    long p = rnd.nextInt(6);
                    fast.update(ts, p); ref.put(ts, p);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (fast.remove(ts) != (ref.remove(ts) != null)) throw new AssertionError("remove result");
                }
                same(fast, ref, "trial " + trial + " step " + step);
            }
        }
        // The heap size stays proportional to the live count plus a constant, even after many corrections.
        StockTimeline many = new StockTimeline();
        for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) many.update(i % 5, i);
        many.maximum();
        if (many.heapSize() > 2 * 5 + 16 + 1) throw new AssertionError("heap grew to " + many.heapSize());
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Trimmed Mean With A Correction

LeetCode 1825

Problem Specification. Design the class TrimmedWindow. The constructor takes m, lo and hi with m > lo + hi. The method add(x) appends the non-negative integer x, and the oldest value leaves when more than m values exist. The method amendLast(x) replaces the newest value by the non-negative integer x, and it throws IllegalStateException when no value exists. The method average() returns -1 while fewer than m values exist. Otherwise it removes the lo smallest and the hi largest values of the window and returns the floor of the mean of the remaining m - lo - hi values.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Window size m is an integer from 1 to 10^5, with lo and hi from 0 up to m - 1.
  • Values are int values from 0 to 10^9, and duplicates occur.
  • Calls number at most 10^5 per object.
  • Sums need long arithmetic.

Example 1. Input m = 4, lo = 1, hi = 0. Add 9, 2, 6, 4, 20 in order and call average() after the third, fourth and fifth value. Then call amendLast(1) and average(). The output is -1, 6, 10, 4.

Example 2. Input m = 5, lo = 2, hi = 1. Add 0, 0, 7, 7, 3, then call average(), amendLast(100), average(), add(1), average(), add(20) and average(). The output is 5, 7, 7, 13.

Prerequisites. The second exercise above and the idea that each live value has one home among ordered regions. This exercise is a transfer prompt. The interview question asks how three regions behave when the two cuts differ and the newest value can change. It changes the contract of LeetCode 1825.

What This Exercise Changes. The cut sizes differ on the two sides, and the newest value can change after the code routed it. Removal and insertion both go through the same repair that restores the region sizes.

Hint

When a value leaves, which region might hold it, and which neighbor refills the region that lost a value?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stream expires and is correctable at its newest value. The window keeps three ordered regions: low holds the lo smallest values, high holds the hi largest values, and mid holds the rest with a running sum. Each region is a count map with a size and a sum. A queue of the window values finds the value that expires and the newest value to correct.

Insertion routes a value by the two boundaries, the largest value of low and the smallest value of high. Removal takes a copy of the value from the first region that holds it, which is safe because equal copies are interchangeable. Both paths end in one repair that sets the targets to min(lo, n) and min(hi, n - low). The repair moves surplus extremes into mid first, and then refills a short side from mid. The invariant is that every value of low is at most every value of mid, and every value of mid is at most every value of high. The mean is the sum of mid divided by its size, and it is read only when m values exist.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log m) per addition, correction and query step, because each repair moves a constant number of values and each move is a map operation.
  • Space is O(m) for the queue and the three count maps.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeMap;

public final class TrimmedWindow {
    /** A multiset of ints with a running size and sum. */
    private static final class Region {
        final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> counts = new TreeMap<>();
        int size;
        long sum;
